TAHITI 

Matales Vairao Taravao Teahupoo 20 Tautira

"IOARANA!" L EAVING the bus at Taravao, I walked toward the sunset and followed the road that curved on to Tautira, the district farthest from Papeete. Supper fires were lit at most of the homes. Occasional bits of song drifted on the still air. 

Suddenly it was night. Fishermen carrying palm torches started out in their canoes. Natives passing me on the road called "Ioarana!' the island greeting. Then supper fires were out, the native homes dark and still, and I began to wonder where my bed for the night would be, for this was Tahiti, an island in the South Seas and no auto courts or motor hotels flung signs along the road begging me to spend the night with them. I had left the island's chief village far behind and it was too late to turn back.

A little off the road several people sat in their cooking shed around a fire of coconut husks. A young man lolled against a palm post playing a guitar. The others sang.

I turned into the pathway that led to their shelter and called "Ioarana."

Abruptly the singing stopped. Excitement seized them. A girl caught up a lantern and held it near my face. The guitar boy put down his instrument and threw palm leaves on the fire to bring a blaze.

An old man came forward and took my hand. 

Tahitian poured forth. 

No one could doubt their sincere welcome. I understood nothing but the one word of greeting, but it was obvious that they had at first been startled by the appearance of a white woman out of the night.

By signs I indicated I wanted a place to sleep. 

They understood readily, and went into a family conference. I must sit by the fire and rest till plans could be made. Everyone had something to say. They talked at length. When the family conference in the cooking shed at last broke up, the natives joined in a body to escort me to their one-roomed house. They showed me their sleeping mats on the bamboo floor and made me understand their regrets at having no accommodations good enough for me.

I signed that I could sleep on the floor as well as they, but they shook their heads emphatically. 

That would never do for the white guest! They solved their problems by escorting me in a trium-

"IOARANAI" · 19 phant procession to the next house down the road.

Here I found another excited welcome-and a wondrous and enormous bed. It was piled high with four home-made kapok mattresses. The sheet was made of flour sacks, but was as white as soap and water could make it. Five plump kapok pillows like fat soldiers, all in spotless flour sacking, were lined up at the head of the bed. 

The bed was never used by the family. Their mats were on the floor. This high whiteness was to be solely mine. They also gave me a white cotton bedspread with very fat tassels which had been stored in a carved sea chest. 

When I took my sleeping pareau from my basket they excused themselves and went outside with a lantern so I might be alone to undress. 

But I suppose someone was keeping watch. The moment I had wrapped myself in the pareau, both families came trooping back in.

Men, women and children stood about the bed in a circle watching me. I chose one of the fat pillows, but I couldn't think how to use all of them. They stared at me with curiosity, plainly trying to suppress their excitement. I hesitated, but it seemed more sensible to get on to bed than to stand staring back, so I mounted the four mattresses. They all beamed.

A dozen hands reached out to spread the cover over me. A dozen hands smoothed out every wrinkle and patted each tassel in place. Then all stood back to admire the fine effect of someone in

the guest bed. Their brown faces showed their pleasure.

At last the escorting family went home. My host turned out the light so his family could prepare for bed in decent darkness. My last thought before sleeping was that I must not turn over during the night. I might wrinkle the bedspread or disarrange its tassels. In Tahiti, it's a grave responsibility to be a guest. But why was I in this South Sea island, a school teacherwife and mother so far from family and friends?

! 

I marry a buckeroo. Babies. The depression separates us. We fight a silly idea-and lose. 

To understand my presence in Tahiti, I suppose you must really go back to 1921.

In October of that year, I started a most glorious five weeks of deer hunting in the sagebrush hills back of San Diego with my brother Bill and his pal, Harry Parsons. Every one voted Harry the most capable of training a new recruit to the game, so he became my special guide and teacher. 

A year later, Sitty, my chum, and I, rode into a naive Arizona village, a town only three blocks long, with Harry at the wheel. We arrived on a 

Saturday afternoon and found it impossible to buy a marriage license. "Must send up to the county seat for one, forty miles away," the Justice of the Peace told us. "Come around Monday and get married.”

Monday it was, and we arrived in the midst of would-be-buyers of somebody's household goods piled in front of his office, an auctioneer extolling the virtues of secondhand furniture. "Confound Lem," growled the Justice of the 

Peace. "Holding his auctions at my front door.

I'll show him I can yell as loud as he can." "What am I bid?" shouted the auctioneer just outside the door. "Do you take this woman"

Justice of the Peace. 

shouted the "Is that the last bid, gentlemen ?" the auctioneer called in an even louder voice. ... "Do you take this man "the Justice yelled. "Going! Going!" roared the auctioneer. "I now pronounce you man and wife," thundered the Justice. "Gone!" boomed the auctioneer. 

Harry and I looked at each other. My bridesmaid waved her cowboy hat, and said, "Guess our trails part here. Have a happy honeymoon. You don't need a chaperone any longer, so I'd better catch my train."

Harry gave me a tremendous hug and a kiss, saying, "Honey, I'm sure proud to have you for my wife." A typical western man's remark, though no other cowboy had said just those words to me before. "Kiss the bridesmaid, too," I instructed my brand new husband, and Sitty disappeared in 

Harry's bear hug. "If I was real sure which one of you was the bride, I'd kiss her myself," mused the Justice. "But you look like three of the boys just in from the range." "Over-developed sense of humor," murmured Sitty.

We looked ourselves over. There was reason

for the man's quizzical stare. We all wore riding breeches, boots, wool shirts and western hats. 

Sitty and I had boyish bobs, for we had been camping and hiking. "Well, you can pick out the groom anyway," I said, "because he took off his hat when he came in here." "Don't want to kiss the groom," the Justice mumbled. 

We started for the door. "What am I offered for this fine baby buggy?" the auctioneer demanded outside. "Fifty cents." "Gentlemen, gentlemen. Look at it. You're joking. Such fine springs. Fit for a big bouncing boy. And take a squint at the size of it. Big enough for twins!" 

Harry, "I'll get you "Come on, honey," said girls through this crowd. Buck the line here." 

And we bucked with Harry's arm around me protectingly, and Sitty clinging behind. "Here's your chance, young fellow. Just the thing for you and the little wife. Are you a good 

Boy Scout? Be prepared." "Shall we buy it, honey?" whispered Harry. "Maybe it's sort of a good luck omen for us." "What! Take a baby carriage on our honeymoon? No, thanks. Let someone else have the twins and the bouncing boy." wad on. "Well, you're the boss!" Cay 

Harry and I wandered through California mountains for three weeks on our honeymoon,

camping under the stars and beside blue lakes.

Then we drove back to the little California town which had always been my husband's home. 

There wasn't a house for rent in the village. The best we could get was the promise of one in December. "Very well, then, we'll camp," and we went on to Sweetwater River and found a tent site under an oak tree.

Harry went to work in the old car, while I took long tramps over the hills, caught up with the books I had been wanting to read. Not entirely worthless as a wife, I had a hot dinner ready when he came home. Then we would stroll up the dry river bed in the sunset, and sit by our campfire when evening turned chilly.

Visits to friends, an occasional movie and a weekly dance at a country club made up our social life.

The next summer found us camping in the hills, trying to escape the heat of the valley, for we were expecting the "blessed event" in the fall. 

My heart was set on a daughter. Months before she was born she was named Hester West, after my mother. 

Then one midnight Harry was rushing me home, frantically locating the nurse and dragging the doctor from his bed. Hester West made her debut at 10:45 next morning. "That's the daughter you planned on darling,"

Harry said with a comical mixture of pride and relief.

"She's so tiny," I gasped. "I thought husky people like us would have a big baby.” "She'll grow," soothed the nurse. "There's another one coming," added the doctor. Twins after all! I wonder now if that auctioneer and my new husband had a hunch?

Little sister followed in fifteen minutes, another mite, but perfect. Four pounds each. They were stowed away in a basket with three hot water bottles as big as they were. Sister was known as "The Little Postscript" until a real name was decided upon. Later we nicknamed them Peggy and Polly.

When the twins reached school age we decided to send them to San Diego, but that city was eighteen miles from our home. We talked possibilities: Let the children board near the school? 

Should I move to town with the girls? But what about leaving our little home where we had all been so happy? Leaving Harry to carry on with his work and "batch"? Not so good. We couldn't all move to the city, for Harry's position was that of field manager for the Citrus Association. He had been with the same company for many years and was not fitted for office work. cracks line to se 

At last I arranged to use my sedan as a bus for a day nursery to help defray the expenses of the daily drive to and from the school. While the children were in school, I would attend state college and get another degree. It might come in handy if I ever wanted to teach again. It would be fun to get back into school life after five years

of loafing, or is rearing lively twins loafing?

My schedule was strenuous: up at 6 o'clock to straighten the house; children must be washed. and dressed. A scramble to get in the car by 7:00.

If we didn't have flat tires or battery trouble we reached the outskirts of San Diego by 7:30 and picked up the first little urchin for the nursery. 

Then a route over town, adding to my load of squirming, squealing babies. Usually I had fifteen in my car between the ages of two and five. No one envied me the work! "weeted an 

Out went my two at their school and by 8:00, with good luck, we drew up at the nursery and spilled the rest. College classes began at 8:10, but college was across town from the nursery. 

I arrived breathless. My last class closed at 4:00 p. m. which was the time I was supposed to be at the nursery to begin the evening delivery of children to their homes. Around the city again, dropping tired youngsters, and gathering Peggy and Polly for the drive home.

Then it was something from the grocery and the butcher shop and try to have a hot dinner ready when Harry came in at 6:00. In the evenings 

Peggy, Polly and I played word games, reviewed their school work, or dramatized the stories from their primer. When the girls were tucked in bed at 7:30, I began preparing my college work for the next day. Finished by 10:00, I started correcting hundreds of my classmates' papers for I was "reader" for one of the professors. aonderful. 

The girls made rapid progress in their school

27. that first winter.

I earned my degree, but learned to hate cars and driving. The daily run became a bugbear with the responsibility of so many little children. There were always traffic hazards. 

One tot climbed out a window and fell under a wheel. A leg was run over before I could stop, but no bones broken. One little imp opened the rear door and another pushed him out on a curve. 

By a miracle he wasn't much hurt, but oh! he looked terribly "flat" when I ran back to pick him up. 

That summer we camped beside a swimming pool, among grey rocks. Harry drove up every evening and we had fine romps together, not knowing how soon our jolly times were to be over. We had heard of the stock market crash of '29, but didn't think it concerned us. Vague rumors of a depression were going around, but we felt secure.

We had just moved back to our home for the fall school term when the depression caught us. 

The company for which Harry worked tightened up on expenses, made each man do two men's work, and Harry was squeezed out. Our home was not paid for, and we still owed on the sedan.. 

Thousands -millions of people in America have had the same "gone" feeling that we did when Harry received his notice of dismissal. He searched for another place, but found that many men were waiting for each vacancy. Two hopeless months passed.

We couldn't keep the sedan -it went back to

the dealer at a loss, so there were no more trips to the San Diego school. I taught the children at home. 

No work! The same story every night. Then

Harry took our savings and bought a dairy in Imperial Valley. It seemed to be a good thing at the time. There were no living quarters for a family at the place, so Harry went alone. Our first separation! He worked night and day, I had no car, so the 200 miles between us might as well have been 2000. We were feeling the pinch of the depression in earnest. 

Three months of that was enough. Leaving the girls with grandma, I borrowed a car and followed to the Valley, determined to find a position in the schools. Armed with references seven years old, a new degree and a smile, I was appointed to a place and told to take my class on 

Monday. Ho all of my Her y with the farm initiad. 

I raced back over the mountains faster than the law calls for; found renters for our home; packed clothing, bedding, pots and pans; left some furniture in the house; stored odds and ends; searched for books and pictures for my new work; bought a car on credit; decorated a tree, for it was 

Christmas eve; moved out the next morning as the renters moved in; stacked the twins on top of suitcases, and drove to the valley on Christmas day. 

That night we moved into a tiny auto camp cabin and felt lucky, for there was not a house for rent in town. Up at dawn, we got breakfast

among a confusion of articles dumped out of the car, and were off to my new position by 8:00 o'clock. The school was ten miles out from town, a little three-roomed country affair. 

Peggy and Polly were added to an overcrowded class of 45 first graders. To my horror, my second graders didn't have a room, but were cooped in an alcove off that buzzing primary hive, with only a cloth curtain between. Two teachers and 85 children going in one room! But had I known of the difficulties, I would have taken the place anyway, for there was a salary attached, and Harry and his dairy were only twenty miles away.

The winter passed like a moment, and the children and I were back to our camp by the swimming hole. But no daddy this time. He was struggling in Imperial Valley, with the thermometer hitting over 100 degrees, trying to put the failing dairy on its feet. That summer it crashed. 

All it had netted was plenty of hard work, a few bottles of milk and red ink. 2nd helped like a decent wife,

Our savings had gone with the dairy, so the it might have next fall took me back to the valley this time survived. she te fut as principal of a little school far out on the desert. Pellent bea 

This place meant a 70 mile drive daily, too tiring for children, so Peggy and Polly must be sent away to school. Another winter separated from my family.

Harry had tried a repair shop, a junk yard and hay bailing after the dairy disaster, but nothing brought in a living. My salary was stretched to bursting to cover car installments, house payalways fucks

. 

ments, taxes and month-to-month necessities for the four of us. Harry returned to the parental acres to try his hand at farming. He cleared land, dug a well, installed a pump and planted tomatoes in high hopes. But that was another unfinished symphony; two nights of frost swept away his bumper crop, labor and investment. 1 Next came the principalship of a larger school, more responsibilities, and new friends took my time, but I missed my family. I enjoyed my teaching always, but that couldn't fill the void. pl 

Harry was doggedly trying farming again, on the other side of the mountains. If we saw each other twice a month we were doing well. How I clung to the little letters the children sent me. 

That summer, back in camp again. It never occurred to us to move into our house although the tenants had not paid rent for a year. They had been squeezed in the depression, and we let them stay on rather than have it vacant, but we paid the taxes! Harry was bailing hay in the country and saw the children only four times before they were off to school again. I returned to the valley, thankful to have work that I loved, but still missing my family. Harry and I were growing more and more dissatisfied with life as we found it.

At this time the C .C. C.'s and W. P. A.'s and other A. B. C.'s were doing their bit for America's jobless millions. Harry's business and farming ventures all ended in the red, though he worked hard. Thousands of other men had the same ex-

perience.

It was a frightful strain on Harry for he was a firm believer in the old-fashioned set up, with the daddy piling out to work, and the mama and children in the little home.

In the fifth year of our separation and struggle, Harry was taken on the state highway work and earned his first salary. How proud he was to bring me a pay check! With both working, we settled his business debts as rapidly as possible, and saved a little money. We were now near enough to meet every Sunday on the desert, half way between his work and my school. We would lie among the sand dunes for hours, planning some kind of family reunion. "Suppose you stop teaching, take the children home, and handle their education yourself. You would have time to do it properly, then." "But that would mean we were in El Cajon, and you 'way off here. Just as bad as it was five years ago when we couldn't bear to be separated."

If we made a new home in Imperial Valley, near Harry's work, that would keep the children in the scorching heat, for Harry's modest salary wouldn't cover summer trips to the mountains. "Suppose the children and I should camp out in the hills all year. It would cost less. We must make the payments on the house! But no, that would leave Harry out of the picture again. We went over possibilities, but never saw a way to get together. In the meantime the children were growing up far from us. They were changed every time they returned from their school, and acent t

YOU COULD DO IT, TOO 

we were missing the pleasure of watching and guiding those changes. If the New Deal would work some magic ... or if . . . ' 99 Then we saw a magazine article which was to change our lives. It described the low cost of living in certain islands, Puerto Rico, Hawaii,

Tahiti and Bali. We read: 

"So you want to get away from it all? Away from this harried, hurried, machine-age civilization, with its social strife, its crimes, its threat of war and its economic stomachaches? Away to a languorous land of lazy sunlit days on a coral beach, and of soft nights beneath the tropic moon? A land of health and laughter and easy, natural life?"

Sounded lovely, but we were skeptical. Fine for millionaires. We read on: "You can do it, and you'd be surprised to know on how little money you can manage it.” 

We thought, "Oh, yeah?" but we read more. "I prescribe only tropical or subtropical islands cooler, breezier, more healthful. The sea adds beauty and tempers heat; boating, fishing, bathing . . . 99 But we were looking for more about costs. 

We found: "A person living in or near the smaller towns will need about $80 a month... not counting steamer fare and original equipment.

One who has sought out the back country retreats can manage for half that. of course if you are the experienced Robinson Crusoe type (nothing could have made us put the book aside now), you ...

can pare this down to the vanishing point cash for basic luxuries such as iodine, kerosene, sugar, salt, hardware, books and utensils, you will simply live off your paradise and dine on native fare." We were struck! "Do you suppose bananas are free?" "Robinson Crusoe type! That's us, honey." 

Instead of grey desert sage before us, we saw a dream island with trembling palm leaves and white breakers; a simple life that would run along in low gear, where Harry could work in a garden, and I would teach the children without distractions, and we would be together. 

We just dreamed of it at first, feeling it would be an impossible move, but pleasant to think of. 

We re-read, "Your proper profession there will consist in making. paradise pay dividends in cocoanuts, fruit, vegetables, fowls, fish, coffee, cacao-depending on location," and "the natives will help you build your bungalow and ask nothing more in return than a cool drink and a pipeful of nicotine . . plenty of spare acres where a wandering Adam and Eve would be welcome to build their dream cottage."

Again and again we went back to the part about costs. Was it true that one could live so cheaply in these islands? Bit by bit the idea possessed us till we were quoting to each other from that article, "where native built houses can be bought or rented for a song or where one can build a place of one's own out of free native materials." 

We had a feeling it was our duty to fight against

such silliness. One day we looked at each other and said boldly: "Why not?" We were lost. No use to struggle against the pull of "tropical or sub-tropical islands" anymore. 

What preparations? One more winter of teaching and highway work. Save all the money we could. Live as cheaply as possible and pad out the nest egg. We remembered bitterly what had happened to the fat little sum we had saved in Harry's years of steady work —all gone with the dairy. Well, we would gather a few dollars and run away to the South Seas for a year, forget our troubles and get acquainted with each other again. "Surely we can live there a year if we have $1,000 over and above fares. We'll leave the towns and strike out for the back country where our 

Robinson Crusoe ways will not be laughed at." 

But where? Which island? From that moment

I haunted libraries for books on the islands of the world: romance, travel stories, encyclopaedias, statistics. We thought: "There are bound to be some drawbacks in every land." The romance gave no hint of trouble. Most of the travel stories chose to tell of sunsets, moonlight and beauty, leaving out bugs and disease. The encyclopaedia described copra making, pearl fishing and trading.

Statistics were concerned with imports and exports. We wished we knew someone living on a tropical island somewhere so we could ask a lot of questions: Did they still wear grass skirts? 

Were there banks and money systems? Would

there be white man's food as well as native? What dangers from hurricanes? Any chance of a job? 

White companions for Peggy and Polly?

We tried to find answers to these and a thousand others, but at the end of a winter of reading, we didn't know all the answers yet. But we had picked Tahiti for our year in paradise. A letter of inquiry to the American Consul cleared up more points, but we couldn't feel sure of it as a suitable place for the girls. We decided that 

Harry should plug away at his work, while I would go exploring in the fall and see if the place was livable. If all the arm-chair novelists had fooled us, I would return and try to get work again. If the island passed all the tests I would wire for daddy and the girls to come on. Harry could get a year's leave of absence from his work and still step back into the position later if island life proved impracticable. Our steamship tickets would be good for a year.

Sometimes we grew a little faint-hearted at the thought of spending all our money on a year's holiday. We'd be broke when we returned to the 

States. We wouldn't have cars to get to our work in. Perhaps we had better stay at the old grind one more year; make a bigger "poke" before starting on our adventure. But when spring came to the desert, with wee blossoms covering the sand, the lure of trees and outdoor things got us. We flung over precautions.

Our last summer in camp was a delight-we had the river, our pool, our horses and four

precious kittens, Adolph, Dinglehoofer, Mac and Blondie. The girls and I loved it all just the same, but now we were talking of the "Isles of the Blest" too, where life would be something like camp, but daddy would be with us, completing the family circle. We fancied brown children playing on lagoon beaches. "I shall let my hair grow," said Polly. "I'll get so brown that people will think I am a Tahiti girl," added Peggy. They practiced hula hulas in little grass skirts.

There was a frantic rush at the end. The kittens were left in grandma's care. Camp equipment was stowed in the rafters of grandpa's barn; three big boxes were packed with things to be brought on with the girls later; there were lastminute purchases and travelers' checks to be got; my new car was sold and my ticket was in my purse at last. Then my passport; ghastly passport pictures; my visa.

We consoled ourselves at parting: "It won't be long now." On the twin's twelfth birthday, I sailed through the Golden Gate bound for "Eden, or what have you?" 

Ten days at sea. Time to catch up on some reading, and write long letters to be posted back from Tahiti. There were ship friends, deck games, and long hours spent in the bow where the spray flew up, and the lunge of the boat could be felt most.

Others on board were escaping from the madness they call civilization, mostly rich folk up in

first class. They wanted to trade the roar of downtown traffic for the wash of breakers and a coral reef.

I could see us, a snug little family of four, in a native house. Day and night, the trade winds would blow through our windows, innocent of shades, glass or drapes. A giant mango tree would drop its golden fruit at our front door, and avocados must fall on the thatched roof of our kitchen. There were to be no bills coming down on us like a snow storm at the end of every month. 

I was to escape cars and driving for the first time in years. What price the simple life? 

The first night I saw the Southern Cross our new home seemed very near. The next evening most of us "third-classers" missed our tea, because we couldn't tear away from the rail. Our eyes were glued on a dim green line in the distance: palm trees in the sunset; one of the low islands. 

At dawn, we got our first glimpses of Tahiti and her little sister isle, Moorea. Were we thrilled? It was all as lovely as the novelists had said. The ship stalked down the reef waiting for full light and the pilot boat which would lead her through the break opposite the port of Papeete, capitol of Tahiti.

Inside the harbor, red roofs of the town, a church spire and flambouyant trees along the circle of the waterfront. French doctors in white ducks and flower wreathes, coming on board for inspection. Did we have any diseases which would bar

us from paradise? No. The ship moved on again and slid up to the quay.

Boat crowds below; natives in shorts and dungarees; French in spotless white and pith helmets, greeting friends; Chinese merchants waiting for cargo; fat women selling flowers; little boys diving for coins; pretty half-castes with their 

European lovers. The porters were coming up to carry luggage to the customs' sheds. Two native girls followed with white flower strings over their arms. Were they for sale? But, no, the girls were flinging them over our shoulders with a greeting, then running away giggling.

We're off the boat, and the ground seems strange underfoot; we'd feel more at home if it would rock just a little. Most of Papeete's cars have gathered near the wharf. "Taxi, taxi, madam?" All the guide books speak of the "old world flavor" of Papeete. I didn't taste it, but I smelled it. The acrid odor of copra in the storage sheds was a little sickening at first. (Later I came to love the pungent scent-it seems like the essence of Tahiti). There were odd smells around the

Chinese stores, and every time we passed a group of young belles or dandies, thick clouds of cheap perfume were almost visible. 

Perhaps, on the day I landed, I would not have been so willing to recommend Tahiti. I took a trip by car with some friends from the boat, and we were terribly disappointed with the looks of the native homes. They were rundown shacks which reminded me of the Mexican and Indian

huts near the Arizona border towns I had just left. 

Had I come thousands of miles for such a litter of trash and dirt? I was almost ready to take the same boat back to the states. "But madame must realize," said the driver, "that these homes are only cheap imitations of the Papeete bungalows. You must go to the more remote districts to see the unspoiled Polynesian life." "Go around the island on a bicycle, or on foot,"

I was advised. "Stay in the natives' homes and see how they live. You can get lodging for a few francs a night, or better still, for a few trinkets, for many of the islanders refuse money for hospitality." 

So I stocked up with talcum powder, cheap scent, a few brilliant combs and some soap that could be smelled from a distance. Stowing these gifts and my sleeping pareau in a small basket,

I boarded the bus for Taravao and the far end of Tahiti.

Bits of Tahitian life flashed by the bus like pictures: women washing clothes in a clear stream, fishermen poised in their canoes with spears upraised, little naked boys splashing in warm salt shallows, young girls bathing in pools, their red and white pareaus clinging to beautifully moulded bodies.

I had left the bus and was walking on to Tautira when night overtook me . . .and here I was tucked away in this "wondrous and enormous bed."

. 

One word at a time. Everybody hulas. The original fireless cooker.

Sunshine was streaming in the open door when I awoke this morning. The natives' mats were in neat rolls on the floor, their coverings folded away for the day. The moment I opened my eyes a small girl who had been left on watch ran to tell her mother.

The older daughter of the house motioned for me to follow her down a narrow path through a lantana thicket to a small pool. A log smoothed off to a flat surface made a step down to the water level for convenience in bathing. Here was the “simple life" with no expense for plumbing. 

An underground stream fed the pool, for it was not at all brackish, though it lay within 50 feet of the lagoon shore.

I tried to imagine us living beside such a pool. 

Would we be completely satisfied with such bathing arrangements? At least we would not be

bothered by leaky faucets or plumber's bills, and visiting babies would be in no danger of swallowing the plug of the bathtub! 

While I bathed, the girl picked "Tiare Tahiti" and arranged the flowers in her hair. When I joined her she tucked some over my ears as part of her morning greeting. Then and there I decided that our homesite must have a tiare bush somewhere.

The mother was working in the cooking shed, a little distance from the sleeping house. The true 

Tahitian homes are all made in two buildings, for open fires under palm thatch are too dangerous to risk losing the entire home. The kitchen walls were bamboo, split and woven. The floor was trodden earth, covered with beach sand.

A rough board table with benches marked the dining room end of the building. On a shelf were a few battered tin spoons, some carved wooden spoons, an assortment of china bowls and a few cracked plates. I was intrigued with six fat yellow gourds on the shelf. They had been hollowed out and dried. Their necks had been cut off evenly so they fitted on the gourds smoothly for lids. To the amusement of my hostess I peeked into them and found rice, flour and brown sugar. Two held some native foods that I couldn't recognize, and the last was full of clean sea water for cooking. 

So you don't even buy salt here! I heard of a man who lived royally on Bora Bora for a year at the rate of $1 per month. Maybe Harry and I can do that too.

I was wondering what these people ate. The answer hung in the kitchen end of the cook shed: enormous bunches of bananas, smaller bunches of the wild mountain bananas called fei, breadfruit, ripe oranges, a string of fish freshly caught from the lagoon, taro and a palm basket bulging with a rootish-looking food that I couldn't name. 

All these foods had been gathered by the family from mountain and valley.

My hostess picked up a long slasher knife of the machete type and struck a quick blow on a coconut that halved it neatly. The water spilled over the ground. She sat on the only piece of funiture in the kitchen-a low stool made from a tree trunk with natural legs that had been branches. The top side of the trunk was flattened off to make a seat, the fore part was shaped out into a tapering nose, something like a toy horse with its neck stretching out in front of the rider.

On the end of the neck was a piece of iron that flared out, fan-shaped, with tiny notches filed in its outer edges making a set of sharp little teeth. 

She pushed the half nut down over the teeth rapidly in sure strokes and the white meat fell in a shower to a wooden bowl. She brought a thin mesh of fibers, put some of the shredded nut in it, and squeezed with a strong wringing motion. Thick white milk oozed through her fingers and trickled into a bowl. So this is the way you get cream in the South seas.

Soon the bowl was half full. I pointed and said, "Milk."

Immediately the woman pointed and said, "Haari." I tried to write it on the white sand floor with a stick. "Ha-ah," I wrote, but the girl took the stick from me and wrote plainly, "Haari."

I took an old envelope from the pocket of my slacks and copied the word. Both showed childish pleasure when they saw I meant to keep the word they had taught me. 

Picking up a coconut, my hostess said, "Opaa."

This was simple. I spelled it correctly in the sand and the girl clapped her hands at my progress. 

She touched the oranges and said, "Anani." When this had been written in the sand, then transferred to my paper, they fairly crowed with pleasure, saying, "Maitai, maitai"-good, good-over and over. Pointing to herself, the girl told me her name was Tapura. She tried to say my name but the unfamiliar sound of "s" gave her trouble; "Hes-s-s-tair," she said, in a long hiss. 

Tapura led me to the sleeping house. The bed had been restored to its high smoothness. Its four fat mattresses had been plumped up and the five pillows were again marching across the head of the bed in a precise line. A small table and a "store" chair held the center of the room.

Green leaves made a tablecloth, arranged in overlapping rows. Two oranges and a banana were waiting for me in a nest of red leaves. In another "nest" was a loaf of French bread with crisp crust. A bowl held coarse-grained Tahitian sugar. 

Another bowl had three leaves in it.

They motioned me to the table. The three leaves

in the bowl looked familiar but not edible. They left the room and I sniffed the leaves. Familiar scent, too, but I couldn't think what it was. I bit the tip of one and found it bitter. Did they expect me to eat them? I was looking for the primitive life, but surely there were limits. But, yes, for 

Tapura returned bringing a can of boiling water, which she poured on the leaves. The fragrance of oranges came up from the steaming bowl. 

Orange leaves for tea, of course!

The bowl of brown sugar was offered with a little spoon carved of bamboo. Then the bowl of "haari" to give richness to the tea. Delicious!

At the end of the meal Tapura brought a finger bowl made of a coconut shell. I dipped my fingers in the bowl and said, "Water." "Papi," they both responded eagerly, and helped me write it, laughing.

I put on my hat and took up my basket to leave. The laughter died from their faces. Both took my hand with the round-eyed solemnity of children. I started to open my purse. "Aita, aita," they both cried, putting up their hands in protest. 

They didn't have to tell me that "aita" meant "no." I put my purse away and tried to write "aita" on my paper. In a moment we were off on that thrilling word game again, their faces all smiles. "Thank you, thank you," I said, but they didn't understand. I tried "merci" in French. Their eyes lit with comprehension. "Maruru, maruru," they exclaimed, and wrote their Tahitian "thank you"

on my paper. Eagerly, they practiced: "Thank you, thank you," in English. 

Taking one of the combs from my basket, I pressed it into Tapura's hand. “Aita, aita," she protested. "A souvenir," I said, hoping the expression was near enough to French so she would understand. She looked at me doubtfully a moment, then, "thank you," she breathed. ducking her head shyly. The mother put the comb in Tapura's black hair, chattering to her in pleasure. Tapura ran to the carved sea chest where their treasures were kept and took out a small mirror. She danced about the room, trying the comb in various ways and admiring it vastly. 

Her mother followed her about, admiring the comb and the girl together. Such excitement over a 10-cent comb! 

I left them smiling and waving at their doorway. "Ioarana. Thank you," they called after me. 

And "ioarana, maruru," I waved back to them.

I rested on the beach and watched some native women wading in the shallows gathering shellfish.

Far out in the purple lagoon were specks of canoes and black dots that were people diving for shellfish in the deeper water.

A woman returned from her fishing along the outer reef, paddling strongly, her 2-year-old baby asleep in the bow. She shoved her canoe up to its rack of palm logs, and stepped out in the shallow water. "Ioarana," we exchanged greetings.

I admired a string of red and blue-green

beauties that looked as though they might have been dipped up from a well stocked aquarium of tropical fish. I touched one and said, “Fish.” She stared a moment, then a smile flashed. She touched it, too, and said "Aiya." I wrote the word on the old envelope that carried my week-old Tahitian vocabulary. 

She looked at my list of words, saw Tapura's name there and touched her breast saying, "Tariri," so I added her name to my list. She read on, “Mimi ori maramarama," and burst into giggles when she came to "heri heri"-sweetheart. 

Down the list she read until reaching "hula hula"; she was so pleased she gave a little shake to hips and shoulders that sent both off into gales of laughter. "Hula hula oe?" she inquired, pointing to me, and put her hands on my hips trying to move them around in approved Tahitian dance fashion. "Hula hula oe," I told her, and stood back to show her I would be her audience. She pulled a red hibiscus flower and stuck it over her right ear; another went into the front of her dress; she loosened her long black hair from its orange comb so it tumbled down over her shoulders to her waist; she tied her red pareau about her hips so the long ends would swish in the manner of a grass skirt.

From the canoe she took a half coconut shell and showed me how to beat a syncopated rhythm on the side of the outrigger. She was pleased when I struck the rhythm correctly the first time

-she couldn't know it was the same kind I'd heard so many times on the dance floors of America. 

She walked a few paces along the shining black sand beach and stood with arms raised like a bronze statue. I began beating the time and her body came to life. Arms, shoulders, breasts, hips and ankles were animated in perfect co-ordination. 

The hula was of the sea. Her arms told of waves leaping, and of the strong paddles. The baby woke up and clapped his hands in time to his mother's dancing. With a long tremulous shaking of her body the dance ended. 

I clapped loudly and called, "Maitai, maitai." 

She beamed, then grew shy at the praise. The baby climbed from the canoe and played with pebbles on the beach. Tariri rested and regarded me with great friendliness. By signs and a few words I told her I had come from America. She pronounced it "Amerita," and gave it a new flavor in my ears. "Hula hula oe Amerita?" she inquired. I nodded. She wanted to see an American dance. I tried to explain my need of a dancing partner, and she gravely offered me a paddle. 

With it clasped to my heart. I waltzed around as smoothly as I could on the sloping sand beach. 

When I started to leave she pointed to her house across the road and signed that I should stop for dinner. Perhaps this would be a good chance to learn something about Polynesian cookery. Cleaning fish was no problem for Tariri.

She rinsed them, dipped banana leaves into the sea, then wrapped the fish in a leaf.

We crossed the road to her cooking house, and Tariri opened her oven, and put the leaf-wrapped package in on the hot lava rocks, a sort of fireless cooker, it was. A hole in the ground and some porous rocks. The stones were heated each morning, and put in the hole with the food. Thick pads of breadfruit leaves on top kept the heat and moisture in. As simply as that, dinner was on to cook!

I can't help but regret the passing of the pareau as the island costume. The wash dresses the native women wear are so commonplace but 

I'm here a hundred years too late, I guess. 

The days of grass skirts-except for dancingare gone, and the pareaus are worn only for bathing or informal use about the 'home. For "dress-up" the islanders demand American clothes.

Tariri set our table in the cook house with four banana leaves for our cloth. Our plates were large round leaves. The fish came out of its wrappings, hot and tasty. The other food which came from the depths of the rock oven was "poi," baked so firm we broke it off in pieces like bread. Ripe bananas and oranges finished the meal. 

I came here looking for simplicity. I found it. 

There were no dishes to wash. No tablecloth to send to the laundry, the leaves were tossed into the sea.

This would be a lovely spot for our homesite. 

It's just beyond the end of the road where the truck turns back for its return trip to Papeete. 

It's peaceful here and primitive. A rough track

leads on among coco-palms and wild growth. Cars cannot travel it. Only the Chinaman's two wheeled cart comes this far carrying bread to the homes that scatter along the beach for a few miles farther. And the Chinaman's horse has to know his business, too. Like most of the island horses, he is about the size of a year old colt at home, but he takes his cart through mud and over rocks, across the sandy beach and through the salt shallows, and he plunges belly-deep through the streams that are not bridged. We'd never smell exhaust fumes here!

Is this where we shall build our little house and escape civilization for a year? I remember reading in some books on Tahiti that "a native house may be built for 10 francs-about 60 cents, the cost of the nails.” I examined Tariri's house. 

Every post and pole in it had been cut from the forest. The walls were upright bamboo poles, with little cracks between to let light and air in, so there were no windows. The floor was split and woven bamboo. The roof was "niao" thatch. 

I hunted high and low but couldn't find even one nail. The whole building was put together with fiber and wooden pegs. 

I stayed over night with Tariri. There was breadfruit and coffee for supper. After dark, with a lantern glowing inside her bird cage house, and light streaming out of its cracks, it looked like a giant Japanese lantern. What a setting for the simple life!

! 

The ladies roll their own. Shrimp are there if you can see them. His name was Areiimana-so they called him Lizzie.

I reached the end of the trail of the Chinaman's cart today, and found that a foot trail carries on for a few miles further through a wilderness of tangled growth. Here native huts are far apart, with tiny clearings here and there planted with yams, pineapples or tarua. The stillness is scarcely broken by the wash of the waves on the reef. 

Crossing a point of land, I came to a little bay, where a naked brown baby, about two, splashed by himself at the water's edge. A woman's voice came over the still air, singing Tahitian words. to the tune of "My Blue Heaven." Such a combination of words and tune piqued my curiosity. I listened. The child looked up and saw me. His eyes widened with wonder and then filled with fright. His mouth opened in a long wail and he

fled as fast as his fat legs could go. 

The song stopped. I followed the child, wondering how I could make peace with his mother. She was squatting by a small stream, washing clothes. 

The baby hid behind her and peeked at me through his tears. I greeted her, “Ioarana," and she smiled broadly and pointed to my sun glasses. Then it was I realized that those big yellow glasses had frightened the child. I quickly snatched them off and held out a small pocket mirror to him as a peace offering which his mother promptly substituted for a shell and tucked the mirror in her gown for safe keeping.

The baby pointed at me and said accusingly, "Popaa, popaa," so I decided I must be a popaa. "Popaa?" pointing to myself. The mother nodded, "Vahine popaa." Then pointing to herself, she explained "Vahine Tahiti." So that was easy, I was a white woman, she was a Tahitian woman.

She told me her name was Mahana, and invited me to rest and chat. I rather wanted to see her get on with her washing. When we get settled down for our year of primitive living we shall be some 6000 miles from the nearest steam laundry, and I've never done a family washing in my life.

A loose dam made of woven palm leaves across the stream held the clothes she had already washed. Below her was another leaf dam holding the pieces that were "soaking." A bar of soap a foot long, of a violent blue, was beside her. She squatted by a large flat stone, just above water level. She pulled a tattered shirt from the soaking

pond and spread it on the rock, rubbing it with soap, wadded it up, then beat it vigorously with a stick. 

She tossed the wad up, flipped it over, soaped the other side and beat it hard and long. Tossing, soaping, beating, with occasional dips into the clear water. In the end the shirt was beautifully white-what was left of it!

Mahana's code of labor called for a rest between ach piece of washing. From the fold of her pareau she took an old baking powder can-her cigaret makings case. Out came her paper in a familiar-looking little orange book, and she rolled a smoke. She sent the boy to a pile of stones where coconut husks were smoldering. He pulled out one and took it to her. She blew on it till the embers glowed. Matches were scarce, it was 10 miles by foot or canoe to the nearest store and this original Tahitian cigaret lighter worker over and over during the afternoon.

Mahana rested and played with the little mirror. 

She turned it about, catching the sun in it and playing the beam on leaves and flowers as a 

10-year-old might. "Mirror," I told her. "Meero," she chanted, "Me-ro, mee-ro." "From America," 

I added. "Aue! Amerita maitai," she responded. 

With my scant Tahitian vocabulary I tried to tell her of my trip on foot around Tahiti. She listened politely, but it was not Tahiti she wanted to hear about, but far-away "Amerita." In one of her rare visits to Papeete she had seen a train pictured in a western movie.

Did the black smoke pouring from the engine mean the train was afire? Was it true there were beds in the train and people could really sleep while tearing along at such a reckless speed? 

And had I ever seen the cowboys leap from the moving trains to their horses? Even more amazing to her was the thought of people living so far inland they couldn't see the ocean every day. And no canoes-dreadful! And no coral reef where they could gather shellfish? And was it true that some people in "Amerita" did not eat fish every day? Nor yet every week? And some did not eat fish for months together? And if the growing boys and girls did not eat fish every day how did they ever learn to swim?

Mahana's laundry was finished at last and spread to dry on grass and bushes. The long bar of soap was only a remnant and the makings in her can were almost gone. With the ready hospitality of the islanders she invited me to her house for the night. She directed the boy to bring one of the smoldering husks to light the supper fire.

She filled two five-gallon tins with fresh water, hung them on the notched ends of a carrying pole, and easily swung the 80-pound burden to her shoulder. Will I ever be able to do that? The child followed, holding the husk behind him to avoid stepping on coals that might drop. He had a store of primitive wisdom at two years.

Mahana's home was furnished more simply than any I have seen yet. There were no bed-

steads. The fat home-made kapok mattresses were on the bamboo floor in the four corners of the room. The plump pillows leaned against the walls. A wooden chest held the night covers and the family clothing. A low table, hand hewn from a breadfruit tree trunk held a lamp and a box of matches the only modern touches in the house. Or is a lamp modern? ness. 

Mahana's husband and her young brother came from the mountains bringing fei, taro and breadfruit. They held an excited conference over me, and the brother was sent off on some urgent busi- He returned in about a half hour with a young native who had worked on one of the interisland schooners for a few years and had picked up some English. They had sent for him to interpret for me, and help entertain where they felt their own efforts might fall short. It was another expression of the deep natural courtesy of these people. 

The newcomer's name was Areiimana but he confided to me that on the boat he had been called 

Lizzie, and he would be happy if I would call him so. He was a splendid physical type, strong and graceful in all his motions. His clothing was a single strip of blue and white pareau. 

The family and Lizzie went into another huddle, and presently asked me if I would like to go shrimping up the stream in the morning. Lizzie would show me how to "bring 'em back alive," and teach me something of the mystery of getting food from the mountain jungles. And if I would

like to hear some native music, he would send word out for all the neighbors to gather for an "Arearea" tomorrow night.

This was better than anything I had hoped for. I turned in on one of the kapok mattresses to dream of us living here like nature people, picking our food from the wilderness. 

Our breakfast was simple this morning: home-grown coffee, oranges from the mountains, and a concoction of dough to take the place of bread. In this lonely part of the island the natives follow many customs of a hundred years ago, and this family still uses the old style 'faraoa' instead of bread.

Mahana's husband was the cook. He mixed flour and coconut milk in the shell of a giant sea turtle. He dropped lumps of the dough into a can of boiling sea water and let them cook about 

10 minutes. They were the primitive first cousins of dumplings. 

Lizzie brought two spears, a light small one for me. Both were home-made from some native wood, whittled and smoothed. The six prongs on the heads were made of light iron pieces, filed to slender barbs.

We started up the stream where I had found 

Mahana washing yesterday. We stepped from rock to rock so the clear pools would not be disturbed. Soon Lizzie stopped with spear held high. "You want to spear him?" asked Lizzie.

"I can't even see him," I had to admit.

So Lizzie plunged his spear and pulled it up with a brown shrimp struggling on the prongs. 

He stripped out the rib of a coconut leaf, knotted the end, and impaled the shrimp on it for easy carrying. Lizzie constantly added to his string. "Popaa no good to see shrimps," I confessed. "How in the world do you see so many, Lizzie?" "Tahiti boy go spear shrimp when he is very young. Then he learn to see them." "Here, I show you how to catch shrimp without seeing." So he turned away from the stream with its deep pools and followed up a tiny branch with sluggish water creeping among rocks, fallen leaves and mud pockets. Squatting down, Lizzie moved a rock then quickly spread his hands over the hole he had uncovered. Sure enough, something wiggled under his fingers and he held up a squirming shrimp for me to see. "This one just a baby. I put him back to grow," and Lizzie tossed it back in the water. So I began lifting stones and pressing my hands down in the mud. Something wiggled under my palms and 

I triumphantly caught my first shrimp.

Slowly we worked our way upstream, adding to our string of shrimp all the time. We came to an open space where a dilapidated native hut stood. The glade seemed filled with the sweet odor of a candy shop. I sniffed. "That's vanilla," said Lizzie. "Come. I show you."

The vanilla vines were all about among the

trees and bushes, trained up on stakes head high. 

Lizzie picked one of the long, smooth brown beans for me. It looked for all the world like a stick of chocolate candy, and smelled like one, too. "Mahana's husband plant this vanilla. When they come to pick it they stay up here three, four days and use this little house." The hut had one room with earthen floor. A platform of bamboo across one end served for a bed. A coconut horse was the only article in sight. "You like to eat here, Tahiti style, as we do in the mountains ?” asked Lizzie. "Indeed I would." I didn't see anything to eat but our shrimps, but I felt sure Lizzie knew his business. "Then we gather food."

We left the shrimps hanging in the little hut and started up a narrow trail that led us out of the glade, and steeply up the mountain side. 

Soon we were in a region thickly grown with fei plants, the wild mountain banana of the islands. Much of the fruit was green, but Lizzie found a bunch that had turned yellow, with deep pumpkin color on the tips. On the way back to the hut he stopped by a plant with big green leaves that reminded me of the elephant-ear plants at home. Digging, he uncovered its roots, and pulled up two brown tubers. “Tarua, just like your Irish potato from America."

He had a fire going in record time. He carried matches in a small tin in a fold of his pareau.

He wrapped the shrimps, tarua and fei in banana leaves and put them in to bake. 

Lizzie picked two papayas from a tree in the neglected garden. He climbed a coco palm and cut two green nuts for drinking, slashed off the upper ends and there were two bowls brimming full of cool coconut water. Then he husked an old nut, and I had my first lesson in shredding the meat on the coconut horse.

We looked about among the banana plants and quickly found a little ripe fruit. The bananas are always picked green and taken to the cooking houses to ripen, for if they are left out till they yellow, the rats and minah birds feast on them.

I sliced our bananas into the hollows of the halved papayas, then with a little pile of shredded coconut on top, our fruit salads were ready. We milked some of the coconut meat into a half shell and had the cream ready to pour over the taroa and fei. 

Lizzie carved two little wooden spoons like paddles, and I spread some broad banana leaves on the grass for our table. Presently our dinner was ready to come from its leaf wrappings. And what a dinner! The shrimps were tender, the fei tasty and the taroa was just liked baked potato. 

What a land where one could pick such a meal from the trees and streams! The rest of the world with its depression and its bread lines seemed very far away. We had dined royally from the land with the help of one match from civilization.

A PARTY IN THE SOUTH SEAS. 

Jungle drums and Tahitian Charleston. 

Returned from our shrimping trip, with long siestas finished, Lizzie remarked that it was time to prepare for our party and dance of the evening. "Now what will happen?" I wondered. They won't call a caterer or a florist, they won't hire a jazz band or a dance floor. Neither would the electrician be called to fix up special lighting effects, for Mahana's husband set about collecting dead palm fronds to make torches for the evening.

The flares were set up in holes spaced around a circle of smooth grass.

Mahana set out a five-gallon jug of home-made wine with plenty of kick. It was made of “fei” and oranges by a process I hope to learn some day. Wine glasses were 20 or so half coconut shells.

We gathered flowers to make "heis" and crowns for the guests. At the nearest Tiare Tahiti shrub 

Mahana spread a pareau on the grass. Lizzie shook the bush until the flowers fell in a shower. 

We piled them on the pareau. 

We three sat on the ground and I had my first lesson in stringing a hei 

And now we heard singing in the distance,

and a thump-thump-thump with it that sounded like jungle drums. We walked the few steps to the beach to await their coming. Full dark had come, and Mahana's husband lit the circle of torches. They burned with a red glare, lighting up the lagoon edge, trees, leaves and flowers. A torch appeared through the trees, moving steadily along on the other side of the near point of land. 

The voices were clear on the night air, singing a 

Tahitian boat song with strong rhythm. The drum was loud, then soft, and changed from simple to intricate beats as the mood of the song prompted. "The friends come soon," said Lizzie. "What kind of drum have they?" "That no drum," Lizzie laughed, "that coconut shell hit canoe."

The canoe rounded the point and was soon threading its way through the opening of the inner reef where the stream poured its cool water into the lagoon.

The men were in white shirts and trousers, incredible as it seems, and most of the women were in crisp white dresses. I was the shabby one in blue shirt and slacks. We flung heis to them and when they were crowned, Lizzie presented each one to me. They loved the ceremony of formal introductions and handshaking. I'm almost sure that several of them came around a second time in line.

Two more torches came in sight down the lagoon with the strumming of ukuleles and a guitar. The canoes paddled side by side, their

A PARTY IN THE SOUTH SEAS 

61 twin flares reflected in the black water, flinging a red-lit path to our feet. Men and women sang a slow song in two-part harmony. "What are all their songs about?" I asked Lizzie. "They sing of the mountains, flowers and love, now," he said. "Many times we sing of the sea and the big fish we take in the nets. Or we sing of the orange and fei that make the wine. Or maybe a picnic by the pool and the hei we make. 

All the things that make us happy, we sing." 

When all the guests had beached their canoes, and the last round of handshaking was over, four young men with ukuleles and a guitar sat down cross-legged on a mat. "We are proud of our American clothes," Lizzie confided to me. But there was not a pair of shoes in the entire gathering! It seemed a little odd at first to see bare brown toes peeping below a white frilly dress that nearly swept the grass. 

Somebody shouted "Papio, papio," and the orchestra boys swung into a lively tune. All joined in the singing with vigor. "What's this 'papio' all about, Lizzie?" "This one of our most liked songs. Always we 

Tahiti love this song. It tell of the fete time when the horses that fly come to Papeete." "Horses that fly?" "Yes, go nowhere, but round and round." "Oh, a merry-go-round. Flying horses, of course." 99 "Yes. Tahiti boy sing to his sweetheart to come

with him on flying horses, for he is young and she is young, too." "It's a child's song then, Lizzie?" "No, this song for Tahiti man and womanbut Tahiti man and woman like flying horses same as children. Always white people come here and say we are children." Lizzie smiled knowingly. "They speak true." 

Other songs followed. They sang in two and three parts, easily and naturally. They carried difficult harmonies perfectly, as a trained chorus might in the States.

Three girls went to the sleeping house and changed to pareaus and grass skirts, and loosened their black hair from their combs. There was enthusiastic clapping as they appeared, and some of the men and women took off their wreaths to fling them over the girls' shoulders until they were loaded with flowers. Voices were hushed. 

Ukuleles and guitar twanged in a slow melody and the girls danced with native grace. 

Arms, shoulders and hips seemed to flow from one lovely posture to another.

At the close of the hula they swayed over and knelt before me a moment, dropping some flowers on the grass to show honor to the white guest.

The wine had made a couple of trips around the circle by now, and the music boys turned loose a lively hula. The girls' bodies quivered with action. Lizzie told me they represented the palm trees with fronds rustling in the trade winds, but it looked more like a hurricane. Several times

A PARTY IN THE SOUTH SEAS 

63 one or another of the young men leaped up and danced with the girls. They looked quite convincing, even in their conventional clothes. 

When someone called "Charleston" I thought 

I wasn't hearing right. But yes, a girl in a slinky silk dress, clutched it up about her knees, and went into the familiar pigeon-toed steps while the boys pounded out "That's My Baby Now," with a full set of Tahitian words.

I was reminded to ask Lizzie about hearing 

Mahana sing "My Blue Heaven," and he assured me the tunes were the same I had known for years, but the native words were not an interpretation of the English. The tunes had traveled here on victrola records, but the first native to learn the melody had put to it any words he fancied about the sea, fishing trips, love or anything that had come to his mind. 

Each familiar tune I heard during the evening was a fresh surprise; "Ta-Ra-Ra-Boom-De-A," "My Texas Home" with a yodel between stanzas, and their newest one from America is "Isle of Capri."

The last of the palm flares were beginning to die as they launched their canoes. A thousand stars lit the sky and their drowned reflections winked in the still lagoon. The friends paddled away, their lights growing smaller, their voices more distant, until we stood in the little clearing alone, with no sound but the roar of the in-coming tide on the reef. 

So this was a party in the South Seas!

END OF THE FOOT TRAIL. 

Poor Tahitian Butterfly. 

Leaving Mahana and Lizzie this morning was like saying goodby to old friends. At last the foot trail ended in a river too deep to wade, so I turned back toward Tautira and civilization. 

I could not go farther for here the black lava cliffs drop sheer into the sea with such rough seams and deep chasms that not even the natives attempt to cross. From this point travel is by canoe. 

Some strange cargoes pass out here: copra, fei, bananas, with chickens tied on top of the load; a calf bound with purao fiber sticks his head over the side of a dugout; squealing pigs protesting against a sea-going trip to the nearest village, eight miles away.

I met a family moving to a new home, nearer

END OF THE FOOT TRAIL 

65 civilization. Their worldly goods were carried on two canoes tied together. A bedstead bridged the space between the canoes and mattresses were piled high. It was a family of means, judged by native standards, for two chairs and a table rode atop the bedding, and-sure sign of opulencethe load was crowned with a sewing machine.

A man paddled slowly, while along the shore came the rest of the family with small articles. 

Surprisingly enough, a white woman led the group.

I rested and waited for them to pass, thinking the white woman might speak English. As she came closer I saw that she was young, and was astonished at her beauty. Bright hair with gold caught in it. Cream and roses skin. The blueviolet eyes of a viking's daughter. "Good morning," I called. She answered me with the island greeting "Ioarana." An older woman of native blood came up, carrying a sleeping baby on one hip and a small old-fashioned box graphaphone on the other. She startled me by saying, "Good morning madame." "You speak English?" "Yes, madame, a little. Long ago I speak much, but I forget now. When I young girl I work for white American lady. She teach me." "You lived on Tahiti then ?" I asked. "Yes, madame, in ver' fine house." She pointed back in the direction she had come from. "Is the American lady still there?" "No madame. She die long ago. Her man go

back to America. Her children go away. Her fine house it-it-," she was groping for half-forgotten words, "it die, too."

She put the graphaphone and the baby down and sat down to talk. 

She must have been nearing 40, and most island women are old at 40. She had been a beauty as a girl, and her black hair was not yet touched with gray. She had the lustrous eyes and long lashes of the Polynesians, but her features were almost European. "The kind American lady take me when my mother die. I live in her grand house. I love her.

I work for her. She teach me to speak the English. 

I never read or write, but all the time I speak the English. You know?" "Yes," I nodded. "Long ago I ver' pretty girl" She stated a fact, without vanity. "When I sixteen her husband's friend come to visit. He ver' white American man. Ah, I love him ver' much. Always he paint the so beautiful picture. The sea. The tiare flowers. 

Me. He love me ver' much. Always he tell me I am so beautiful. We ver' happy." Her voice was a caress. "We make little house together. We love much. 

One year two year we love. Always his mama write him the letter-come back to America. But all the time he love me too much. "I sing for him about the sea, the mountains. 

I play guitar for him in the dusk. We walk on the beach. We swim in lagoon when the moon is

END OF THE FOOT TRAIL 

67 big. I dance for him the hula. And always we love much."

The white girl sat and watched the woman's face. The brown children were playing a little game with shells on the beach. Her eyes came back to me. "There was one big war, you know? World 

War they call it? Yes? When American men go to war, then he must go, too. I could not have him leave me, but how is it you say he had the duty? Yes? Then I mus' let him go. I mus' smile, but my heart is-is stone. I think I never dance or sing again." 

She sat a long time staring out at the sea. "You never saw him again?" I asked. "No time more." Then her eyes came back to the present and she said, "But I have the letterhere."

She took a small leather tobacco pouch from the fold of her pareau and opened it. "I said that I never read the English. He write me the so beautiful letter." She pulled a worn paper from the pouch and smoothed it out on her knee. "You read the English? Yes? You will read the letter?" It has been sewed to a white cloth to preserve it. "Flower of Tahiti," I read, "when shall I hear you again singing to me in the dusk? When shall I feel the ripple of your soft hair again. 

When shall your slender hands place the white tiare again around your shoulders and mine, together in love?"

I glanced up wondering if she wanted me to continue. She nodded, and prompted me, "when shall I see you dancing," she knew the letter by heart! "dancing in the moonlight?

When shall I hold your warm beauty again to my heart?" 

The whole letter was the cry of a dreamer for his beloved dream. It ended, "My thoughts go to you from the ends of the earth." It was signed with a Tahitian name "Tamarii Taina.' "That not American name," she told me gravely. "That Tahiti name I call him. That say 'White Youth,' for he ver' white, but blue sea in his eyes." 

I glanced at the girl with the blue eyes. The woman touched her lightly with one finger as though she were so precious she might break. "This his baby," she said simply. Her name 

Pupure. That say 'Fair One.' He never see Pupure, for she come to me after he go away."

The girl was well named. Nature had left out all resemblance to the dark mother, unless it was about her delicate mouth. The look the mother turned on the girl was almost idolatry. "Pupure like him. Same eyes, same hairsame white.” 

I folded the letter and returned it to her. "And this baby?" I asked. Surely this fat brown baby sleeping in the sun didn't belong to the 'Fair One.' "He my baby, too," answered the mother, "and those," waving to the youngsters on the beach.

END OF THE FOOT TRAIL 

69 "Six year I wait and wait. No more letter come. 

My papa tell me I must marry. Better have man to go in mountains for fei, to go to sea for fish. 

Better have sons to help when I am old. So I marry."

Her glance sought the distant canoe, a blur on the lagoon. "He good man. He work hard. He give me many things same as white people. Always he try to make me forget Tamarii Tainia. Now we go to fine house in Tautira. Sometimes American man come to Tautira. Sometimes paint pictures. 

Paint sea. 

Mountains. Flowers. You know?" "Yes. Artists." "Artists. Same as Tamarii. Maybe one American man come see Pupure. She ver' white. Maybe he love her same as me long ago. Maybe she live in little love house, same as me long ago. Maybe she be ver' happy."

She looked at me expectantly, and what could I say, but, "maybe."

POI? 

Terei and I go to market.

Making my way back to the village in the late afternoon, a native woman greeted me with "Hello, cowboy." Now here was real imagination! "You eat banana?" she inquired. "Yes, thank you, I'd like one," I answered, and sat down to rest on her doorstep. She bustled away to the cook house, but returned almost immediately with a whole bunch, and held them out to me. "You eat," she commanded. So this was her idea of a banana! That bunch must have weighed 50 pounds.

I accepted four bananas, and we began to talk. 

My Tahitian vocabulary had increased to about 100 words and she had about 200 in English, and a couple of women can go a long way on that many words. And this woman, Terei by name, was a genius with pantomime. Her questions came thick and fast and personal, but no matter what she asked, I knew she meant no rudeness.

She was only trying to get a little drama into her life. "Where are you going? Are you married? 

Where is your husband? He come to Tahiti? 

You got babies? Where babies?" 

She seemed delighted to think my family would join me in Tahiti. You ask how that could be a

POI? 

break for her? I didn't know, but she was eager for all the details. "Where you live bime'by? You like native house? Like French house? Your America house big?" "Where did you sleep last night? Where did you eat today? Where will you sleep tonight?"

When I admitted I didn't know, she joyously claimed me for her guest, and bustled out again to the cook house, bringing back a loaf and a half of French bread for me to eat with the bananas.

I was hungry, and I had walked some fifteen miles, so I ate about a third of the loaf. 

When I could eat no more Terei began again: "You got mamma and papa? Where they? How big? Eat much? Ride a bicycle? Wear a pareau?"

After I had described my husband, given the age and size of the children, answered questions about the mama and papa, it was time to begin on brothers, sisters and in-laws, according to Terei's formula. 

More questions were asked. "Got brothers? How many? Where? How big? Married? Got babies? Have sisters? How tall? Married? Babies? Ride a bicycle? And the husband has a mama and a papa? How big? 

Live in a big house? Husband's papa ride a bicycle? Have much land? Make copra? What!

No coconuts in America? Oh! Toi, Toi. Too cold. How strange!"

She can scarcely picture a land without coconut palms and bananas or fei, those weeds of the

South Seas, that grow without planting like milkweed and cockleburs at home. "And no breadfruit on the husband's papa's land? Hard to believe. No fei growing wild in the mountains for food? Too bad, too bad, what a sad state of affairs!"

She is pleased to hear, however, that the husband's papa has many orange trees on his land. 

That's one thing to eat, anyway. "You like Tahiti food? Poi? Fei? Avoca? 

Maoa?" I assure her I like them all. Then, "Name food you eat in America, no same Tahiti food?" I try describing American foods, but I soon get into deep water because she has no idea what most of our vegetables are like. Of course, she doesn't know their names, and I haven't a very good vocabulary of Tahitian. 

Terei is so eager I hate to let her down. I struggle on trying to give her a picture of a grocery store, with butcher shop, bakery counter and delicatessen counter. Her background for this picture is the poor, little shops of the Chinese, where an assortment of six kinds of canned goods at one time is a magnificent display. 

The one big general store in Papeete run along English lines is so grand in Terei's eyes, that she has never stepped inside. She has only peeked through the doors as she walked by, pretending not to look in.

Terei has seen the vegetables, raised by the Chinese, in the Papeete market, but many of our plants won't grow in the warmth of Tahiti. And

POI? 

how are you going to describe broccoli or artichokes without a full-grown vocabulary?

I wonder what sort of garbled picture poor Terei had in mind when we closed the subject of stores. 

So then my mamma, who lives in that strange place called Cleveland, never takes her canoe and goes to the reef to get shellfish? What-no reef? 

No coral reef? How can you ever get 'maoa' or 'pahua' then if there is no shallow water inside a reef? And what keeps the sharks from coming right up to the beaches if there is no reef? 

But Terei is worried about my mamma. Poor soul, she has no canoe and does not ride a bicycle. 

How then does she get about? Or possibly she rides in a car? Yes? Oh, wonderful to think of! For a few Fords in the family represent untold millions to her. 

Terei beams happily at the thought. She has never been in a car herself, but she knows they must be grand! "And the husband's papa, who lives near the sea in California, has no canoe either? Too bad. But maybe he has a motor boat then? Or he is very rich and has a trading schooner? No? No boat? 

But, ah, he has a car. Oh, that's better. Better than a canoe or boat. The husband's papa and mamma are then very, very, very rich if they have a car. Then the papa and the mamma do not tire their arms paddling a canoe, nor tire their legs pedaling a bicycle." 

Then if my family is so rich, my husband does not work? What! He works every day? But why?

Oh, to buy food. Now she remembers again that in that strange land the people cannot go out every day and gather food that grows wild, and there is no lagoon for fishing at every doorstep. 

So, no money, no food! Oh, sad! Sad! So the husband must work every day. Even Saturday? 

Surely he doesn't work on Saturday, for that is 'Mahana Maa'- 'day of food gathering,' when work with the copra or anything else is stopped so every man in the village can go to gather food from the mountains or shellfish from the lagoon. 

Even if I had Terei's entire language at the tip of my tongue I would not attempt to explain to her what it is to work overtime, on a Sunday or at night. She would never comprehend the reasons for such an absurdity. Sundays and evenings in Tahiti are for dancing, music and love. 

At sunset Terei began preparing supper. Her grown son served it to us on her little porch. I was expected to eat as much as she did, fish, poi, breadfruit and fei. We finally compromised on a bowl of Tahitian tea, and one bite each of the other things, so Terei would not be hurt and think 

I didn't like the food. She let me off when I explained it would make me sick if I ate more just before going to bed.

Terei spread a mat on the veranda for her two sons, turning them out of their bed. She moved into their usual sleeping place and insisted that I should take her bed, which was the only one with a real bedstead. She placed a chair to help me mount the five mattresses. She put me to bed

POI? 

and tucked me in as though I were a 3-year-old child. And should she massage my legs to take the tiredness out? And did my head ache? If so she would rub it. With the slightest encouragement she would have sung me a lullaby. Such was Terei! 

Next morning Terei's son served breakfast to us on the veranda. We had enormous bowls of strong coffee, a loaf of bread each and enough fruit for six people. I ate all I could, but Terei sat down and ate three times as much as I.

The sons took a canoe and paddled away to some land they owned at a distance to get bananas and firewood, to be traded to the Chinese bakery and storekeeper for their bread, sugar and tobacco.

Terei was busy all morning fixing poi and pahua ready to take to the market in Papeete. Both these foods she packed into hollow lengths of green bamboo, then tied fresh leaves over the top. I decided to go along with her.

We ate early so as to get started to market in good time, though I couldn't understand why we should leave her house at noon when we had to walk only two miles to Tautira Village to catch the 8 o'clock truck that night.

We had no burdens to carry except a small basket that Terei wove of a piece of palm frond just before we started. It was to carry 10 bananas in case we got hungry on the road. The sons were to bring the poi, pahua and other things by canoe to Tautira.

We started off at a slow gait and went perhaps 

200 yards when Terei stopped and said we would make heis and flower crowns. She shook fragrant white blossoms from a tree and we gathered them in her big straw hat. The palm rib for needle and the long fibers for threads she seemed to pick from the surrounding atmosphere. While 

I sat on the grass by the roadside clumsily stringing flowers, she wandered about gathering hibiscus, Tiare Tahiti and the small blue flowers these people love so well. 

Two elaborate wreaths were for me, she said, for who would go on a journey, long or short, without first decking himself and friends with flowers? 

I insisted that she should wear one wreath, so she let me put it around the crown of her flapping hat. Then with great ceremony she put the finest crown on my head. She wanted me to wear all three heis, and it took me some time to persuade her to have one about her neck. Then she spent at least 10 minutes arranging the rest of them about my neck and shoulders. But she was not suited yet, so we stopped at a nearby house where she picked four pink roses and wanted to add them all to my outfit. One more rose over each ear was about all I thought I could take on, so I arranged the others over her ears. 

After many thanks to each other for the flowers, and many little bows had been exchanged, we started on.

We must have walked a quarter of a mile when

POI? she said we would stop and rest on the white sand by a little bay. She rolled and smoked a cigaret.
