The next lap of our journey was probably a half mile, before she stopped at the home of some of her relatives to chat. We stayed an hour and the entire conversation was about me and my family in America. All the information she had got from me during the morning, and the night before was now relayed. This is their way of contacting the outside world, for they do not read books or newspapers. They've all been taught to read French, after a fashion, in the government schools but it never seems to occur to them to read for pleasure or mental stimulation, so they make the most of an occasional stranger. 

The next stop came a little way down the road where a clear stream made a pool waist deep, and we must stop for baths. Terei washed her hair and we sat in the sun while it dried, and she rolled and smoked more cigarets.

I thought we were going to get on faster after that because Terei looked at the sun and tried to figure out what time it might be, but I guess she was only wondering about the time, not worried, because within a quarter of a mile we stopped for another lengthy conversation, and more cigarets, with friends she met returning from the village.

Two more stops for smokes, and eating some of the bananas in our lunch basket, then at last, near sunset we came to the village beach. Here

Terei's sons were unloading their canoe. Terei kissed them and put flowers over their ears as though she hadn't seen them for a week. And now Terei said we must stretch out on the sand and take a real rest after such a hard journey. 

The sons wheeled the fruit in a hand push cart about a hundred yards to the Chinaman's store.

I noticed they were leaving one big bunch of bananas and some other fruit in the canoe. I was to take that with me to Papeete for a trifle of fruit in my hotel room. The bunch weighed about 

200 pounds! After a long pow-wow concerning bananas and hotel rooms, we compromised. I accepted 15 bananas, some mangoes and a whole pineapple-but refused the raw shellfish, the 10 coconuts and the yard lengths of bamboo filled with poi which they had planned to give me. 

When the canoe was unloaded and beached on its back of palm logs, we all went to the home of another of Terei's many sons. Here I was immediately put on a sofa to rest. When I was allowed to get up I was led to the shower for another bath. Apparently the baths of the afternoon had worn off, for Terei took another one, too, and so did the other sons and all the family members.

The sons cooked supper and made the tea, while Terei took me about the garden. She discarded some of the flowers of the afternoon, and tucked fresh ones in my hair, around my ears, and filled my shirt pocket with Tiare Tahiti, and stuck one blossom in each button hole. But she couldn't get

POI? 

ahead of me. I tucked them in her hair, too. We had a great time thanking each other for them.

Presently the sons called us to supper at a special little round table set for Terei and me, while they and the rest of the large family ate at a long table in another room. 

The boys waited on us, bringing fresh tea and different kinds of fruit at intervals. The main dish of the supper was corned beef, out of a giant-sized can from Australia. They thought it a great delicacy, and it was bought to do me honor. I was expected to eat half of the can while the other half was divided in tiny portions among 

10 or more people.

I dislike corned beef, but under the circumstances I thought I'd better take a spoonful anyway, to please them. I was served again with a whole loaf of bread, eight bananas, an entire pineapple, five mangoes and a water pitcher of steaming tea. I did the best I could with the tea and fruit but Terei was disappointed that I couldn't eat it all.

We finished the meal at last, and the young men cleared the table and washed the dishes while 

Terei sat and smoked. A young man wandered in with an accordion. He played melodies and American jazz. I was treated to "Smiles," "Let Me Call 

You Sweetheart," "Over There" and "In the Shade of the Old Apple Tree," all learned at the drinking and dancing place in Papeete run by an American. 

Six other young men arrived in the course of the next hour and all sat around Terei in a semi-

circle while she told them about me and all my in-laws. Conversation was interrupted by a loud and continuous blowing of the bus horn, gathering passengers for the trip to Papeete, 70 miles away. 

With bananas, coconut, and poi stowed on the 100f, we got in the truck and sat-and sat-and sat. It was after 8 o'clock, the starting time, but the bus driver was in no hurry. He stood talking with a group of natives. Other men and women arrived, leisurely putting strings of fish, baskets, bundles, live pigs and chickens on the bus top, and under the seats. 

It was 8:30 and there were no signs of leaving.

More chickens arrived, more ducks. The seats were filling with native women and babies. A man took the seat behind us bringing a squealing little pig with him. The pig was in a sort of harness made of fiber, with a handle up over his back, so he could be picked up and carried like a traveling bag. His front feet were hobbled so he couldn't run away, yet loosely enough so he could stand up when the man wanted to rest from carrying him.

At 9 o'clock we started. It had been about three hours since supper, so Terei got out some bananas and urged me to eat. The driver blew the horn most of the time and made frequent stops to take on more passengers, pigs and poi. Although the people couldn't help but hear the horn a quartermile down the road, they never came out of their houses until he made a full stop and blew

POI? 

especially for them.

I could see them through open doors, get up leisurely, gather bundles, say long farewells to assembled friends and family, kiss the babies and children all around, give a last pat to the dogs, and stroll to the bus. With bundles and livestock settled on board, it was necessary to kiss all around once more before getting in. No one seemed to fret at the frequent delays. Time means nothing here.

At Taravao the bus drew up at the Chinese cafe and most of the people went in to drink coffee. This made a stop of about 15 minutes. 

Next door was a service station. When all the passengers were back in the bus it pulled forward 50 feet to the tank and stopped to take on gas and water. I wondered why all that hadn't been done while the passengers were out drinking their coffee, but no one else seemed to wonder about it. 

Another short run and we stopped by a Chinese store and everybody got out. Terei tried to tell me why, but I couldn't understand. We all sat on the ground or the store steps while the empty bus backed up the road and waited. 

At 11 o'clock we still were sitting on the steps, though the Chinaman had shut up shop and disappeared into the darkness. I was getting sleepy so Terei told me to go sleep in the bus. I napped until another bus came up behind us and 

Terei woke me. We all squeezed in. 

A really beautiful girl, a half caste, with incredible masses of black hair sat on the front

seat with her arm about the driver. Her crown

Tiare Tahiti smelled like orange blossoms in the rain. Her dress was very pretty, white and scarlet.

She was bare-footed, which is usual for the country, but the scarf which she had wound round her lovely brown throat was a cheap bath towel!

Later two native girls got on and squeezed in the front seat. They tried to persuade her to move over so they could have a turn at hugging the driver. But the half caste would not give him up, so when the bus stopped again, one of the girls ran around and climbed in on the other side of the driver, and wound her arms around his neck and leaned her head on his shoulder for the rest of the journey.

The driver, with superb indifference, kept both hands on the wheel and scarcely looked at either girl.

The truck was open to the night wind which got uncomfortably cold about midnight, so Terei brought a cotton blanket out from one of her bundles and we wrapped up in it together. We drew in to Papeete about 2 a. m. with much horn blowing, and found the streets lighted around the market, and far busier than I had seen them in daylight. Three busses from other districts arrived at about the same time, and the shouting, the horn blowing and the pig squealing made the night hideous with noise. For the next three hours busses were coming and going, pouring loads of "Marketi" folks into town with their fruits and livestock. The din near the market

POI? 

was equal to the traffic noise of any big city. 

All the Chinese shops near the market were open and doing the big business of the week. The cafes, open to the street, were serving hot coffee and French bread to the crowds for a franc. Words are too weak to give an idea of the confusion around us, or the deafening commotion with the arrival of each bus.

The half and quarter-castes in Papeete know how to dress, and often are stunning in appearance. I believe that right in Papeete where there is so much mixed blood you can see more pretty girls to the city block than you can anywhere in America.

This was the big night for the Tahitian street girls and they were parading the streets, barefoot and flower-crowned until daylight. Native girls and young men strolled up and down the middle of the streets, with arms about each other, laughing, yelling, and singing snatches of naughty songs. Terei frowned on them and told me "bad words, bad song." They had their bottles of wine with them and as they grew more and more wobbly, had greater difficulty in missing the pigs and push carts. 

Their flower crowns wilted and slipped down at rakish angles, and about daybreak they were having a hard time keeping each other out of the gutters. And then suddenly, with the coming of full daylight, they disappeared. 

When we had first arrived, Terei had arranged her poi and pahua ready for sale and then tried

to put me to bed on the cement floor, but I had told her I wanted to walk about to get warm. 

Walking out on the main street by the water front I counted seventy-two natives sleeping on the pavement in front of the main store of the town. From two months old babies up to old men and women! Not one had anything more than a mat under him and a cotton blanket over him. 

Some hadn't even a blanket and I was shivering in the cold night air. I walked around eight blocks as fast as I could go to try to warm up, but it was no use. I was chilled through. Coming back I saw a baby of about a year dressed only in a little shirt some four inches long, sitting by his sleeping mother, waving his bare arms about, apparently warm and happy.

Back at the market I drank hot coffee and took some to Terei, and now nothing would suit her but to put me to bed. She spread the cotton blanket on the cement, where it was partly dry, and had me lie down. The rest of the blanket she tucked over and around me. I didn't suppose I could sleep on such a hard cold bed, with natives crowding past me with their pigs, but I did, and dreamed I was sleeping on a glacier and a cake of ice was falling on my feet. I awoke to find a native man resting a string of fish on my feet while he talked, and there was Terei, on the floor without any covering, fast asleep. I put the blanket over her and walked again to try to thaw myself out. 

South Sea Islands may be balmy in the day-

POI? 

time, but I'm here to say they can get mighty cold at 4 a. m.

Just before daylight customers began to arrive. 

In two's and three's and dozens they came, the men far outnumbering the women. The well-to-do 

French arrived, stepping from shiny big cars, wearing fresh morning heis and white duck.

Yes, government officials were out at dawn, personally selecting vegetables and ducks! Their 

Chinese cooks and house boys, or their Tahitian maids, followed and carried the provisions back to the cars in baskets.

All the French hotel proprietors were there shopping. On foot and on bicycle came hundreds of the young men of Papeete, French, half-caste and native, shopping for Sunday feasting. Almost every man, young or old, wore a white flower stuck over his ear, or a crown of flowers.

By 8 o'clock most of the food was sold, and the attendants were sweeping and turning the hose on the floor. Busses were filling with the country folk returning to their homes. 

By nine o'clock the market was practically deserted, the Chinese shops closed their doors, the busses tooted their way out of town, the streets were almost empty. I made Terei a gift of three meters of cloth for a dress and a can of talcum powder which she seemed to prize highly. She waved me a fond farewell as her bus started back to Tautira and I sought my hotel to sleep long and blissfully.

SEEKING A HOMESITE. 

Moorea is breath-taking-but I won't live in Moorea.

They told me the motorboat for Moorea would leave at 8 o'clock in the morning, so there I was at 7:45. No one but the captain was about. He interrupted his nap long enough to put my box down in the cabin. I sat on the only bench for passengers and waited. At 8 o'clock I woke him to make sure I was on the right boat and to inquire what time we would start. "At 8 o'clock," he assured me, and settled back to his nap.

At 8:30 the engineer ambled on board and tinkered with the motor. We gathered three more passengers. Two native boys who worked on the boat came about 9 and blew the warning whistle.

During the next half hour several families came with babies and bundles and by 10 o'clock, when

SEEKING A HOMESITE 

87 the final whistle blew, the boat had a fair crowd. 

Ropes were cast off just two hours and 15 minutes late, but no one seemed concerned. 

A shout from across the plaza and the gangplank was put back again to let more people on. 

We drew away from the seawall with many farewells between passengers and the groups that had come to see them off.

It's hard for these islanders to say goodby. 

Parting even for a day is a weighty matter, and such "sweet sorrow" that they must prolong it. 

They waved and threw kisses as though our boat were going on to China instead of just 10 miles to Tahiti's little sister island Moorea.

Once outside the reef we struck rough water and the little boat rolled until many people were sick. The native women slept on the forward deck and the roof of the cabin, while their husbands looked after the babies.

Waves washed over the after deck and I had to put my feet up on the bench to keep out of the wash. Then, not having any way to brace myself, an extra big lurch slid me off of the bench down to the deck in the middle of a passing wave. So this was why the natives went forward, or on the roof! I've lots to learn.

Moorea loomed up, lovely as a painting. At last we drew into a sheltered bay, and I began my pilgrimage around Moorea's shoreline, looking for a nook where we might build our home. The mountain peaks on tiny Moorea are as breathtaking as anything I've seen in the high Sierras

or the Cascades. One great cliff drops sheer to a blue bay, and across its face I counted 28 waterfalls in half a mile. Moss and ferns were clinging to the rocks.

These islands are the photographer's paradise. 

The steamship folders and novels cannot exaggerate. The colors in the sea, the coral, the breakers on the reef, the tiny islands inside the reef, the palms, ferns, flowers and fantastic peaks are all as gorgeous as they say. There's been only one disappointment for me, the lack of white. sand beaches. Most of them are black here, and they are strewn with coral, dead and broken which makes walking a tricky business. 

Most of the Mooreans are full-blooded Polynesians. They are good looking in their brown style, strong and erect. Many of the women have superb figures that cannot be spoiled by their cheap, ill-fitting clothes. It's pretty bad when a two-hundred-fifty-pounder squeezes into a size 

38 dress that strikes her at the knees. Usually the dress is the only garment, unless you count a big flat straw hat.

Men wear pareaus more commonly here, and they look grand! Brown skins, and bulging muscles. But the cutest of all are the country boys and girls up to about three years who run about completely clad in old straw hats. 

The custom of offering a fresh drinking nut to passing strangers holds here. A young nut may have almost a quart of liquid in it, and each nut seems to have a slightly different flavor. I

SEEKING A HOMESITE 

89 know that doesn't seem possible, but it's true. 

Some taste almost like ginger ale, and some like the charged water they put in sodas at home. 

I believe if you added chocolate flavor to the water of a real tasty nut, and dropped a hunk of ice cream in, it would make a mighty good soda. 

I stayed four days at the home of one Tetua

Nui, who is a rich man for an islander. He and his wife both own much land, and make copra from their coconuts. He offered to lease us a piece of land at the edge of the village and supply us with all the native foods for $40 a year. We could have all we wanted of coffee, avacadoes, bananas, fei, papayas, pineapples, limes, mangoes, coconuts, tarua, breadfruit and "ufi," whatever that is. A bargain, I thought, as we would have to buy only our bread and sugar. 

Of course fish from the lagoon, and shellfish from the reef are there for the taking. 

Workmen who might help build us our house and bring bamboo from the mountains, get about 

40 cents a day as wages.

An islander here, who spoke beautiful English, told me we would have to build a close fence if we were planning a garden. It's a case of "no fence, no garden" at this place, for the land crabs are bad here. But Peter told me how to trap them. 

You can't drive them away, because they live in holes in the ground.

The trap is made by sinking a five-gallon gasoline tin down in the earth so the open top of the tin is level with the ground. Then a stick is laid

across the top, and a piece of copra is suspended on a string from the stick, so the bait dangles down in the middle of the can. Then when Mr. 

Crab tries to reach the copra he tumbles into the can and cannot climb out again. Every morning take the crabs out of the trap and kill them. In time there are no more crabs in the garden. 

Like all Tahitians, the folk here are loud eaters, dipping their food in liquid, then sucking it and fingers together. They let coconut ferment in sea water to make a tart sauce to dip their fish in. Poi and some of the fruits are dipped in coconut milk. Bread is always dunked in tea or coffee. It's too bad they make their food so soft -their teeth are very poor, hardly any native over 15 has a full set of teeth. They rot away and even the natives with money don't see any point in going to a dentist when they can still suck up their soft foods without teeth. They give the six months old babies bread dunked in strong coffee. The two year old has his bowl of coffee, sweetened terrifically with brown sugar, twice a day. He eats endless hunks of bread sopped in it, and very little else. 

I found all of Moorea's 35-mile coast charming, but the boat ride back to Tahiti was not so good. 

High winds and cross currents made the 10 miles a matter of nine hours. The boat was overloaded with pigs, calves and firewood. The decks were entirely covered with wood except in the spots where the livestock was tied. The bench on the rear deck was out of sight under wood, so I had

SEEKING A HOMESITE 

my choice of sitting on the pigs or the bundles of wood. Some of the natives settled down on the rows of bound pigs to sleep out the journey, but the porkers got seasick almost immediately, so I chose to perch on the wood. 

Did you ever go chopping through a rough sea, sitting on faggots for nine hours? I did, and I don't believe we'll build our house on Moorea!

IN TAHITI MEN ARE VERY GOOD MOTHERS. 

Babies are wanted and loved here. 

Week-end in Papeete! It's a quaint little town, a queer mixture of saloons, dance halls, hotels, restaurants, shops, convents, churches and monasteries.

The French government offices are here. Only one building in town four stories high, and that's a hotel run by an American.

Most of the shops and stores are Chinese, and you should smell them! They are one-story, wooden affairs, with no window displays as we know them in the States. Wares are piled in and piled up in apparent confusion. But ask the proprietor for any article and he puts his hand on it immediately. Everything is there, from lichee nuts to coal oil, from mosquito net to Victrola records.

IN TAHITI MEN ARE VERY GOOD MOTHERS 93 

There are no regular sidewalks. Some of the storekeepers and hotel owners have paved a strip two or three feet wide in front of their property, but if the neighboring owner does not choose to pave-there ends the walk. What walk there is it is always cluttered with parked bicycles, Chinese babies, sleeping dogs and native families using it for bedroom, kitchen and bath. Overnight the islanders put down a mat and call the sidewalk home, but if they stay over several days in town, the babies, bananas and bedding are parked there all day.

One great blessing is the scarcity of house flies down here. There is a kind of outdoor fly, but it never ventures indoors unless lured by the smell of fish frying or cabbage cooking. There's not a screened native house here. Not even the hotels and cafes are screened, yet I've scarcely seen a fly in town.

The hotel beds have mosquito nets around them, but the mosquitoes I've met so far are weak and small compared with those back home. 

Sunday I went to the horse races held about two miles outside Papeete. The entrants were in it just for sport, and the races were free. 

There was feverish betting going on, and high excitement with each event. There were a few thoroughbreds, but most of the events were run with the little island horses. They were fast, in spite of their short legs. The 'best' people were there with their Frenchiest clothes on. Half and quarter castes rivaled the French in dress, and

natives were swarming everywhere. 

Last night I had a great Tahitian experiencewent to the Papeete cinema. It's worth the five francs admission to hear the native interpreter and the music. The pictures are so old and queer they're really funny. They are mostly old silent films of John Bunny, Mabel Normand, Mary Pickford in her first westerns put out by Biograph and, of course, the Charlie Chaplin of fifteen or more years ago. Funny, flickering old films that break at each showing. The pictures are shown in a big old building under about an acre of corrugated iron roof. Can you imagine the roar when a heavy tropical rainstorm turns loose on that roof?

The main film I saw was of those "bound-torise-to-fame" kind. You know, where the poor, awkward country bumpkin makes a hit with the girl and her rich papa, rises to fame and fortune, becomes graceful and handsome overnight and marries the girl.

You don't realize the advance in motion picture photography until you see one of these old-timers.

The native audience was enthusiastic-clapping and cheering at the bravery of the hero, making little purring noises in their throats during the love scenes, and wailing, "Aue! Aue!" when the villain seemed to get the upper hand in the plot. 

Above all the racket would rise the wail of the interpreter, telling what the English captions meant. There is nothing else to call it but a wail. The interpreter must have been chosen,

IN TAHITI MEN ARE VERY GOOD MOTHERS 95 

not only for his knowledge of English, but because of his peculiar, piercing voice that could be heard over the din as he explained the situations in Tahitian.

The musicians sat up on a balcony, under the roof peak, four men and three women, who sang native 'himinies' accompanied by guitars, ukuleles and rattle drums.

The main feature of the evening was in six parts, but a short comic of the pie throwing kind opened the show, followed by parts one, two and three of the feature. Then a bathing beauty comic, and parts three and four of the main film. Then came a western that must have been part of an old broken reel, gleaned from somewhere, for it had no beginning nor end-just a disconnected series of cowboys chasing bandits, then bandits chasing cowboys, then a grand fight. During the fight the film broke for the third time, and that was that!

Then came part five of the feature. I thought with only one more part to go they surely would finish it up this time, but no, they started another feature instead. I know it was a feature because there was a cast of characters and a statement that this was a film of four parts, and of outstanding quality.

The first part of that film was shown with only one break, but they had bad luck with part two.

When it broke the fourth time, they gave up and never tried to show the rest of it—and they stopped just where the heroine, married to one

man, was holding before her the pictures of three other men, trying to decide which one she should run away with. 

At last they got around to show part six of the main feature, and I left at the end of it, for it was 11:30, but another film was startingmermaids this time. I wonder if I had stayed another hour if I would have seen more of the lady making a choice of a lover!

I've been out in the districts again, this time on the other side of Tahiti, to see the places I hadn't seen before.

I stayed a day and a night in the home of a man whose great grandfather was one of the first English missionaries here. He married native, and all the children and grandchildren since have married native, but the men of the family have kept their English clear and excellent through four generations.

This one, John, has never been to school, but his language is that of a refined Englishman, and his vocabulary is amazing. He has inherited a pair of gray eyes which look odd with his deep chocolate skin.

He has no children of his own, but has built a large home for his wife, two sisters, nephew and niece, five of their children and three other children of assorted blood.Two are half Chinese and the other Marquesan. All seemed to be loved and cared for equally. That made eight children in the family between the ages of 2 and 7.

IN TAHITI MEN ARE VERY GOOD MOTHERS 97 

And that reminds me: Children down here don't have store toys, except marbles. They make their own playthings, miniature trucks and cars from bits of wood and big round seeds. Stilts, kites, fish spears and little canoes. And they're the best natured children I've ever seen. Native babies are so placid they can't seem to think up anything to cry about. I wonder if it's because these islanders all take life so easy-working so little especially the women.

The men do all the copra work and the house building. Men do most of the fishing, the food gathering and about half the cooking and serving. 

All that's left for the women is the sweeping of the sleeping house, the family washing of their scanty clothes and the baby-raising. 

The country woman here does perhaps onetenth the work done by a country woman in the States. And the men take over the baby-raising when they are not occupied with anything else! 

There are no bottles to wash and sterilize, no feeding formulas to mix, no cod-liver oil or fruit juices to give at stated hours, no daily oil rubs, no cribs to air and clean, no daily bath in warm water-they are dipped in the streams of cold water from earliest infancy. Usually there is no special baby clothing of any sort to be washed daily no diapers! A country baby goes naked in the warmth of the day and in the evening wears a little shirt that comes to his middle, with a flour sack or bath towel tied around his head.

During the day the 'baby tending' means holding

him if he isn't sleeping on the floor, or carrying him around if the parents go somewhere. At night baby is put on a mat in an old box beside mother's bed so he won't roll away on the floor. 

On the schooners mother sleeps while father holds baby. When the family spends the night in 

Papeete on the sidewalk, father gets up at odd hours and holds baby in a convenient position over the gutter.

I saw a country family going to the village on foot. The man carried 100 pounds of bananas on a pole balanced on one shoulder, the six-monthold baby was on the other arm. A toddler of two clung to his pareau, and three more offspring tagged behind. The mother followed chatting with a friend, unconcerned about how her husband might be getting along with his load and five children.

In the States we used to say, "Way out west in the great open spaces where men are men," but here it's "In the South Seas where men are very good mothers."

Friends commonly exchange babies and raise each other's. Many families have just as many exchanged, adopted and picked up children as true sons and daughters.

Babies are wanted and loved here, regardless of parentage or blood. So John's family was not unusual in having three "extra" children. 

Eight other relatives and several friends were visiting him for the week, so all meals had to be eaten in two shifts-children first.

IN TAHITI MEN ARE VERY GOOD MOTHERS 99 

With the copra workers, who came in to eat at the adult table, there were twenty-nine of us for each meal. Yet no one hurried or worried about preparing food for so many. All the food was native, except the bread, butter and sugar. The meat was roast goat. John had turned a number of them loose in the mountains years before, where they had increased to about 200. When he wanted meat, some of the men went hunting and it cost them only the ammunition. The fruits, yams, taro, and coffee grew on his own land, and the fish came from the lagoon almost at his doorstep.

STILL SEEKING. 

Too many Uncles. I meet a Bolsheviki 

Angel. Everything finniel Eight posts, half a roof, and then it rained. 

There is a lovely point of land near John's home that would be ideal for our homesite. On the left we would have our own little bay with palms and three thatched houses on the far shore. Off to the right at a greater distance we would see the sharp mountain peaks of Tahiti Nui. In front of us would stretch the whole Pacific, with sunsets included. The native who owns it speaks a little English and calls himself Charlie. He promises to supply us with fruit for a year, let us use the entire point for our garden and build us a three-room house, native style for 600 francs. 

That's about $33.00.

There is a native hut there now, but Charlie declares it will be a simple matter to get a dozen men who will pick it up and carry it away down the beach a bit, out of our way. Charlie's uncle lives there, but he assures me he will be glad to move away for 10 francs. So I've found the place for us at last!

I wired Harry and the children to come on, so now to get the house built. 

There's nothing like trying to get a house

STILL SEEKING 

101 built by natives to make you realize you're in 

Tahiti. Charlie, who contracted to build our house, assured me it would be finished in ten days. "Everything finnie," he declared many times. That was the place on the point of land with the million-dollar view.

At first Charlie claimed the land was his alone, so there would be no trouble in renting it, but soon it leaked out that there were seven uncles and aunts who owned shares in the land. 

I despaired at the thought of interviewing all these people and arranging terms of rent to suit them all, but Charlie said just give him three days and he would get the written consent of the relatives. He said I should pay him 600 francs and he would settle with his family. That seemed to be the only way out, though I hated to delay the house building three days with the folks starting from the States so soon.

Three days passed, five days passed, and still Charlie couldn't get them all to sign his paper, so at last he wrote a paper for me guaranteeing me the right to the land for a year, and in my ignorance I thought that would settle the matter. I didn't know that to Charlie it was only a "scrap of paper," given so he could demand a hundred francs. But at this outrage my Yankee blood rose up and I cut his demand to 25 francs, for he hadn't started building yet. 

Next day Charlie began cutting palm fronds to weave for the roof and walls of the house. The second day I went to see how things were mov-

ing along, and he had brought some trunks of purao and palm logs for posts. But still he hadn't moved the uncle's house away, which was the first thing he was supposed to do.

When I urged him to move it at once, he promised to get that done first thing in the morning. 

But the next morning he disappeared and didn't reappear until the following noon. When I finally caught him, he was on his way home for dinner and his siesta. 

At 3:00 o'clock I trailed Charlie to his house, woke him up, and told him I must see the old man's house moved then and there. It was at this time he broke the painful news to me that the uncle had changed his mind and would not have his house moved unless I paid him 50 francs a month for the privilege of living on the point. So that ended the point affair.

Then I had to start out all over again on my hunt for a homesite. 

I was discouraged, but Charlie was not. He tried to induce me to have my house built a little way down the side of the bay, but that would mean the old uncle would pass through our yard to get to his home on the point, and that would never do, because he had elephantiasis in the last and most contagious stage. So that was out. Then

Charlie offered another place where his sister lived. But it was back from the ocean, beside a big swamp, so that wouldn't do. I was at my wit's end. 

I was saved-by a Bolshevist! As he came

STILL SEEKING 

103 down the road I thought it peculiar a native should wear patent leather pumps and carry an umbrella. But with a loin cloth his only garment, and skin tanned to a deep brown, I didn't realize he was white until I was close enough to see his 

Van Dyke beard and blue eyes. He hailed me in 

English, "What are you doing in this wilderness?" "Hunting for a place to build a house," I answered, “a place that hasn't seven aunts and uncles on it." "Well, why not here, then?" he said, pointing to a clearing beside the road. "This is my land," and he waved grandly to the beach, the clearing among banana plants, and the adjoining plantation. "I am a Russian Bolshevist. I believe in

Communism, and share my land with anyone who wants it." 

I gasped. "Well speak up," he went on, "do you like the place? Just a step to the beach-no aunts or uncles on it-running water piped here and if you want a cow, there's plenty of free pasture. 

Use all the coconuts and bananas you want, and it won't cost you a cent."

It was a good sales talk, and he wasn't sellingjust giving. 

Did anyone in America ever walk up to you and offer you a free building site, with some food thrown in, when you were all prepared to rent or lease? We had stopped under the shade of a giant mango tree. He squinted up at its ripening fruit. "And all the mangoes you can use and more, right in your front yard." That settled the

matter. I had always wanted a mango tree in my front yard, and here it was.

I told him of my family, to arrive soon. "Call me Uncle," he advised, "and there will be only one uncle on this land. Now come on home for dinner and meet my wife. She's American and will be glad to have neighbors from her own country. She'll be like a mother to you. You'll stay with us until your house is built." 

His wife is a lovely lady who mothered me right away. She had me call her "Mother Angel."

Charlie still seemed eager to build my house in the new place, and spent a day bringing his "niao" and posts down. Then a day of grass pulling to clear the spot where the house was to stand. 

That's the Tahiti system-just pull up grass. By this time the week and a half had passed and instead of "everything finnie," there was a grasspulled spot on the ground. Another day he set up eight posts in the ground, and then invited me over to see his progress. 

Eight posts, standing among banana plants, under the coco palms! Charlie was so enthusiastic over the posts he demanded 100 francs for his labor. After that was paid, he appeared on the job about two hours every day, and when questioned, protested that the rest of the time he was up in the mountains cutting bamboo for the floors. 

But that turned out to be only an alibi for his laziness, for when the bamboo finally reached the building site, it was a small bundle which could have been gathered in one afternoon.

STILL SEEKING 

105 And so it went, until the time when I knew Harry and the children were actually on the steamer, drawing nearer every day, and no house ready to receive them. 

One night Charlie asked for another 50 francs which was not due until the house was completed. 

I refused and he went home, mad clear through. 

I sent him word that I was through with him and would get other men on the job. At that he dashed back and promised so faithfully to work fast and well that I gave him one more chance. 

But it was all talk. He went home to his siesta that day and never came back.

The next day I scouted for other workers and got the new men started just two days before the boat arrived on Saturday.

The family got here safely-joyous reunionand all came out to Vairao on Sunday, but the house was still only eight posts and half a roof. 

And that day it started to rain. It rained and poured torrents by turns for the next two weeks, scarcely stopping, day or night. We would have been in a frightful mud hole if uncle and Mother Angel hadn't taken us in, while the two natives worked intermittently on the house. As soon as the roof was on, the floor laid and the sides partly finished, we moved in.

A few palm branches made a half-way shelter over an outdoor fire, but we cooked for a week with rain dripping down our backs and the children couldn't step outdoors without raincoat and caps.

Bit by bit, the rest of the house took shape, with Harry helping the natives on carpentry and in time living became more tolerable. The rains stopped about the time the house was completed. 

We're quite comfortable now, with large double bedroom, big living room and a roofed patio between. The kitchen is a lean-to behind the living room and the cost, complete, was $27.50, plus all my agony with Charlie.

I warn you, when you come to the south seas, order your house two months ahead of time, and give the natives five times as long to do any piece of work as they think they will need.

This is our 'niao' home that cost $27.50. The roof, niao also with the rough ends left exposed, helps to shed the tropical torrents. Notice the big posts supporting the middle shelter, just behind us. They've "gone native." They are sprouting leaves and growing lustily. The roots they put down will make the house secure in wind storms. The big leaves, left, by our bedroom window, are banana. One of the trees in the back is an avocado growing right through the roof of our kitchen to a height of fifty feet.

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE. 

Our table is a '49'er. Harry Robinson Crusoe 

Parsons. Fei-not upon-but for you lovers. 

Potatoes with tender rhinocerous skins and coconut milk dumplings.

During the three weeks I stayed with the "Angels" before the family arrived, I made beds and box furniture in feverish haste. Yes, I dropped boards on my toes and pounded my fingers.

When you come to visit us you will find the beds rather wobbly and the cupboards staggering on legs of assorted lengths, and then you'll recognize the "feminine touch."

The beds are like nothing you ever saw before, built in two sets, with two sleeping places in each set. One set is for the girls and the other is for the mamma and the daddy. The mattresses are separate and so is the bedding; but one mosquito net canopy spreads over each bed set. Something like twin beds hitched together, with old-fashioned four-poster effect rising from the outside corners holding the net over and around the set. 

The lumber for the beds cost about $3 in Papeete, and the mosquito net cost a little more.

But I didn't want to "go native" in my sleep, which would mean sleeping with my head under the blanket on the bad mosquito nights. The mosquito bites don't hurt much, and the mosquito does not carry malaria, but I hate to hear them sing around my ears. The little pests come and go, suddenly descending on us in swarms when the wind blows them out of their swampy homes, then they disappear for days, so you'd think they didn't exist.

The food cupboard, dish cupboard and dresser were simple carpentry after my struggles with the beds. Ah Sook, the storekeeper in Papeete, provided the boxes for these, so my cupboards bear such signs as "S. and W. Tomato Sauce," "Anchor Butter, New Zealand," "I. X. L. Jam, Made in Tasmania" and "Gilbey's Gin, O. E."

We didn't bring any furniture from the States, just two big packing boxes which held four narrow mattresses, old clothing, some of our camp pots and dishes, bedding and four pillows, a large mirror and some half-used bottles of medicine and boxes of salve which we had on hand. The mattresses went two and two in each box, standing on edge, around the walls of the boxes and the hole left in the middle was stuffed with the bedding and miscellaneous articles. 

Our third big box was filled with the children's school books and paper, a choice few of my books and my typewriter.

One of these packing boxes became a fruit cupboard, after we got our house built and every-

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

111 thing unpacked. At the moment it holds about 

200 pounds of bananas, in different stages of ripeness; a bunch of fei, 20 avocados, 45 papayas, and uncounted mangoes. Back in the States I would have said a fruit cupboard that size was absurd, that no family could use fruit in these quantities, but in this warm climate we eat the cool, fresh fruit three times a day with pleasure. 

Another packing box became a clothing cupboard with a mouse-proof door to store extra clothes and bedding in. The winter coats in which we left San Francisco, the black suede shoes I wore appropriately at home, Harry's wool shirt, the warm comforters we snuggled under in the States, the fuzzy bathrobes of other winters-all relegated to the cupboard for the duration of our stay here. 

Most of our cupboards are built with legs so they can stand in coconut shells filled with water for protection against ants. These Tahitian ants are not satisfied with eating food, but go gaily about consuming the cupboards, books, wooden bowls, tables, chairs and floors.

The third big packing box made a fine bookcase, and its lid became shelves. 

Harry and I finally learned to make quite a comfortable chair from a box turned on its side, with slanting sticks nailed to each end, with burlap tacked across from stick to stick for a back. 

Then a pad of personally picked kapok from the tree down the road completes the seat. 

The only piece of factory-made furniture in our

whole layout is a round dining table which Mother Angel lent us. It is old and battered and anteaten, but still sturdy, having been built in the days when tables were expected to remain tables for 100 years.

Built in New England, the table rounded the 

Horn in a sailing schooner with Mrs. Angel's parents in the days of the '49'ers. It served the family in California, where Mother Angel was born in a mining camp, then moved again with the family when the rush for gold was on in the Klondike. After moving from camp to camp by flatboat in summer and dog sled in winter, the table was allowed to settle down in Sitka with the family.

There, Mother Angel, as a young woman, was the first American postmistress when the United 

States bought Alaska from Russia. And there she met "uncle," then a young mining engineer from

Russia. He had been educated for the priesthood, but later left the church. 

Through the long winter evenings in Sitka 

Mother Angel taught English to the young engineer, while he taught her Russian. And so the table got tangled into their romance as they sat by it, night after night, studying. After their marriage, the old table went with them from place to place in Alaska, where uncle was engaged in opening different mines.

Twenty-five years ago the table returned to California in the hold of a steamship, a new experience, for it had always traveled on sailing

Harry, Peggy, Polly and... beautiful Chinese bowls.

The table is the one which came 'round the horn in '49. That dark object on the right is two large joints of bamboo, fitted together to hold sugar. Yes, that's a wine bottle but it holds nothing more intoxicating than honey, and aren't those bowls lovely?

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

115 vessels before. There was a short stay in San 

Francisco, a brief residence in Riverside, then came the long, long journey to Tahiti. After 18 months' stay in the district for Faaa, it was loaded on the little shore schooner that served this end of the island before "le truck" was mistress here, and was put in the Angel's new home in this district, the first white man's home in Vairao.

Still for many years it was the dining table, but time had darkened its wood, moving and hard usage had scarred its top, and ants had eaten into its legs, so at last it was put aside in favor of a newer one. For another 10 years the table served as a catch-all under the house, a place for odd boxes, lined with leaves for hens' nests. And that was my first view of it-covered with old boxes where hens came and went all day as they felt the urge to lay.

When I heard its story I was so delighted with it that uncle immediately offered it for our house. 

So we transferred the nests and setting hens to a big box, killed the lice with kerosene, washed an ant colony out of one leg with boiling water, scraped the 10-year accumulation of dirt from its top with knives, and gave it a sea bath. Then we soaped it, scrubbed it, scalded and rinsed it. 

I can't say we restored the table's youth or beauty, but we got it clean. We painted it a soft, moss green, and when our living room was finished, it had its most recent ride along the road on a wheelbarrow, with Harry at the helm.

So the old table again rose to the dignity of a dining and study table. Our daily life goes on around it. My typewriter is on it now; the children study here every day; the family gathers around it three times daily for food, and in the evenings after supper we often play cards, dominoes or checkers on it.

Well, that's all our furniture, unless you count the broom and the kerosene lamp donated to us by two departing American men. Our home is simple enough, and plain, but somehow it looks. livable and cozy, with our books in order, our big mirror and several pictures of friends and family smiling from the walls. 

When I headed for the South Seas I intended to cook native style. But when I found that native style meant doing without vegetables, I changed my mind and sent a hurried letter to Harry, telling him to pick up more pots and pans to bring with him. A 10-cent pan costs 65 cents down here. 

Harry arrived in due time, with cooking pots enough to supplement the few things we already had packed, and then came the problem of a stove. 

But Harry made one-a wonderful contraption of rocks and cement, wires and old pieces of iron picked up around uncle's house. The stove pipe is hand-made from old pieces of tin roofing. It's all held together with native cement, then coated over thinly with American cement. The Tahitian cement is made of coral rock, pounded fine, mixed with water, rock salt and brown sugar. Yes,

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

117 believe it or not, brown sugar! I wonder if Ripley knows about that one!

The stove stands about as high as a modern electric range, but there the similarity ends. It's built in three sections, vertically. The bottom opening is for a fire under the oven, then the baking oven, and above is the fire box which heats the top of the stove where I do most of my cooking. I light the bottom fire only when I want to bake, which isn't very often. Cookery isn't my forte.

Our fuel is mostly coconut husks. They burn well and are a handy size to put in the fire box without any chopping. Every time uncle makes copra there are several wagon loads of split husks to be had for the carrying home. Harry brings bamboo from the mountains for kindling. 

My baking pans are five-gallon tins cut open lengthwise. I wasn't smart enough to think of that by myself, but I had seen the tins used by the natives in a hundred different ways. They come originally from America or Australia with oil or kerosene in them. When empty they sell for two francs. Then the natives cut the tops out, put on handles and they are the only pails you ever see natives use. Sometimes the tins are cut in half cross wise in squares to make cooking pots, serving dishes or basins. Cut down lengthwise they make larger containers for feeding chickens or mixing poi. I use them for dish pans, mixing bowls, as a coffee roaster. They're not pretty, but are quite usable.

I'd never get a washing done without these tins. I use a "soak, sozzle and squeeze" system. I soap the clothes and soak them overnight in my trusty five-gallon tin. Then I rub and sozzle in another tin cut lengthwise, until I get tired of the job then I pass them along to the children who sozzle some more and have a good time playing under the spigot with them. When they have whirled them around to throw water on each other, and thrown them back and forth a few times, and let the water splash over and around them for a while, they squeeze the clothes out and hang them on a wire.

This method gets clothes cleaner, but never clean, and is not approved by the Good Housekeeping Institute. The palm log where I put my tins for the washing is smoothed off to a flat surface on top and is known to the family as my washing machine. 

You'd laugh to see the tools Harry works with.

We brought an ax with us, and bought a digging fork in Papeete for three prices, but the rest of the tools are odds and ends culled from the 25-year collection of junk under the Angel's house. They were rusty, broken and without handles. But 

Harry ground them and sharpened them, mended and fitted them with stout handles of guava wood that he cut himself. That way he collected a trowel, hoe, rake, spade, mattock, weed cutter and pick. He even found an old buck saw with half its teeth rusted away, and filed it down by hand until he made an entire new set of teeth

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

119 below the line of the old teeth. And it saws! He's been a real Robinson Crusoe down here. 

We arranged with the Angels for Harry to pick fruit for both families from their mountain plantation and the valleys beyond. This gives us quantities of free avocados, breadfruit, bananas, fei, papayas, guavas, and mangoes, and saves the 

Angels from hiring natives to go after fruit, for they are not mountain climbing people.

Harry gets a great kick out of taking the wheelbarrow as far up the trail as he can, then gathering 25 papayas, as many avocados and a bunch of bananas weighing from 100 to 200 pounds.

Then I hear the squeak of the wheelbarrow coming down through the plantation and Harry yelling to me from a great distance. He loves the life. 

We use six papayas a day, two or three avocados, a dozen fei and goodness knows how many bananas. The milk from one or two coconuts a day makes any kind of food rich. Our pineapples we buy from the market. We plant all the tops and so perhaps we will have a crop of our own next year. Oranges grow wild, far, far up in the mountains.

Our coffee we buy from the native policeman at about 12 cents a pound. We roast it ourselves and grind it as we need it. In the States I never cared whether I had coffee or not, but here the coconut milk makes it taste so different and so delicious that I crave it.

One of Harry's first jobs was to clear the land near our house for his garden. If you have never

seen jungle land, it takes a keen imagination to picture the amount of green stuff that can grow on one acre of land. The tangle of tree, vine, bush and head-high grass is terrific in a climate that averages 85 degrees daylight temperature, and gets plenty of rain. With mattock, pick, fork and hoe he cleared the mess away, bit by bit, and planted a garden. 

We have Tahitian beans, lima beans, sweet potatoes, carrots, lettuce, radishes, beets and cucumbers coming on. We already have eaten 

Chinese cabbage, cucumbers and radishes from the garden, and in a week or two our green beans will be ready. Weeds grow fast in such a luxuriant climate as this, but the garden stuff grows rapidly, too.

Uncle gave us enough chicken wire to fence our garden, but we are fortunate here in having no land crabs. That's because this section is a little bit high. The low districts are riddled with crab holes and tunnels. We have only one crab on the place that I know of, and he is a gentlemanly fellow who lives under the big lava block that is our doorstep. He seems to have no family -strange for a Tahitian-and he never ventures near the garden. The fence is to keep out pigs and chickens. 

Another one of Harry's jobs was to break a cow to milk for a native who lives a mile down the road. The cow had her sixth calf and had never been milked. That's a common state of affairs here, for the natives don't know the value of milk

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

121 as a food and are mortally afraid of the cows. 

This bossy was a wild one but Harry tied her head to a palm tree, hobbled her hind legs so she couldn't kick, and milked her while she bellowed her anger at having her calf taken away.

But Harry has a way with cows and inside a week she was standing quietly for him, without hobbles. The calf gets half the milk and we get the other half. That settles our milk problem until we can find a suitable cow to buy. 

Just now Harry is building a chicken house for we want to start a little flock. We all love fryers. 

And he's talking about making a trail clear up to the mountain top, fit to take a horse over, for he wants to experiment with crops up the mountain. 

There are thousands of acres of land up there, untouched, that he may use freely. Of course

Harry is supposed to be on leave of absence from his work in the States, and we speak of this as his "vacation," but he finds so much to do here that he goes about on a high run all the time. He's so enthusiastic that he can't be contented unless he's working long hard hours every day, so I guess that's one way to take a year off!

Our family has increased: Two kittens, a rooster, a milkgoat and a borrowed horse. The kittens were gifts to the children from our American neighbor about a mile away. The kitten's grandfather is the famous Persian Tom living in Papeete, owned by Nordoff, the writer, co-author of "Mutiny on the Bounty." Seems as though such connections

should give us entree into some of the best circles of Tahitian society. But I suppose the whole setup is somewhat spoiled by the fact that the kitten's grandmother was only a Papeete street cat.

Our goat is angora, direct descendant of those first goats brought here by the Spanish some 200 years ago. I guess the stock has run down because the best she can do is to give the girls each a glass of milk night and morning. But she cost only $2.66 so we don't regret our bargain. She does a good job cleaning up our banana skins, papaya rinds and mango peels. A coconut a day is her main food besides grass. She is a tiny thing, and lives under a shelter of palm branches. 

The rooster is of ancient and honorable Chinese stock. When we bought him we expected to get about 10 hens the same day and start a little chicken yard, but the man failed to bring the hens, so the rooster leads a solitary life tied to a banana plant in our yard, singing Tahitian love songs to the attractive pullets next door.

The horse was lent to us by a native man, Teata, who hasn't enough feed for her on his place. We have plenty of grass which we want cropped off so we keep the mare here, use her all we wish, and Teata borrows her back occasionally when he needs her for some work. We are trying to get her fattened up so her back bone won't be so prominent. We've wished a hundred times we had brought along the old army saddle or Harry's western.

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

123 She did a pretty good job of clearing off the long grass in our front yard. The trouble there was that the niao fence was a constant temptation to her. Made of palm fronds, dry and brown, it was something like hay to her a change from green feed. It kept the children busy driving her away to save the fence.

She's a real Tahitian about fruit gathering. The tree in our yard drops about 100 golden mangos a day now. She hears the thud of one falling and instantly hunts for it in the grass. Two bites, and she has stripped off the meat, then spits out the stone. She eats all she can reach, and the native kids who come every day to gather the surplus have to be quick on the grab to get them before she does.

Harry has used the mare a few times to go in the mountains food gathering. He cut a trail to a "bench" nearly a half mile up. Taihai, a native who has adopted us, insisted on helping with the trail, and they put in three weeks of hard work together. When Taihai found out what Harry was doing up the hillside, he simply picked up the shovel and went to work. 

Harry thought Taihai wanted a job, and explained to him that he couldn't pay for help-he was only making the trail for his own benefit and pleasure, and he was in no rush to finish it. But all that explanation slid right off Taihai-he was working because he liked and admired Harry. 

With his little bit of English, he protested, “You good man, I like work you. You, me make

broom," (road) "no pay me, all right.”

So faithful Taihai came to work every morning until the road was finished. When Harry began planting beans, cucumbers and pumpkins up on the first bench, Taihai worked right beside him. 

He explained all over again that this wasn't work he was paying for, just fun, an experiment. Still 

Taihai insisted on helping. "Maybe some day you catch'um money. You pay me. You no catch'um money, you no pay me. All same me I like you."

The Angels call him our Friday, he is so devoted to us all. He goes with Harry twice a week fruit gathering. He has shown him where the wild 

Oranges and limes grow. One day he took him to a valley where pineapples were to be had for the picking. He showed us new places to find fei and told us the legend that declares you will become enchanted with the island so you'll never be able to leave, if you eat fei. That's why Tahiti belles give fei to their European lovers in the best of faith.

Last week Taihai showed Harry where to find wild tarua, the root with a skin that looks as tough as rhinocerous hide, and tastes like potato. 

The skin is rough and wrinkled and brown, but the surprise comes when you start to peel it and find that the skin scrapes off as easily as the tender skin of a new potato. They can be boiled, mashed or fried, and they make fine potato chips. 

Harry's trail is the first one ever made in this district that a horse could travel on. All his life 

Taihai has struggled up the mountain side on

PACKING-BOX FURNITURE 

125 foot, through dense undergrowth, and come slipping and sliding down again with his fei and bananas on a carrying pole on his bare shoulders. 

How his eyes shine with pride when he comes down the trail leading the mare with the 200-pound load on HER back. He is proud because Harry is not afraid of wild cows, because he can rope a horse, and because Harry can go up the mountain faster than he can himself.

Like so many of the natives, Taihai is shortwinded.

It was Taihai who taught me how to make poi. 

There are many different kinds of poi, according to the flavor wanted, banana, orange, pineapple, coconut, papaya or tarua. Coconut is used in every kind of poi, it's the other fruit added which gives it the flavor and name. Plain poi is used only if there are no other fruits available. That would never be the case on Tahiti, but on some of the low atolls where only coconuts will grow, it is the old standby. The fruit is mashed to a pulp, a double handful of fresh grated coconut added, then a big spoonful of pia-native cornstarch-and then enough coconut milk to moisten the whole to a paste.

Since I haven't a native rock oven, I baked it in my oven to a crisp brown. It all disappeared at the first meal.

He taught us to use coconut milk in different ways, too. Now we have it in gravies, soups, over fruits, in mashed tarua. Today I used half grated nut and half flour to make dumplings and I make

my chocolate cookies half and half, too. In cakes the coconut milk takes the place of butter and milk. Soon we'll be like all the animals down here, unable to get along without our coconuts. The little mare likes her coconuts. So does the goat. Our rooster lives on coconut and our kittens come running when they hear me shredding a nut.

We're all going native together.

Teata's home and some of his family. The man standing in the background is the policeman who came along to add 'local color'. Back of the girl holding the child is a 'Hape', a fish cage made of bamboo. Floated in the lagoon it keeps the fish alive. The white spot above the canoe is the lagoon.

FOOD IS FREE, 

-if you have what it takes to go after it. 

Seven zigs and six zags.

This week I made my first trip to the mountains with Harry. It was a grand trip-the kind that makes you discover all sorts of new and painful spots on yourself the day after. We took Teata's mare and another one which had been lent to us. My mount was named Pi-Pi which means bean, but she could better have been named barrel. A matting sack filled with kapok was my saddle, and a single rope around Pi-Pi's nose was my bridle. Harry's outfit was about the same except that his mare wore the backband of an old harness to which fruit could be fastened.

The first stage of the journey was up Harry's trail to the first bench. That means a rise of 2000 feet by 13 zigzags. We climbed this on foot and led the horses. They are small for such work. They weigh only 500 pounds and Harry can stand on

the ground and swing his leg up over the back of one without much effort.

The second stretch was up a long steady incline of three miles that leads to the sharp pitch of the second mountain ridge. We rode this part, following a narrow path worn by the bare feet of natives going after fei. At first the trail went through tall brake and guava bushes so stiff we had to wrap our legs around our horses' necks, or tuck them up behind us to keep our trousers from being torn off. 

After a bit, we came out to a region of shorter growth. The plants looked familiar, but I couldn't think what they were at first, then realized we were riding through acres and acres of wild cow peas. Here were peas by the mile, and not an animal to eat them. What would the dairymen of 

Imperial Valley give for all this? As we climbed to a greater elevation, the plants were taller and taller, until they were swishing in our faces as we rode. There were carloads of peas there!

At last the peas gave way to a scattered growth of pandanus. These "trees" grow with a lot of roots above the ground like the poles of an Indian wigwam, and they don't even try to have a trunk until the roots meet about four feet above the ground. Then the little whiskbroom tops are fantastic against the sky. We went through a jungle of these things before we came to the steep rise of the second mountain. Here we tied the mares in the lush grass and continued on foot for it was much too steep for any horse to navigate.

FOOD IS FREE 

131 Soon we had to take off our shoes and sandals to stand up at all for the trail was slick with mud and rotting leaves. 

Wild bananas grow here by the thousands of plants, covering the steep canyon walls where they are not overshadowed by taller trees, Tahitian chestnuts, limes, oranges and others. Wild pineapples were among the grass, here and there, and taro growing thickly. All sorts of other plants were there, but I don't know their names.

Canyon walls up there are such jungle-y jungles you couldn't fall very far if you went over the edge of a cliff because you would get caught in a tangled mass of plant life that would support your weight.

After a mile of this we came to an orange tree, 60 feet high, and Harry climbed up it-barefooted! The first limbs branched out 20 feet up.

When he was there, I handed up a bamboo pole he had cut on the way. He tied a small stick to the end of his pole at an angle so he could catch the orange stems in the crook. 

We got three dozen fine oranges, large and sweet. There were about five dozen more green ones on it. This is not the season of best bearing now. Whether we get the other ones later is just a matter of whether we get there first or not when they are ripe enough to pick. 

For an hour clouds had been gathering, blacker and thicker; lightning had been flickering around the peaks nearest us, and thunder was rumbling. 

When the rain started in earnest, Harry scram-

bled down in a hurry before that tree trunk got slippery.

We stood under banana leaves while the rain pelted down so hard the drops stung when they hit our bare arms. But we soon were so drenched there was no use trying to stay under the bananas, so we started out on the trail again, down to a stream we could hear racing below us, 1000 feet down. Here the trail had been cut down the cliff in stairsteps, long before. They were spaced so far apart I had to sit down on each one in the mud, to reach my feet to the one below. 

It took us forty minutes to work our way down to the stream. The rain let up but we were soaked and muddy from head to foot, and sweating from the labor of getting down the canyon. We went in the stream, clothes and all, and waded up it, just exploring. We found another orange tree, and garnered five dozen more. Looking up the canyon walls we could see, not just thousands of fei, but countless thousands. 

I had the novel experience of cutting down a fei tree with Harry's slasher knife, chopping off the bunch of fruit at the top, and packing it back to the point where the mares were tied. Uncle declares it's the first time a white woman ever climbed that mountain or brought fruit out of that valley. I don't know about that, but I do know that I chose the smallest bunch I could find. 

Just at this time the rain roared down in sheets again, and the horses were feeling frisky with their rest and feed and the cold breeze that swept

FOOD IS FREE 

133 up from the sea with the storm. Harry wanted to walk to keep warm, so we packed all the load on his mare, tying everything on to the backstrap she wore. I was determined to ride but I had my troubles. A matting sack is a slick thing to sit on when dry, but when wet it is like a greased pole. It was tied around Pi-Pi's fat stomach with a small rope, and as I rode her down the long incline of three miles, it constantly slipped up on her neck, leaving me to hang to her slippery back the best way I could, without stirrups or girt.

Pi-Pi was on her way home after a long day, and she knew it mighty well. She craved to gallop. I held her in as well as I could with the one bit of rope around her nose, but it lacked authority. I longed for my good curb bit, but that was 6000 miles away. The trail was wet and slippery but she danced and cavorted the whole way. I couldn't stop her to get off, and didn't want to fall off and let her get away from me.

We were far ahead of Harry; so there was nothing to do but hang on with my legs, pull as hard as I could with one hand, and try to hold myself from pitching over her neck with the other. 

Well, I guess it all comes under the head of pleasure, because I want to go again, sometime.

. 

We love this Tahiti. On the reef. 

During Christmas-New Year's week we had a sudden spurt of traffic out our way-as many as four cars a day passed! They were loaded with flower-crowned boys and their sweethearts, out from Papeete on holiday rides. Always with guitars twanging and a dozen sweet voices singing, always in two or three-part harmonies. These folks never have had any formal training in music. 

They just sing easily and naturally as birds do. 

These holiday cars are decked with flowers and streamers of red leaves. They cruise along slowly, with music covering the hum of the motor. 

In the afternoon quiet we hear them coming in the distance, with music swelling louder as they pass, then fading away like a dream, into nothing, as they curve on around the point toward Teahupoo. 

We made a Christmas tree for the children by tying branches of feathery leaves together. There aren't any trees here that even faintly resemble evergreens. Peggy and Polly made paper chains and silver paper stars for it. We tried to get some little candles in Papeete, but that was a hopeless search; so we took big white candles and cut them into short pieces and fastened them on.

The natives gathered around, quite enchanted with it all. They had never seen a Christmas tree. Our Taihai and his best friend, a middleaged man, spent two hours arranging and rearranging the paper chains and stars and candles. 

We invited the whole neighborhood around for 

Christmas Eve, and had our tree lit, out under the roofed patio. Harry distributed fire-crackers and the crowd had a wonderful time. 

On Christmas Day many of the natives came bringing us small gifts of freshly caught fish, a string of shrimp, oranges and a breadfruit, hot from the roasting fire, wrapped in purao leaves to keep it hot for our dinner.

Taihai presented a small roast pig and stayed to dinner and supper to help eat it. 

There was a dance over at the Angels' home, on their big veranda. The neighbors brought guitars and ukuleles and supplemented the victrola. Of the dozen native men and women, the six or eight half-castes and two French, not one wore shoes of any kind. So when the natives asked Harry and me to dance with them, we removed our shoes, too. These folks know the fox trot style of some 10 years ago, but were eager to learn a few newer steps which we showed them. 

All the natives keep open house for Christmas week, serving home-made orange and fei wine, or the cheap French wine they buy in Papeete. 

Groups promenade the road, flower-crowned and singing, going from house to house all day. As they get more and more hidehi they put on more

flowers, and hula more vigorously. At New Year's they celebrate more than at Christmas, with singing and hulas lasting all night. 

The folks passing on the road drop in to greet us and take another look at the wonderful Christmas tree. If I am typing they stand outside the "opens" and watch the process. Most of them are speechless with wonder, but one or another exclaims, "Good" occasionally. 

I don't think they ever saw anyone type before.

They've seen machines in offices in Papeete, but never were they allowed to stand within three feet of a typewriter and watch the typing going on. And the gasps that go up when I change the page of paper! Sometimes boys stand rooted just outside the window for an hour, listening for the tinkle of the little bell. 

The itinerant barber who comes by once a month has been making good money over the holidays. He set his chair on the porch of the Chinese store, tacked up his tiny mirror on the wall, and went to work, making the young bloods of the neighborhood presentable for New Year's We're still laughing over Harry's experience with the barber. He didn't have hot water, towels or even a comb, just a pair of scissors, a straightedge razor, a squirt gun of soap and a plain kitchen chair. First he squirted cold soap on 

Harry's face with three pumps of the gun. Then beginning low on the neck he brought the razor up, over his jaw, and over his cheek with one long, quick movement. Six of these upward swoops

finished the shave in half a minute. There was no steaming, or washing off of soap, or patting, or fanning such as an American barber will indulge in for 15 minutes. Nor was there any conversation. 

When Harry indicated he wanted his hair trimmed up at the back of the neck, the barber picked up a scrap of newspaper and cut a curve like a new moon with one swipe of his razor. Then holding this paper pattern against Harry's neck he shaved around the edge of the pattern with a single quick swish. He waved Harry to the little mirror on the wall where he was supposed to comb his own hair. But he hadn't known that customers supply their own combs in Tahiti, and hadn't one with him. So that ended the shave and hair trim. A minute and a half. One franc.

We had our yearly visit from the house and yard inspector, too, during Christmas week. He is a native of this neighborhood employed by the French government to go about and see that yards are clean. He came with two men friends, the native chief of this district, and a following of small boys.

The chief carried a huge bouquet of flowers which he smelled frequently and loudly. He was very sociable and very happy. The inspector and his friends were very happy and very sociable. 

They all wore large, floppy wreaths made of red flowers and ferns. We had seen them coming down the road in the best of New Year's spirits, talking loudly and singing. They stopped in the yard of our neighbor for an hour or more.

Arriving at our house, they shook hands with us heartily, telling Harry over and over, "I like you. You good man." It was the first time they had met him.

By way of inspection, they ordered all the grass cut down close to the house. After many protestations of friendship, and much hand clasping, they discovered a small amount of firewood stored under the bedroom and said it should be stacked under the living room instead. I don't know whether that order was based on esthetic or moral grounds.

When they began kissing all around, French style, I escaped into the house and got busy on some work. Then they joined the crowd that was marveling at the typewriting, and remained until 

I closed the machine and put it away.

The schools are on summer holidays now, from 

Christmas to the end of February, so there are many boys and girls from the upper schools in Papeete, vacationing in the country with families and friends. The districts are festive with the home-comings of the youngsters. The Friday evening truck that brought them out from town was decked with flowers and the streamers of red leaves these people love so well. We heard the truck far down the road, with its siren shrieking and, between blasts the stamping of many feet in time to music. A number of guitars and ukuleles were accompanying the voices. 

The boys and girls sang with abandon and glee.

Home for the holidays! You could hear it in their voices. They were singing the rollicking, rowdy dance hall tunes they had learned on the streets of Papeete. 

Their voices came nearer until the truck stopped before the school teacher's home a few yards down the road. A group of laughing boys and girls jumped off. Shura, the young teacher, ran to meet them, and was fairly smothered with hugs and kisses. Six were friends and relatives who would spend the holidays with her. The others were her former pupils in the district school, just off the truck a moment to greet her.

Shura's visitors, like all the youngsters on the truck, wore flower garlands with their best clothes of spotless white, the girls in thin, sleeveless dresses, cut low in the back, island style, and the boys in white duck suits.

The next afternoon they came to our little home to tell us all were ready to go to the "Hopu Papi," the fresh water stream with the swimming hole. 

They brought us flower crowns, for we must deck ourselves before starting. Taking our paddles with us, we joined the group on the beach where two big outrigger canoes were waiting for us. 

Nephews, nieces, sisters, neighbors, friends; all in picnic attire, the boys in swimming trunks, shoulders shining with coconut oil, the girls in brief shorts and "bibs" just as you might see on any California beach.

The boys steadied the canoes as they floated in the shallow water inside the shore reef, while

we climbed in. We headed for the long palmfringed point that juts far out into the lagoon down toward Teahupoo.

The sunlit water rippled back from the bows of the long, slim boats, and the forward, upturned ends of the outriggers. On our right lay the line of the white surf, breaking on the big outer reef. 

Behind us rose the rugged peaks of the main island, sloping to the low land of the isthmus, and then again on our left, swinging up to the ridges of our own "Little Tahiti." We moved through a world of color: Blue, green and purple were in coral, mountains, sea and sky. 

Then Shura began to sing an old boating song of her mother's people, with the strong rhythm of dipping paddles. The others took up the song.

A boy took the half coconut shell that was used for a bailer, and beat it against the side of the canoe, adding a thumping, syncopated rhythm that was strangely primitive. 

From one song to another they went, led by 

Shura. Polynesian stories of the past, set to plaintive tunes. Tales of fishing and storms on the reef. Songs of wine drinking and dancing and love, in the Tahitian tongue, and many more in

French, old chansons and folk rhymes, with the lilting touch of France about them.

We neared the point of land where the mountain stream tumbled into the lagoon, and headed the canoes into the river mouth. We paddled up stream until the water grew too shallow for the loaded canoes, and the boys leaped overboard and waded,

pulling the lightened canoes after them. Soon we were in water less than knee deep, and we all waded ashore, beaching the dugouts on the gravel bank. 

A short walk brought us to the swimming hole, and there was a race to see who could dive in first. 

Then brown arms and legs seemed to be everywhere, and the white flower crowns went drifting down the stream.

After an hour of romping and tumbling in the cold water, the girls came out with glowing cheeks. They went to a nearby house and picked flowers for new heis. They sat in the sunshine, spreading their hair to dry and stringing blossoms.

Sun-dried and refreshed, we gathered around 

Shura, who was serving lunch from the biggest canoe. I had expected native foods, but the younger set from Papeete is as modern about picnics as clothes. The food, which was unwrapped from clean napkins, was exactly what you might have on any picnic in the States. Roast pork sandwiches with butter and mustard, sweet pickles, marble cake and orangeade in gallon containers.

The sun was getting low and the mountains were gathering purple shadows when we started home. The lagoon was glassy smooth, reflecting sunset colors.

In quiet mood, the boys and girls sang the songs of the island. Lights of supper fires began to twinkle and blue smoke drifted up through the palms. 

Twilight in the tropics is only a moment's

indecision, then the stars are with you.

The songs died away to a humming, with heels barely tapping an accompaniment. A full moon came up over the mountains and lit our way through the shore reef. They landed us at the little beach by our own front door. "Thank, you for a happy time," "bon soir madame," "merci," "good night."
