"Ioarana, ioarana, vahine, Harry" and their happy voices faded into the night as they paddled on through the moonlight to Shura's home. 

From my own little girls, "Oh, mother, we had such a happy time. We love this Tahiti."

I was awakened the other morning by a voice calling "Ioarana" outside my bedroom window, and sat up to find my neighbor peeking at me. 

She grinned broadly to see me still in bed at 7 o'clock, for all the natives are up at dawn. She invited me to go with her to the reef to get shellfish.

For about three days in each month the tides are low enough so people may walk on the outer reef if the winds are moderate or off shore. So I dashed around with twice my usual speed to get a hasty breakfast. Fifteen minutes later Hina stepped over our fence and crossed our garden, ready to go. She had changed her crisp white dress for an old red one, full-skirted and knee length. 

Her curling hair was caught in two braids that glistened with coconut oil. A broad pandanus hat shaded her dark eyes. She carried a copra sack

and a slender iron, sharpened at one end, and indicated that I should take the same equipment. 

Taking our paddles from their place in the rafters, we stepped across the road to the beach slid our canoe across the black sand, and floated it in the narrow strip of water inside the inner reef. This inner reef circles the island like a colorful but broken necklace lying close to the shore. 

The coral thrives in the warm saltiness of the lagoon, but dies at the touch of the cool water from the mountain streams, so each brook makes a break in the necklace. The coral grows close to the surface, so only at the highest tides can a canoe cross over it to reach the deep water beyond. 

On this morning the tide was so low we had to wade along the shallow beach lane to the nearest break, where we could push out into deeper water then leap aboard, heading for the outer reef a mile away.

Outside the shore reef the lagoon bottom dropped away steeply to dim blue depths where tropical fish glowed like tiny colored lights.

Farther out the color changed to ultramarine as we passed over the sea caverns of the middle lagoon, then changed again to cobalt and a fresh sky blue as we neared the iron marker that warns of a submerged coral bank, We paddled slowly and watched tiny fish dart in and out of the myriad caves in the coral. A strange and glowing sea world lay a paddle length below us in water so clear we could see the separate scales on finger-length fish.

Rare and lovely tints were in the coral, lavender and blue, amber and gold, palest shell pink and titian, shading down to the red of wine in the sunlight.

The coral shapes are as varied as their colors. 

Some grow like flowers, leaves and miniature trees. Others are the perfect branched horns of a deer. Round mushroom shapes were there in all sizes from wee mites to those that were large enough for a mermaid's couch. On flat-topped coral tables we saw strange bits of sea life: a starfish lazily moving his points as he searched for food; the mottled shells of tiny sea creatures moving at a snail's pace, going about some business of their little lives, and the scarlet, hairy legs of hermit crabs scuttling after some morsel of sea fare. 

Looking back, little Tahiti, our home peninsula, appeared to be a great green jewel, rising from her watery blue bed. Before us the line of black that was the exposed reef, grew plainer to see. 

The green combers of the Pacific loomed higher and higher as we neared the reef, beating with steady thunder on the far side while we, on the lagoon side, cut through water as quiet as a sheltered lake. But now we had shot out of the deep water and were coming to the coral shallows that lie just at the side of the reef. At high tide the reefs are covered with ample water to float a canoe, but today the water had drawn away until there was a bare four inches in many places. 

Twisting water lanes were between the coral

"flower beds" so we stood up to see the passages better and worked our way among the coral clumps until we were about two canoe lengths from the bare reef. Hina stepped out on the coral. 

I put on tennis shoes and followed. 

We stepped gingerly, for fear of breaking through the lacy growth, stepping on the heavier mushroom corals when we could. With each gentle swell we lifted the canoe and eased it toward the reef until we were able to set it out high and safe on the rock-like reef. Hina looked about among the seaweed growing on the coral and found a solid little arch to which she could tie the canoe. 

I've never seen the great wall of China, but that's the first thing I thought of when I looked along the curving length of the reef. There's no fear of breaking through here for it appears strong and enduring as solid masonry. Wide as a highway, its level top is black, water worn coral, partly hidden under the green seaweed and moss that cling in a million crannies. On the shore side, the lagoon shimmered away to the palm-fringed beach. On the off side the deep purple of a tropical sea heaved rhythmically and spent its force in white breakers against the bulwark of the reef.

Taking our sacks and irons, we began our search for the shellfish called maoa. Stooping down, Hina called and showed me the twisted shell of the little creature. She pried it up with her iron, then turned it over to show me how quickly it closed a little round door in the bottom of its shell. It had a snug house with the door closed, and resisted

all my efforts to open it. Later Harry broke the ice pick trying to force the "lock." 

In the next 10 minutes Hina found a dozen more, while I was still trying to see my first one. 

She was vastly amused when I passed them by without seeing them. Then she would point them out to me and insist that I put them in my sack. 

She was delighted when I found my first one, and and I don't know which one of us squealed the most over it.

We weren't the only food gatherers on the reef. Two boys were having an exciting time capturing a small but lively octopus that one of them had speared. Twisting himself off the spear, the octopus has retreated to a nook in the coral. 

Again and again the boys almost got him out but he would writhe away and get a new grip on the rough coral with the suction cups of his tentacles. 

The octopus was fighting hard for his life and the boys were fighting for a prized delicacy for the family table. They won in the end, but it was a terribly mutilated piece of sea life that was finally flung quivering into the canoe.

A breeze came up from the south and began to ruffle the lagoon. The incoming tide sent the breakers against the reef with greater force so that an occasional wave washed over in a thin sheet, filling all the pockets in the coral and giving new life and action to the seaweed. Hina and J made our way slowly along to the big break in the reef where old ocean surged into the lagoon in long green rollers.

The breeze brushed the water into a million ripples. Waves from the ocean side swirled up on the reef ankle deep, and we had to wait until each wave passed over for we couldn't see the maoas through the foam and swirling seaweed.

Our sacks were heavy with our find. The wind veered and drove breakers, knee-high across the reef. Our canoe rose on the waves and strained at its rope.

The whole reef was awash. The coral shallows were deep under water. 

We reached the outrigger and with the wind at our backs, and waves rolling up from the stern pushing us, we made a quick trip across the lagoon and beached the canoe under the old melo tree. 

For supper we ate maoa stew. The muscular foot which had held each little creature to the coral was too tough for us, though the body was soft. We gave the tough parts to the cats. Polly remarked, "The cats eat their feet. We eat their faces."

"DOCTOR," TANE 

Severe treatment.

Well, we live and learn. We've found out much about native cures lately. Harry was the sufferer but his pains are over now. First he had a plague of boils on both hands and one foot. There isn't a great deal to do for boils until they head up, but these seemed so slow in developing, and the agony was prolonged. Then the cores didn't come out and new boils started in almost the same old spots.

At last our faithful Taihai, who idolizes Harry, couldn't stand it any longer. In his sympathy he seemed to suffer almost as much as Harry. 

So Taihai took it upon himself to get aid. He disappeared down the beach one morning and returned about an hour later with a young native "doctor." This man was from Taihai's home island of Raiataia. He was six feet two, with the muscles of a side show strong man. He had the deep bronze of sun and wind added to his natural, soft chocolate color. A mop of black stood up in curling confusion on his head.

A beautiful specimen of manhood, by name 

Tane, pronounced in two syllables.

"Doctor," Tane

"DOCTOR," TANE 151 Tane is popular in our district, a good dancer, captain of the local football team, and has ever ready laughter in his eyes. When he came to us as a doctor he was dressed just as he had been on the football field. His only garment was an old pair of duck trousers slashed off midway between knee and hip. Four of his team followed him.

Later we discovered they were his assistants whenever he was called on a case. Tagging them came the usual group of small boys looking for any excitement the neighborhood had to offer. 

Tane and his helpers entered our living room, led by Taihai. Harry lay on the couch with hands and foot bandaged. Their greeting was subdued in the presence of pain. Their faces showed great concern as these islanders always do at the slightest suggestion of trouble or illness. Since both 

Harry's hands were bound, Tane placed his hand on Harry's chest in lieu of handshaking. His gestures were eloquent of sympathy. 

Taihai brought a box and Tane seated himself by the couch. He shook his head gravely. It was a perfect "bedside manner," but Tane knew nothing of that. "My friend, you-you-" here his English gave out and he finished in Tahitian, "mauiui roa,”— "very sick." Harry groaned appropriately. The four assistants stood at the foot of the couch, looking utterly miserable.

Very gently Tane removed a bandage and exposed an inflamed boil that was nearing a head. "Aita maitai, no good," Tane murmured, and

his four assistants took up the chorus, "aita maitai, aita maitai."

Tane now dispatched three of the helpers on errands, while he sat stroking Harry's arm above the boil, with a downward, circular motion. 

Helper No. 1 returned shortly bringing a fiber from a coconut husk. Tane held this by its two ends across the head of the boil pressing firmly on it. Then he began twisting the fiber ends around and around until the rough scales of the fiber caught into the core. 

Helper No. 1 was rubbing downward on Harry's arm all the time. Meanwhile helper No. 2 returned with several articles which he laid on the table, then he took his place at Harry's side ready to help hold his arm if he should try to jerk away.

The hard core began working up and up as the fiber pulled it and the massage pushed it to the surface. At last it was so firmly caught in the fiber that Tane lifted it with a steady pull. 

Harry yelled and the assistants held him. Out it came, and relief was immediate. The other two helpers returned with various articles: A bottle of coconut oil, several Pini leaves, a tiny green coconut just out of the flower stage, a tin can and a large nail, and one of the hard green fruits of the Hotu tree.

The one with the tin can cut off the lid and split the can down lengthwise. With nail and a hammer he made holes in the half can till he had an improvised grater. He grated some of the outside shell of the little green nut with this, and

"DOCTOR," TANE 153 half of the Hotu fruit, putting enough coconut oil with them to make a paste. He mashed it together and had a poultice. Tane put this over the opening of the boil with several Pini leaves over and around the poultice.

In the old days this would have been bound on with purao bark, but since we offered bandage, Tane used it. "You sleep." said Tane. "You sleep long time."

Tane petted Harry as though he were a child. "Tomorrow I come. You no mauiui long time. You sleep all same baby."

Harry slept. Tane came later when the other boils had headed up, removed the cores, and they all healed quickly.

About a week later Harry stepped on a rusty nail and punctured the sole of his foot. We soaked it in hot turpentine water, put iodine and salve on it and kept it bandaged. But it grew steadily worse. Such sure signs of blood poisoning developed that we made a rush trip to the Papeete Hospital. The French doctor in charge gave him a hypodermic injection to prevent lockjaw, and cauterized the wound. For two days it improved, then suddenly swelled and became very painful. 

Taihai was keenly distressed. "No good. One week you die. Me get Tane. Tane know."

So the second time he brought Tane to Harry's rescue. He arrived as before, followed by his helpers. He tenderly removed bandages, and shook his head sadly. Two assistants were sent out again for various leaves and fruits and coconut

oil. He stroked the leg and ankle a long time then called for hot water. He soaked the injured foot well with some kind of root in the water. 

Next Tane whittled a sliver of bamboo to a needle point and opened the hole in the sole of the foot. He wrapped the point of the bamboo probe with some fluff of kapok and dipped it in coconut oil. 

His next move was to get all four helpers on

Harry's leg for the painful part was about to begin. Tane held the oiled kapok above the flame of a little torch till it was almost on the point of bursting into flame itself. Then he stuck it in the opened hole and pushed it down as far as he could. Harry yelled and jerked-he couldn't help it but he didn't get very far with the four huskies holding him.

The burning oil operation was repeated six times, then a poultice applied. Harry slept and the swelling and pain were almost gone when he awakened.

Tane refused all payment for his services, but Taihai told Harry on the side that it was customary to send the doctor a bottle or two of wine so he and his helpers could throw a little party in memory of a good deed done. Tane, the native doctor who cured Harry's boils so efficiently, handled a really bad case of broken bones not long ago, and the patient recovered beautifully.

The sufferer was a young Tahitian named

"DOCTOR," TANE 155 Poria, who has his little thatched house down the beach about a mile. Poria fell from the top of a coconut palm and was sorely broken up. 

Tane rolled the patient in a flexible mat of pandanus fiber and took him to his own home. Still rolled in the mat he was lowered into a canoe filled with cool water from a mountain stream, then the mat was gently removed till the body was relaxed in the water, half floating with the head raised slightly on a pad of breadfruit leaves as big as a sofa pillow.

Young men were sent up in the mountains for the leaves of the mato tree. These were crushed in the water and their juice had the effect of lowering the temperature considerably. Poria lay in the chilly water and shivered and shook for 12 hours. Tane sat by, pressing the broken bones in place very gently, aided by the constant shaking and shivering.

After 12 hours in the chilled water, a fire of coconut husks was built nearby and some porous lava rocks were heated. These were put into the canoe with the patient, one by one, until the water was as hot as he could bear it. By changing the stones constantly the water was kept at a high temperature for the next 12 hours.

When I saw Poria he was just finishing his first stretch in the steaming immersion. 

He was nude except for a waist cloth. His skin glowed darkly red through the clear water. He lay in a state of stupor that verged on suspended animation. Sweat stood out on his forehead and

upper lip. His eyes were partly open most of the time, yet he seemed asleep, as cats sleep with the whites of their eyes showing. 

I could see the rapid beating of his heart between his ribs as his blood raced. It was the only movement in his broken body. He did not seem to breathe.

Tane explained that the heat was to prevent undue swelling in the broken limbs. All the time 

Tane was gently massaging the sufferer, feeling for the broken ends of bones and placing them almost by instinct, it seemed. 

This was the end of the hot water treatment, and it was time for the cold again. An assistant arrived from the upper mountain fastness with fresh mato leaves. The hot rocks were removed and the water allowed to cool slowly. Poria's body lost some of its ruddiness. His heart gradually slowed. The mato leaves were crushed into the water, chilling it bit by bit. A convulsive shiver ran over Poria's body. His eyes opened unseeingly. then closed. His face became drawn and his teeth chattered. 

I dipped my fingers in the water and was amazed at its chill. A bluish tinge began to show around the patient's mouth. Shiver followed shiver up and down the length of his body until the water was constantly agitated. "Tane, you sleep?" I asked. "No too much sleep," he answered. "Two days I no sleep. By an' by I sleep long time." 

Tane's wife brought him endless bowls of hot

"DOCTOR," TANE 157 black coffee and small loaves of French bread. "Too much coffee no good," I remarked. At that he threw back his head and laughed heartily. "Ah, coffee good. Coffee plenty good for Tahiti boy. Coffee no good Amerita, maybe," and his eyes twinkled, "plenty good for Tahiti boy." "How long you keep Poria in water?" I inquired. "Ten day, 10 night," Tane answered. "You'll both be dead by that time," I predicted. But I was wrong. Poria's bones set in record time, and he is about as good as new now. 

I just saw him the other day climbing to the top of a tall cocopalm, carrying his slasher knife in his teeth. He was going to get fronds for weaving "niao" for his unfinished cook house. 

That's what he was doing when he fell.

WAS IT CANNIBALISM? 

Quite a different situation arose in our village lately, and the natives have been upset for two weeks, excitement runs high, the bamboo telegraph is busy and the coconut radio is broadcasting throughout the land. Small knots of natives gather at the store, on the beach, along the road to argue the matter of cannibalism. 

At first there were just sly winks and nods, slighting whispers and criticisms, 'sotto voce.' 

Later more disputing voices rose and loudly, fingers were pointed, hints were no longer veiled, until at last fists were shaken openly, and the entire village was dividing into two opposing parties. Relatives were at odds with each other, and the friendships of years were ruptured.

We knew something was wrong, but didn't know

Note the little one second from the left: See scars on buttocks, left leg and both ankles? That's where the pigs feasted on him. He is almost four years old now, and as big as his next brother, five, who is losing his pants.

WAS IT CANNIBALISM? 

161 what it was all about until a delegation from each side came to us to explain the trouble and ask which side we would be on. 

The matter started, they told us, some eight weeks before, and concerned the family of Teata and his wife Hutia, who live on the beach across from the Chinaman's store. 

Hutia, then 23, bore her fifth baby at Christmas time, when the next youngest was nearly 

11 months old. Most of the care of the new baby fell on the oldest girl, a child of six. Such families are common and the mothers take their responsibilities lightly. So when Teata earned 60 francs making copra, he and Hutia boarded the 2 A. M. truck for Papeete to celebrate, leaving the children to get on as best they could until their return at six in the evening.

All went well until the six-year-old went out to play leaving the infant asleep on the floor of the cookhouse. A blazing coconut husk rolled from the fire and burned the baby's leg severely. His screams brought the sister from her play but the best she knew to do was to push the burning husk away. So the baby lay on the ground for the remainder of the day, crying in pain, and dozing from exhaustion, by turns.

When the parents returned at dark they washed the leg in cow's urine as an antiseptic measure, and that finished their duty as they saw it. 

For the next three weeks the baby rolled about the dirt floor of the cooking shed with no farther treatment of the burn. The flesh festered and fell

away. Never bandaged, it was exposed to dirt and insects daily, but at last it started to heal. 

Neighbors advised Teata to call Tane to treat the leg, but the father declared that if God saw fit to burn the baby, perhaps let the baby die from the burn, that was not his business. 

At last tender new flesh began to build up from the bone outward. The child's incessant whimpering stopped, and he now learned to smile again, but his troubles were not over. 

Hutia, now expecting her sixth child, was away at a neighbor's house, making a little mattress for the coming baby from the cottony pods of the kapok tree. She was called home hastily by the frightened 6-year-old, who ran screaming, "pigs! pigs!"

The mother ran home to find the baby screaming in agony while two half grown pigs attacked it. A brother, three, was beating them off as best he could with a stick, but the new flesh of the healing leg was eaten to the bone. 

Now Teata and Hutia began to wonder if they were taking the right care of the baby, and discussed ways and means to keep the pigs from the child until its leg should be healed again. Of course they might build a fence around the bamboo cooking hut where the baby spent his days, but that would mean a day's work. 

Would it not be simpler and pleasanter to have a feast on the pigs? Yes, surely, for a feast is always more welcome than labor. 

So the pigs were killed, and friends and relatives

WAS IT CANNIBALISM? 

163 invited. And the dancing, singing, and all the guitar twanging that go with every roast pig in the South Seas, continued until the early morning hours.

It was the next morning that the criticisms and the whisperings began, and grew and grew, until the community was divided. 

The all important question was: Since the parents ate the pigs that had PARTLY EATEN 

THEIR CHILD, were they not guilty of cannibalism?

It was a knotty problem for the simple minds of South Sea islanders. So at last they came to us for our opinion.

We refused to take sides in the matter. We questioned them a bit and found out that all the criticism was coming from the group that had 

NOT been invited to the feast. So you see folks are very much the same the world over! 

You want to know about the baby? He is getting well. Infants are tough down here.

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y! 

"Arearea!"

The local coconut radio has been sizzling for weeks. "A rich American lady is to visit our district. 

A multi-millionaire. She is traveling in the odd places of the earth, dropping coins with the greatest of ease. She is rich, rich, rich! "She is young. She travels in a private yacht. 

She gives "plenty moni' to poor natives. She wants to see fishing with nets, dancing, a native feast. She will come on Saturday and fling a super super-party. No, 'grief and disappointment,' she is leaving for America." Joy again, "it was a side trip to the Poumatous, only. She has taken a sweetheart from one of the tiny islands. A handsome, unsophisticated giant. He wears a diamond which she put upon his finger, and thinks it a pretty stone. She

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y 165 

bought shoes for him, his first. He is bursting with pride over the shoes. "At last she is coming to Vairao. timey' will be at the chief's house. 

1500 francs for a dancing team.

The 'big 

She will pay

She will pay 500 francs for a fishing party. She will buy so much wine and beer it will flow like water. Blissful expectations." 

For a month the dancing team was drilling on the grass by the biggest house in our district. 

Old man Amaru, a veteran of ancient days, was entrusted with the drum. His pride was beautiful to behold. His grey head bobbed in rhythm with shoulders and arms as he beat time with guava sticks. His drum? 

His drum? Empty coal oil tin held between bare feet. With shining eyes he watched 

Henry for signals. 

Henry . . nineteen, slim, an interesting pale look from his strain of white blood. Soulful eyes which break hearts. Agile. No more hips than a snake. An abdomen which rotates with hula action as naturally as a fish' tail waves. 

Henry always starts for evening practice wearing a white shirt and sky blue pants with the "poured-into" look; a white flower stuck over his left ear. He comes down the road from his home a couple of miles away, twanging a guitar. His pal, who lives in Henry's family when Henry isn't living in his pal's family, accompanies him. They sing as they stroll toward the meeting place. 

Neighbors hear and drift out. The young men wear white, with an occasional pair of yellow.

green or pink trousers among them. The girls are in white or light-colored dresses of cheap American make. A few wear silk dresses, fearfully and wonderfully made by the Chinese women in Papeete. They always have numberless ruffles and flounces, but no fit. Many are barefooted, but some wear white tennis shoes, or sandals with high heels. They follow Henry and join the singing. Their band grows. 

Since all the native homes are on the single roadway which girdles the island, there is no one out of hearing, and even the smallest child knows what is afoot. There are no frantic last-minute telephone calls to gather the team; nor notices from pert secretaries. No "push" or "pressure" talks by organizers and managers. 

Passing the meeting place about sunset, the group continues down the curving road to gather the dancers who live in the other direction. At the Chinaman's store they pick up a tagging of small boys and girls who add squealing, giggling and pushing to the tail of the procession; the same sort of play you see on any schoolground in 

America. Another guitar and a couple of ukuleles have joined the parade.

We hear the natives coming as we eat supper in the arbor twined with bean vines, beside our house. They pass within thirty feet of us, but since a discreet glance shows we are at our meal, they pretend they haven't seen us. (Such strange ways the white people have . . . carrying their food outdoors and watching the sunset over the

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y 167 

ocean while they eat!) They wander a mile beyond our house, adding to their group until fifty dancers are collected, then return strengthened by fifty more onlookers, all singing.

Our supper finished, they "see" us for the first time. "Harry, Harry, come hula hula." "Harry Vahine," that's me, "come hula hula." "O. K. boss," we answer, for that is the proper form for us to use.

They linger while we get light wraps and dim the lamp in the living room. They open a space for us in their ranks behind the guitars and ukes, and we are off with the singing procession. 

We turn from the road at Amaru's home. 

Torches are lit. Off come shoes and sandals. Bare toes wriggle in relief. The tendency is to sit down and just sing on and on, but Henry is getting busy, ordering dancers to their places for the first hula. A few respond. Others sit and smoke cigarets rolled from strong native tobacco. Henry urges them. He tries to keep youngsters from romping on the dancing space. He is never helped by the mothers; it's none of their business! 

They are concerned with gossip and flirtations. 

Henry has enough white blood to persevere. At the end of a half hour he has most of them in their places. He is perspiring freely, and has shed his shirt revealing pale green underwear. 

He signals to Amaru who drives experimenting boys away from his precious kerosene can. He beats a loud and tin-y tatoo. A volley of Tahitian

from Henry, who calls the name and action of the first dance. 

Stragglers leap to place as if touched by live wires. Henry poses like a high priest of the ancient Druids, and leads off the singing. Then 

Henry becomes fluid: swaying, twisting and shivering along his spine until you think he will dislocate every joint. He doesn't.

After leading the dance three times over, Henry retires. When he reappears for the next set, he is clad only in a loin cloth. The sky blue pants are at rest for the evening between two mattresses on Amaru's guest bed. The green undershirt is hung on a limb to dry. 

One hula was danced by a double circle, men inside, and their women partners on the outside of the ring. All sang. It was a dance of courtship, as most of the hulas are. Then the team sat crosslegged on the ground in a close circle, the guitar and ukulele players in the center.

Skirts were pulled up and bare legs slapped in rhythm. 

A girl would leap up and dance, solo, until she chose a sweetheart. The man whom she courted would answer with an ardent love hula until she retired to her place. Then the man would pay court to some other girl. She would respond after some coaxing, and dance opposite him. After a time she would flirt away and choose another partner, while the deserted man took his seat in the circle. So it went until eight or ten had danced.

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y 169 

Each solo dancer has a different style, expressing his own personality, so it is not wearisome to watch. One dances almost entirely with arms and shoulders, another with hips, a third does wonders with his abdomen, while a fourth leaps and prances. They begin learning in childhood, imitating their elders for fun. 

Another hula is practiced and sweat gleams on their bodies. 

But that "What a shame they don't wear grass skirts or pareaus all the time," we think. fashion went out 200 years ago.

Now they are in all stages of undress. Most of the men have stripped to loin cloths, or have their trousers rolled high. Some of the girls have discarded frilly dresses and are dancing in their slips. We, who are watching, are glad of our jackets, for the mountain breeze is cool. 

When practice is over, they are quickly dressed again, for they are sticklers for propriety on some things. Where an American might think, "Oh, I'll just go home as I am," your Tahitian must be in full regalia for the procession on the road. 

They drift into groups and trail homeward, still singing.

The big day came when the rehearsals should bloom into an "Arearea”. The American lady was expected at 2 o'clock. By noon natives had gathered at the chief's house. Dancers came carrying skirts of ti leaves. Young men had gathered the leaves at dawn. Girls and women had been busy shredding them into fluttering

ribbons, and weaving them with purao fibre to make close-fitting yokes around the hips. 

Skirts for men and women were alike, reaching a little below the knee. The men wore loin cloths, diaper style that's what they call it hereunder their skirts. The women wore bibs and skirts of red and white pareau cloth. The girls' hair streamed loose in black clouds. All were loaded with flower strings on heads and shoulders.

At four o'clock the multi-millionairess had not arrived. We were handed to chairs on the verandah where a dozen of our white neighbors were gathered. We waited. 

The chief was nervous. He has much white blood and takes responsibilities hard. He looks like an English major, with white skin, clipped mustache and military bearing. It's a surprise when he speaks Tahitian, for you expect a broad Oxford accent to match his pith helmet and blue eyes. You are inclined to call him "Colonel" and look for a monocle. But he was born here and has never left the islands. His wife is very dark and they have a houseful of mahogany grandchildren.

As the crowd grew restless, the coconut radio began broadcasting among the natives. "The rich lady has changed her mind. She has been fooling about the 2000 francs. White people are no good. 

They always fool poor Tahiti people. The chief has received two kegs of wine from the lady. 

Why isn't it passed around?"

A wine keg was hoisted onto a sawed-off stump,

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y 171 

the district policeman put in charge, and a line formed. A hilarious, good-natured line, with grumblings forgotten. Each person had his purao leaf folded to form a drinking cup. The original sanitary drinking cup tracked down to its source! The man at the spigot was everybody's friend. Children got their share without question. Mothers gave their babies little sips. 

Everybody was happy. 

A shrieking siren. Shouts. A surge to the road. The lady had arrived and all delays were forgiven. A child in white was pushed forward to present flowers. Henry and his band struck up a song of welcome. The chief bowed, holding his helmet on his stomach quite properly. The lady was escorted to the beach amid cheers.

A roofed hut had been prepared on a platform laid across two large dugouts resting in the water. It was in similar shelters that royalty was transported from island to island in ancient times. Natives picked up the lady and her companions and carried them through the breakers to the little floating house. More cheers. The guests waved to us on shore, and the house and a dozen canoes headed for deeper water.

Men threw out a fish net as they paddled in a great circle. The net was like a fence in the water, standing upright, weighted with little stones along the bottom, and buoyed up with purao plugs along the top. A dozen men dove into the lagoon at points around the net. At a signal they began swimming, pushing the net before them, closing

in from all sides at once. Canoes followed with men yelling and beating the water with paddles to drive the fish to the middle of the ring. Terrifying experience for fish in those waters! 

The onlookers shrieked encouragement and advice. Back seat fisher-women advised husbands in the water which way to turn. The chief waved his helmet and lost his flower crown.

When the net was gathered close, men dove under and caught the bottom together, making a bag of it. The catch was fair. The lady declared she would take two of the fish back to Papeete. 

An expensive breakfast at 500 francs. 

The sunset was the kind to make artists rave.

The royal shelter rocked a little and spilled flowers and colored leaves on the water; as romantic as a stage backdrop.

The important folk must have a dip in the gold and green lagoon, and then a feast on the chief's veranda, while the rest of us had our choice of returning home for supper or buying some of the foods offered for sale on the road, by the Chinese storekeepers who were out for business. 

At 7 we gathered again. The dancers were in their skirts. Mysteriously, most of the natives had fresh flower crowns; they can always magic them from somewhere. The wine keg had been flowing again, and all were gleeful. Most islanders need wine before dancing as a mechanical toy needs winding before performing.

The feast was drawing to a close, but champaigne bottles were still popping. Henry and his

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y 173 

singers were grouped at the bottom of the steps chanting lustily. The chief was dispensing the lady's food and wine with a free hand. His flower crown was over one eye. Several natives were getting wobbly.

Torches were lit around the dancing space. In olden days the lights would have been made of palm branches bound together with oily nuts among them. But the white man's ways are easier, so these were large bamboo stalks, stuffed with strips of sacking well soaked in kerosene; lamp wicks 30 feet long. Chairs were placed for the white guests and I found myself next to the 

American lady. 

Her first words were, "I wish my daughters and grandchildren were here to enjoy the fun with me. I'm a guilty grandmother to have such an exciting time without them." 

A grandmother! She didn't look over thirty.

Slim, well-kept figure, sport clothes and a becoming hat. Alive, alert, a typical American woman! 

Charming. Friendly.

The first hula was a boating dance with everyone seated, as in a long war canoe. Only the helmsman, at the end of the lines of paddlers, stood. He held a bamboo pole which he swung about as the song told of a storm at sea. swayed with the rising and falling of the boat in waves "mountain high." All

Not all of the dances were graceful. The next was a modern thing, poking fun at the Chinese. 

The words of the song were, "amu rite" - 'eat

rice', over and over. It was a clownish performance by two men. They came out with rubber inner tubes around their heads instead of flowers; with coconut husks for rice bowls. They gobbled "rite" from the bowls making noises. They pranced and tried to snatch food from each other, grinning and yelling. 

The chorus of "eat rice," "eat rice" grew louder and louder until the two clowns stuck tongues out at each other, pulled hair, tread on toes and burlesqued a fight. The audience howled approval. 

It was their revenge on the yellow men who had taken their copra, pigs and land to settle the debts the improvident brown brothers couldn't pay at the stores. 

In snatches, between dances, the lady told me of her early life, of her struggle to educate her girls on a western ranch with the nearest school twenty miles distant, of their poverty until a gusher brought wealth overnight, of her two years of travel. This was her last stop before returning to America and the children who were sending daily wires for her to hurry home. 

And the handsome native sweetheart? More misinformation and groundless gossip. He was the husband of the lady's Tahitian maid. There was no diamond ring. She had bought him shoes, however, and he was proud of them because they had a loud squeak!

At midnight a truck came by on its run to Papeete. Many visitors boarded it. The wine kegs were empty. A few natives were snoozing peace-

VAIRAO PREPARES FOR A BIG TIME-Y 175 

fully in the bushes, but most of them were still fairly steady on their feet. The fishing boys and the dancing team received their promised francs. 

The lady made her adieus.

We were yawning, for our usual bedtime is 8 o'clock for all hands in the simple life schedule. 

As we dropped to sleep we heard the dancing team pass by, and their songs faded into the night.

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 

No jobs for Americans. Land is the answer, but now you own it and now you don't. A 

Princess to the rescue. We become plantation owners-flat broke.

Days flowed on placidly in our island home. 

Harry was always busy, he was doing the things he wanted to do. The children studied every morning and I was satisfied with their progress. 

Afternoons were filled with canoeing, swimming, walking or gardening. 

We had experienced only one difficulty: A tropical disease which attacked me on the left ankle and hung on for three months in spite of native cures. When I went to the French doctor he ordered Dakin water and the infection disappeared like magic. But we had got back into shoes in the meantime, afraid of exposing ourselves to it or other germ maladies. With the exception of that and Harry's boils, we were all in good health. The girls were unfolding like flowers.

When we four gathered at the dinner table, Harry or I would say, "Isn't it great to be together?"

It was almost like a table grace, we were so thankful for our chance at real family life. "Suppose we were in the states now . . . the

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 177 

children would be in school, miles away, instead of on their little bench opposite. "Girls, do you wish you were back in school?” "No, mother. We love this Tahiti. She is a siren." They have been reading Pierre Loti of late, and picking up new words. "If we were back in America, you and I would probably be a hundred miles apart, looking forward to a Sunday together on the sand dunes. 

Those Sundays went by like a moment, didn't they dear? "Remember when we had to date each other for Wednesday evenings and meet in that horrid little town half way between your work and my school?" Those meetings had meant unsatisfactory dinners in the one dirty little cafe. A stuffy movie, when biting winds drove us in from the desert. Making dates with your husband doesn't sound romantic, does it? It wasn't romantic. Simply the best we could do under the circumstances. No wonder we were happy in Tahiti! 

One night after the twins had gone to bed, Harry said, "Do you know that we have been here eight months, dear? Only four more, now, and we'll be right back where we started - all separated again." "Four months? Is that all the time left to us? 'Tahiti's spell inoculates you like an amiable drug,'" I quoted from the magazine article which had led us here. "I haven't counted days since that morning you and the girls arrived at Papeete.

When I saw you at the ship rail, looking for me in the boat crowd below, all time stopped." "Four months more will finish our year. Has it been worth the price we paid?" "Yes, dear, even if we land at San Francisco, broke." 

Then Harry threw a bombshell, "Why go back? What about staying longer? 

Four or five years?" "But how could we? It isn't costing us much to live, but our book of traveler's checks is growing thinner. Sometime it will be finished, and then what? There aren't any jobs here except for French people." "I've been thinking of ways to make a living for us down here, so we could stay. That is, if you want to, honey." "Of course I want to. So do the girls." "My plan calls for land the first thing. A coco plantation. Make copra. Raise a few cows and sell milk to the white people in the neighborhood. 

They're just pining for clean milk and can't get it." 

We were too excited to sleep much that night. 

We talked and planned. How much must we earn to live here? What would our taxes be? How much would land cost? Did we have enough traveler's checks left to buy land with?

We went over my household accounts to see what expenses were averaging. From the stores we were buying salt, sugar, bread, cooking oil, onions, whole wheat in the grain and chocolate bars. The last were an unnecessary extravagance,

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 179 

but the girls and I had a weakness for them. 

From a native we bought honey at 25 cents a gallon. We didn't try to compete with the native fishermen, but bought fish from them, twice a week, at 4 cents a pound. Fresh meat was peddled on the road, and we had pot roasts to make your mouth water: baby beef at 12 cents a pound. We indulged in doughnuts from the bakery once a week, which was as often as they were on sale. We knew we could reduce the food bill by taking out the white bread and candy bars; eating more breadfruit and honey instead.

But we didn't -we let our stomachs rule us. 

That made up our food buying, since we didn't pay cash for our milk, fruits or vegetables. 

Then for the incidentals: Harry's tobacco, shoes, stamps, kerosene, typewriter ribbons, toothpaste, etc. They amounted to as much as our store bills for food. What more? Taxes. Those bugbears in the states. We had paid a head tax of $30 for the family on taking up residence here, but it would be only $1.50 in following years.

We would have to pay a road tax of $2, or Harry might work seven days on the road instead. Since we had water piped to our house we would owe a water tax of $1.25. No land taxes here! European houses are subject to a small tax, but our native house would be exempt. Dogs are taxed when owners can be found; our cats would get off free. 

If we should stay over a second year our tax total would be $4.75. We compared that with the $250 we had paid to Uncle Sam the year before

on cars, trailer, water and property. We would want a surplus emergency fund for dental work, a long illness or a broken leg. 

After several consultations over the account book we knew just how much we must earn to live here. For the next month we were talking with white neighbors and brown, about coconut yield per tree, expenses of making and handling copra, equipment necessary and selling price per kilo. The information boiled down to this: Two and one half acres of land will support 100 cocotrees. These should average 1 ton of copra a year to sell at $40, the present price. If we hired our work done, wages would take almost half of the income, but Harry planned on doing a great Ideal of the work himself. With all the figures before us we knew just how many acres of plantation land, in full bearing, we needed. Where to find it?

Harry trailed around the country looking at land for sale. He wanted a plantation with a valley behind where he could keep cows. He found that good land near the beach cost $100 an acre. Land farther back could be bought for $40 and $50 an acre.

We heard of so many troubles connected with land buying that we almost gave up a dozen times.

The old tribal custom of inheritance still holds, so that land is seldom divided among heirs, but is left to all surviving relatives in one piece. There may be from five to fifty shareholders in one plantation.

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 181 

At the same time those five or fifty relatives may own shares in other widely-scattered pieces of land. When the present holders die, their children and grandchildren will automatically get shares. 

Who can say what each person owns? The families wrangle over it. They divide the use of the land and products as best they can. One family builds a house on it. Another plants vanilla. The coconuts are collected by one brother this month, by another next month. The bananas belong to three sisters. The first coffee crop goes to one cousin, the second crop to another cousin. Two brothers reserve a corner where they may keep a jointly-owned pig. The papayas are aunty's crop, but the tar was put in by uncle, who shares with three nephews who helped in the planting. Grandmother says the pineapples are hers because a son of her fourth husband planted them, but great aunt Moana cuts them, for she argues, "the boy who planted them was my third cousin by blood, which is closer than a step-son." 

Who is responsible for keeping the swamp drained? Which shareholders must pull which weeds? Who shall burn the fallen palm leaves? 

Whose is the loss when a stray pig roots up the taro, or a loose cow feasts on the bananas? Who is to blame when a 30 foot palm frond falls and wrecks vanilla plants below? For every hour of labor on the land, four hours are spent in fruitless discussion.

Each party is sure he is being cheated a little, so it is natural and right that he should take something from the others when opportunity offers. But there is always a brother passing to see the theft, or a cousin spying from the top of a cocotree. He's been hiding, waiting for a chance to steal a few green drinking nuts, but he slides down in righteous indignation at seeing someone else playing the same game.

Rarely is one shareholder able to buy out the others. If he does he is never left in undisputed possession, for relatives can't forget that they once owned a share. On moonlit nights they return to gather some of the crop they think should be theirs in spite of money received and release papers given. The courts are in a tangle of family cases all the time.

In olden days, a white man chose the land he wished to buy and arranged with the tribal chief for a meeting of shareholders. When all were gathered a candle was lit and placed on the floor with ceremony. The pow-wow began. The white man made his offer of money, trinkets or whiskey.

The natives must take it or leave it by the time the candle burned down. If a shareholder were not at the parley to get his part of the price paid, it was his hard luck.

Today the French government protects the absent shareholder. Many a white man has bought land believing he has interviewed and paid all owners. After he has put up his house and improved the land he finds a dozen more claim-

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 183 

ants. They straggle in from distant islands, demanding substantial sums for their shares in land which they have never seen before. They believe that all white men are rich, all Americans are millionaires, and this is the time to reap a harvest.

If they can prove some claim to the land, the white man is sensible to pay them, get release papers and live happily. If he balks on payment there will be long and expensive court trials which swing in favor of the natives. The purchaser pays through the nose to shareholders, lawyers and court.

Sometimes shareholders refuse to sell and make life miserable for the white man by coming on the land, keeping goats in the flower garden, or building a hut under the living room windows and moving in with a large family. A French lady whom I met on Moorea has been in court two years trying to settle claims on land she bought at auction. An American in our neighborhood cannot buy out certain natives though he offers more than a reasonable price. They own a strip in his front yard and plant taro in his lily pool. 

They claim a part of his back yard and insist on keeping pigs near his water supply.

After weeks of cycling over the island Harry found the perfect combination of two small plantations, in bearing, with a valley behind. 

Where was it? Just two minutes' walk from our house. That same great valley we had looked down on that day we went to the mountains for

oranges. We dreaded to think how many shareholders might claim such a big piece of land. 

Would it be possible to dicker with them all? 

We heard conflicting stories of the ownership, but a trip to the surveyor's office in Papeete convinced us we were in luck. Unbelievable good luck! Plantations and valley were held by one person only. That miracle happened because the owner was the Princess Tereii, one of the few living members of the Royal Family of Tahiti.

Some years before, at the death of Queen Pomare, the crown lands had been divided among the children in white man's style of separate ownership.

The Royal Family gets no money from the government, but has been allowed to keep a large part of the property owned by the last king, Pomare. The royal titles are only honorary now. 

Members of the family have no ruling power, but they hold an important position in Tahitian society.

Then began a series of visits to the princess and consultations with her lawyer. The princess speaks perfect English as well as several other languages, is charming and human. The third time Harry talked with the princess, she asked him to become her land overseer. She would pay expenses and a per cent of the income from her many plantations. Harry and I discussed it, but didn't see how we could accept her offer for the position would mean constant traveling about 

Tahiti, and frequent trips to other islands which

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 185 

lie at a distance. After getting our family together at so much expense and trouble, we would be foolish to separate.

The princess did everything she could to make the sale of her land to us go through smoothly, but in spite of all good luck, the transaction called for three months of patient effort, 19 trips to

Papeete and half the value of the land in 'extra' unforseen expenses. 

When the end of our year's vacation rolled around, instead of going back to the states, almost broke, we were starting life as plantation owners, completely broke.

In the six years of our separations in the states, when we didn't live in our home, and couldn't collect rent from it, we had said, "If we can ever sell this place we will never buy another piece of real estate as long as we live." 

We remembered saying that a thousand times, but here we were with two plantations and a valley on our hands, added to the useless house in America. "We're fools," we told each other. But there must be a special power looking after such fools, for at the time we were feeling "flat broke", a mail steamer brought us a fortune: a $100 payment on our California home which he had left with an agent for sale. "Cows!" said Harry succinctly. "Cows?" "Yes. Two of them to go on our land."

Harry had been looking at cows while he hunted

for land, and had two good ones picked out. 

The day the land became ours we fared out to see our new domain. "It's jungle," Harry had warned me. "Put on a shirt with long sleeves and your stoutest trousers, or the weeds and briars will get you." We explored our great valley, following the brawling stream, climbing its little waterfalls until late in the afternoon, but we never reached the end. We don't know how many miles it extends into the mountains, for the upper end is unsurveyed. These thousands of acres cost nothing. They simply belong with the planted coconut land below. We will never be able to use it all if we live here the rest of our lives. 

The next day we took our map and set out to locate the boundaries of the two plantations. They touched each other at one point, then angled off, with other plantations coming between. In Tahiti, the groves are every shape you ever saw before, and all the shapes you haven't seen. Not satisfied with angles, they go in for curves following streams or lines of cocopalms.

A piece of land, which seems small to possess a separate name, turns out to be subdivided into a dozen smaller bits curving and angling about each other, each with its descriptive name. When the land was used by uncle, grandma, brother and great aunt Moana, each named the portion that he used.

Our map showed us to be the proud possessors of two plantation lands, Torimea Vahine-woman carrying something;

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 187 

Nuuteri-regiment of soldiers with a purpose; and the valley lands designated as: 

Terre Fare Puaa-land of the house of pigs; 

Naonao-mosquito place;

Maimai-land which has illness in it; 

Vaipao-river which digs its bed; 

Vaipoe-river of pearly water; 

Tarahina-silvery point; 

Tetaa-the chin; 

Vahariri-mouth which speaks with anger. 

But which was which? There were no sign posts nor fences.

The native policeman had shown Harry over the land and explained the boundaries before we bought it. The lines were marked by boulders, but they were hidden in grass and weeds, and identical with many other boulders.

We scrambled through the undergrowth for an hour without locating anything that matched our map. Some lines were marked by trees, but since there were trees everywhere, that didn't help. We were hot, scratched by thorns, and tired of pushing back branches.

We called the policeman. Wearing nothing but a loin cloth he slipped through the tangle without getting scratched and went unerringly to the first boundary stone. He pawed aside moss and earth, disclosing it as though it were big as a house, and anyone should have been able to find it!

From stone to stone he went, and from tree to tree, explaining, "tree here-api-young, all same

fence," until I was dizzy trying to remember young trees and old trees and greenish stones and whitish stones. The policeman assured us that any native in the neighborhood could point out these boundaries to us. They knew every land's name and every marker. 

What to do first? "Get a barb wire fence up around Torimea," said Harry, "buy the cows and turn them in. Let them eat some of the green stuff and tramp down the weeds." Harry and our faithful Friday made quick work of the fence, the cows were purchased and told to eat all the jungle they could. "We'll clean off one plantation first," Harry decided, "and get good feed for the cows coming on, then we'll tackle the other." He and Taihai armed themselves with axes and slasher knives and began a four-months' battle for the supremacy of the jungle. They cut down trees and headhigh weeds. They grubbed out stumps and planted sudan grass. Every afternoon the children and I put in two hours' work picking up palm leaves and rotting coconut husks and burning piles of trash and weeds.

There was an appalling number of young coconuts on the ground, knocked down by rats.

They were ruined, with the tender meat scooped out. We would have to buy bands to protect our trees from the marauders. The bands are of metal, sixteen inches wide and long enough to encircle the palm trees. They are tacked around the tree trunks twenty feet above the ground, and

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 189 

present slick surfaces which the rats can't climb over or jump. Thousands of rat families have happy homes in the tops of the trees, so men must climb up and oust papa, mama and baby rats from their nests, before banding the trees. It all costs money, but when a plantation is cleaned and banded, the copra output leaps up 40 per cent. coco grove hadn't

Young Princesses, It was pioneer work for our been cleaned for thirty years. living in Papeete, don't work on their land in the country. This plantation had been in the care of a native who lived near, but the 'care' amounted to picking up the nuts which could be found readily and leaving many to rot or sprout in the ground. Clean land will save one-third of the crop which is lost in the tangle of an unkempt plantation.

The Princess had never seen this place. She owns too many plantations to oversee them personally. She lets them out to aspiring natives on half shares. They gather nuts, make and sell the copra and turn over to the Princess what they think they can spare. She is lucky if she gets one-quarter the value they take off the land. It is because of her vast holdings that her income allows her to spend half her time in Paris.

Our coconut crop was scanty for another reason than rats. We had been told by our white friends to expect a short crop, for there is a way the brown brothers have of putting three month's profit in their pockets at the expense of a new buyer. When the caretaker finds out the

land in his charge is being sold, he waits until the last week before it changes hands, then gathers sons and nephews and they climb the trees cutting down thousands of coconuts. Under ordinary circumstances the nuts are allowed to ripen on the trees until they fall, but this is the caretakers' golden opportunity, so he grabs ripe, semiripe and green nuts. The green nuts will not dry for copra, but they can be sold in town as drinking nuts. Then the caretaker explains to the old owner that he was just about to harvest the nuts when the new owner prevented. The new owner, who hasn't his papers yet, cannot do anything so the plotter has everything his own way. They work it on the whites and on each other. White men, in turn, have learned the trick, and are guilty of it, too. 

There was no possibility of keeping our purchase secret, though we had been warned by friends to do so. With the coconut radio buzzing, every native for miles around knew that the Americans were buying Torimea, Nuuteri, Tetiriri, Torahina, etc., etc., etc. So it happened to us, and we had to wait until more nuts developed before we could make our first copra.

Most of the whites and many natives have racks or dryers with tin roofs which slide back to expose the copra to the sun, and can be rolled back quickly at the first hint of rain. We had no dryer so we planned to work native style at first, and build a rack with a movable roof later. 

Taihai and Harry had picked up nuts as they

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 191 

cleared land, until a respectable pile had grown, then one day Taihai announced that the weather was right for the big event our first copra making. He spread the coconuts on the ground in the sunshine, and gave Harry his first lesson in splitting nuts. Whack! His ax split the thick outer husk, cracked the shell inside, cut through the meat and laid the nut in halves, opened to the sun. The water gurgled out of the nut and the drying process began. An expert worker can split 4000 nuts in a day. They couldn't work so fast with their stone axes in olden times, but neither were they making copra in those days, for it is the white men who use it in making butter substitutes, soap, lubricants and candles. 

If a sudden rain comes, all hands must be quick to flip the drying nuts upside down. Or if they must stay in the shell until the next day, they must all be turned over, backs up, at night, for fear of rain or dew. A native will always have half a dozen children to do that work, but to the European, it's a back-breaking job. In the morning all the nuts must be turned to the sun again. 

A half day of hot sunshine will dry the meat enough so it can be cut out of the shell with a copra knife. Knife! They ought to call it a spoon.

It's built on the lines of the old-fashioned spoons our grandmothers used when they gave us castor oil big, and curved more than your modern teaspoons. Today copra knives are of steel, but

Taihai remembers when he was a boy on a distant island, he helped carve them of orange and mara

wood, and worked for hours sharpening them at the tips and along both sides, heating them among coals, plunging them in water and sharpening them again.

As the white meat is cut out of the shell, the pieces are spread on mats or sacks in the sun, in a clearing or on the beach. Everybody is nervous for the next four days, for rain will ruin it. At night it is taken in the house and looked over for mildew. Hairs of green mold are scraped off. All pray for clear weather and spread it again the next day. Someone watches nearby to drive away pigs, dogs or chickens for they all smell it and come for a feast. A bit of copra is a treat for the horses, cows and goats, too.

When it's dried at last, the Chinese buyer is called. Under his beady eye we sack it. He watches for green meat, mildew or stones to increase weight. Our copra passes all tests and we are paid a fraction of a cent more for "clean" stuff. 

The Chinese trader brings out his "buying" scales, and if we're wise we bring out our "selling" scales. After a proper amount of argument, we split the difference in the weights, the francs are paid, and we are all relieved. The troubles are now with Ah Sing, Ah Ming or Ah Wing who bought the copra. If the price drops suddenly the loss is his.

The Chinese buyers hold it for speculation, but eventually they sell to exporters who are filling orders from France. The copra takes a truck

CAN WE BECOME SELF-SUPPORTING? 193 

ride to Papeete, and lies, sacked, in the big tin sheds at the wharf, until a freighter takes it for a sea journey. You will get a little of it back there in the States when you buy imported French products which contain coconut oil. 

The next time you purchase choice pomades or soaps, labeled "Made in France", you may guess that the oil in them may have started life in a humble way in the top of one of our cocotrees.

A few tricks to revive a rapidly waning enthusiasm.

We had not owned our land three weeks before a delegation of thirty-five young men appeared one evening to speak to Harry. We knew something important was on hand for all were in their 'store' clothes, with flowers stuck over their ears.

They were accompanied by a couple of guitar players. Tane, the doctor, was in the lead. "Ioarana, Harry. Ioarana, Harry Vahine. You big land. Plenty land." 

We agreed. "You plenty land football. Tahiti boy like football. Nuutari-plantation, good football."

Taihai was with the group. His English vocabulary had increased rapidly in the year he had worked with Harry. He came forward to explain. 

The team, with Tane as its captain, wished to use our land called Nuutari as a football field.

The boys would clear and level two acres as their play space, and also cut down the tangle of purao and guava trees on the rest of the plantation in payment. There was an open space in the middle of Nuutari so that ten palms only would have to be sacrificed in the name of sport. 

Harry promised an answer by the following night, so the "boys" went to the Chinese store next door to indulge in their usual round of singing and dancing on the porch. Their thumping, playing and singing has become such a part of our evening now, that we scarcely know how we would go to sleep without it. There is just the right distance to lend enchantment to it. 

The next night the team cheered and excitement ran high when Harry gave permission. There was no dance that evening, for the football theme was too thrilling. There was endless talk. Tane made a speech of thanks. All shook hands with us over and over. When might they begin work? 

Any time. Tomorrow morning they would begin.

They milled about our yard, our patio and living room till we went to bed, then they retreated a distance down the road, still cheering and making plans for their great venture on the morrow. 

At dawn they were gathering. Not only the team, but old men, women and children! They came with bunches of fei, bananas, strings of fish, chickens and young pigs slung on carrying poles. 

Men brought axes, scythes and slasher knives for work, but it was to be a gala occasion, too, crowned with a feast.

Women, babies, pigs and fish stopped at the policeman's house. A pit was dug in the yard. 

It was lined with rocks, then a fire built in it. 

When the stones were sizzling hot, the coals were raked away. Fish wrapped in leaves, dressed pigs and chickens and packages of poi in leaf coverings were put into the oven. Hundreds of fei were scattered over and around the other foods. A covering of banana leaves went on next to hold the heat and keep the food clean. Earth was heaped over and the fireless cooker was working.

The men, 70 in all, went at the work of clearing in a hilarious mood. Tane had organized his groups the night before, with a foreman over each work team. First they must cut the ten palm trees. With the trees down, there was no danger that the white man would change his mind. Axes flashed, men sweated and rivalry was keen. When the first palm crashed to the ground there was wild cheering, and re-doubled efforts from the other teams. 

They chopped the trunks into 15 foot lengths and left them to dry. Old men and boys had been hacking at weeds with their long knives. 

Strands of purau and guava were falling. Children were pulling grass. The place was alive with workers. 

At eleven o'clock the feast was served and we were invited. 

For three days enthusiasm was at a high pitch, and every day they feasted. The fourth day was

Saturday, so all work stopped while men went to the mountains for fei or to the lagoon for fish. 

When Monday rolled around there were some fifty workers back at the field, but no feast. Most of them went home for dinner and did not return. 

Tuesday brought 25 men, Wednesday 16 and 

Thursday only a handful. On Friday there was 

Tane and the four faithful helpers who attend him when he is called as a doctor. Tane was low in spirits for the work was not finished. He and 

Taihai came to Harry for help. 

They discussed ways and means. How to get the crowd back to work? They hit upon the idea of suits and football shoes. They would be a novelty for Tahiti. Tane was sure the idea would dazzle the boys into activity again. We found pictures of suits and shoes in the catalog of an American mail order house. Tane craftily held back the pictures until Sunday evening when the boys were gathered at the store for dancing, so their newly-aroused enthusiasm wouldn't melt away before Monday.

It worked. Thirty men sweated all day in dense clouds of smoke, for they had reached the stage of burning the piles of half-dried trash. 

Freshly bathed, and in their best whites, they came to Harry that evening to be measured for their suits. Tane had wisely held back the shoe idea to use later when another spur was needed. 

Harry donated a notebook. Each man came to our table in turn and wrote his name. It was a big effort for most of them. Tongues stuck in

cheeks. Faces were distorted and bare toes clenched the bamboo floor. Most of them never wrote with ease in school, and these boys had scarcely touched a pencil since they finished their required education at 13.

Tane and Taihai were busy measuring shoulders and backs with strips of purao bark. Without inches or meters marked on the bark it's a mystery how they figured, but each man was told his size and it was marked in the notebook. 

Every night that week they came for further measurements and to look at the pictures in the catalog. They would group six deep over the book and turn the pages slowly, struck silent by most of the wonders they saw, but growing voluble over guitars, bicycles and motorcycles. 

Others who couldn't get a look-in at the catalog, we entertained with colored funny papers from 

America. They would spread one comic full size on the table and surround it. Those who saw it upside down enjoyed it just as much as the others. 

That week the clearing of the field went on, but workers dropped off every day. On Sunday night

Tane played his other big card, suggesting shoes, real football shoes, with spikes! The next week we added foot measurements to our notbook, and saw the middle of Nuutari free of the last stone, well leveled, goal posts set up and palm log seats arranged. 

Harry made the boys a present of a shiny pigskin, and the opening game of the season was announced for Sunday afternoon.

"When do they keep their promise about clearing the rest of the plantation?" Harry inquired of Tane. Tane drew a long face, forgot all his 

English when he couldn't make the right answer, and poured out Tahitian which he knew we couldn't understand. The other work was never done. "How are all the suits and shoes to be paid for, Tane?" "We sell wood. Plenty trees go. Plenty 'moni' come." And Tane beamed for he knew the answer this time. They were going to sell our wood to buy their suits. We couldn't help laughing, and 

Harry good-naturedly gave permission. They cut the wood and put it in neat bundles ready to send to Papeete. But that plan failed, for one "boy" after another began carrying bundles home till it disappeared.

Their next plan was for Harry to buy the suits and shoes as a gift to the team. They would only cost $130 and was he not a rich American? When that didn't get results, we should advance the money and get it back by charging a franc admission to the games until we were reimbursed. "How are we to charge a gate fee when there is no gate nor fence?" That problem was beyond them. Suits and shoes were never ordered.

Sunday afternoon brought a rumor over the coconut radio that the governor of the island was to come and 'kick off' for the first game. Excitement was terrific. Friends, sweethearts, babies and dogs turned out in full force. There were two

or three hundred onlookers. The teams seemed entirely happy, barefooted and stripped to shorts or loin cloths. Tane confided to us that suits and shoes would be much to cumbersome and hot. 

The governor didn't come, but the chief of the district did. A dozen Chinese storekeepers arrived in their two-wheeled carts selling donuts, cake, buttered bread and slices of watermelon.

The policeman appeared with a five gallon freezer of ice cream. It had come out from Papeete on the truck and was on the verge of collapse, but still popular. The policeman put it into cones with a teaspoon. Fifty centimes. Bottled pop and peanuts were favorites in a big way. Everything to suit the tastes of an American circus crowd except the hot dogs and the spun sugar. 

The game they played was not intercollegiate football, nor rugby, nor soccer. It combined points of all three and had some Tahitian quirks not common elsewhere. They kicked the ball with shins and thighs, bounced it off their heads and turned back to the ball and butted with their hips, but it was all "football" to them.

There was daily practice for two weeks and several Sunday games, but enthusiasm waned. At the end of three months the field was hiding under a covering of grass. Soon it was deserted. Harry turned the cows in and they had good pasturage. 

We heard nothing more about football.

SAVED FROM THE CURSE OF TUPAPAU! 

Three-layer farming. Our man Friday and Saturday. Full moon good for cropsand thieves. A buckskin blond makes things easier. 

When we decided to stay longer in Tahiti we planned to fix up a bit. The bamboo floor of our living room had worn threadbare. We hadn't known at first to insist on old bamboo, and the little bugs which consider green bamboo a delicacy, had feasted on it. Our thatched roof was looking moth-eaten so we had another put on for $2. We enlarged the woodshed, bought another length of pipe and brought water to the kitchen door. Harry made a rock and cement basin on the ground to catch the water, in lieu of a sink. 

A piece of hollow bamboo, under the ground, carried waste water to a distance. With the spigot placed shoulder high, it became our shower as well as our kitchen tap.

Harry helped Taihai build a hut on our land and gave him the guardianship of the big valley.

Taihai was proud of his authority. Especially because his "paper" was typewritten. The biggest piece of work we planned was the building of a copra rack. We employed Taihai regularly and called him Friday, so when we took on another worker, he became 'Saturday'.

Came the day when Harry, Friday and Saturday began work on the copra dryer which was to be near the football field. Harry had noticed some large blocks of black rock piled in a square, flanked on either side by wings of smaller rocks. 

Good material for a foundation. At a little distance stood an old mara tree and behind it another square of stones. Both rock platforms were overgrown with brush and trees which had thrust roots among the stones.

Harry left Friday and Saturday to get out certain blocks which he had marked with chalk while he went off to other work. Returning in an hour Harry found one stone dislodged, two frightened workers and a group of natives arguing excitedly. Friday tried to tell Harry the trouble, but lost most of his English in his excitement. 

However, Harry understood there was some danger connected with the two piles of stones. 

There are no poisonous snakes on the island, no lions or tigers, nothing larger than a stray pig could be near. When Harry sat down on the nearest block, a dozen hands grabbed at him and pulled him away.

A boy was dispatched to bring Papa Poria, the witch doctor, who came as fast as his elephan-

SAVED FROM THE CURSE OF TAPAPAU! 203 

tiasis would allow. He made a long speech to the crowd. Friday and Saturday reverently replaced the stone they had moved. The chief arrived, harangued the people and added unintelligible warnings. A boy was sent on a run to fetch the school teacher who spoke a little English. School was dismissed and Shura, the teacher, arrived panting, preceded by the entire school. 

Everybody tried to tell her the trouble. "Ghosts," said Shura. "Ghosts?" echoed Harry. "Tahitian ghosts. Spirits. Tupapau.' Harry added "Tupapau' to his vocabulary. "Don't move a stone, Harry. You die."

For the next hour she told Harry of the dreadful consequences if he moved any stone from either pile. There would be illness, misfortune in many forms, death to every member of our family, or worse, the living death, Leprosy! 

Ancient Tahitian curses would fall thick and fast, brought down on us by the Tupapau of the dead buried under the stones.

Harry said he needed the stones for the foundation of the copra rack. When this was relayed to the chief he ordered young men to scout about and bring other suitable rocks. They obeyed eagerly, believing that they would avert a thousand ills from themselves and all of Tahiti. For the curses and plagues would not stop with our family, but would haunt friends, neighbors, all of Vairao, our descendants and theirs. After a long pow wow, and another speech by the witch doctor,

they believed they had calmed any spirits who had been disturbed by the moving of that first stone, and the crowd melted away. 

Later we got a full story of the stone blocks. 

I don't vouch for the truth of it: Back in the early days of the white occupation of the island, while King Pomare the Second still had power, he visited Vairao and admired the fresh beauty of this part of the island. Later he sent an emissary to notify people of this district that he was well pleased with the region and would return in two moons for a prolonged visit. That meant feasting, dancing and all honor to the king.

The two month's notice was well given for the preparations would keep all hands busy. Homes on the land which the king had admired must be removed, the people giving up their land at the whim of their sovereign. Special buildings were erected for the feast: a house for the king and his consort, another for the princesses and their attendants, one for the young prince and his fellows, pavilions for feasting, for dancing, for performances by clowns, jugglers and players who would come to provide entertainment. These buildings would be freshly decorated with flowers and colored leaves each day of the royal visit.

Great quantities of fish were taken in nets and kept alive in cages in the sea. Pigs and puppies were fattened. The place swarmed with workers and activity was feverish.

The crowned heads arrived in houses built across war canoes and were installed in their

SAVED FROM THE CURSE OF TAPAPAU! 205 

temporary homes. The entertainment lasted for as many days as the king was pleased to remain. 

The land which he admired as he strolled about, became his forever and was tabu to all others. 

When the king left, his priests remained in Vairao and set up altars for the worship of the pagan gods on the royal land. No one dared step on this land without the permission of the priests. 

Where the land extended to the beach, the poorer classes must get around it by canoe. Friends of the king only might walk on the trail. Any dog or pig which strayed on the land was seized as a sacrifice by the priests, the blood being offered to the god, the meat, well roasted, to the priests themselves. Human trespassers, even little children, paid for their folly with their lives. But there weren't many fool-hardy enough to go near for the priests taught that the curse of leprosy would be the penalty. At the priest's orders, the fruit and fei from the valley were gathered by slaves and given to the royal family and the priests. Lower classes were invited on the land to attend religious services several times a year when they must use the greatest care not to step over certain boundaries. At these times they must lower their clothing to the waist to show proper respect for the king and the gods.

Years passed, and the French who were getting a strong hold on the island persuaded King 

Pomare toward Christianity. He, in turn, tried to convert his subjects. The people of Vairao and other outlying districts, stubbornly clung to

their old gods, represented by stone images, called "Tiki'. The king ordered his priests to overthrow the idols, but the people and priests rebelled. 

The king came, with a guard of soldiers, and removed a Tiki from the altar. He put it in a cook house to disgrace the god and show the people what little respect he had for the gods of their forefathers. Thoroughly aroused, the people gathered in fighting array. The king sent a chief with a regiment of warriors to teach the rebels a lesson. They met in battle on the royal land.
