Many were killed on each side but the king's men were successful in their purpose of carrying away the stone Tiki which was sunk in the depths of the lagoon.

The story hints that the victors feasted on the flesh of their enemies and scattered the bones to the dogs. Their brothers-in-arms were buried near the battlefield and a great square of stone blocks was erected over their bodies and weapons. 

The general, who had fallen while fighting, was buried alone, with his battle axes and spears about him, and a memorial mound of blocks flanked with two wings of smaller stones was built over his body. Then a young mara tree, symbol of eternity, was planted between the two monuments. 

Years passed by and Christianity spread. Many another Tiki was overthrown. A few idols still stand in isolated places but most of them are in museums. The royal land passed from father to son. Pieces were sold at different times. A portion was given for a chief's house. Later the

SAVED FROM THE CURSE OF TAPAPAU! 207 

house was removed and a school, civic house and teacherage now occupy the beach end of the crown land. Our two plantations and the great valley form the other end of the same land. 

The present football field was the old battle ground and the name, 'Nuuteri' or 'regiment with a purpose' is explained. Harry and I were tempted to dig around a bit to see if we could unearth a war club, but gave it up remembering the hubbub which followed the moving of one stone block. Every illness and death in Vairao for the next decade would be blamed on us if we dared stir up the Tupapau of the old general. 

With the plantations pretty well cleared, Harry turned to the valley, where the wilderness was 'thicker' than below, for most of that land had never been shorn of its original jungle. The foot-path up the valley crossed and re-crossed the stream, Vaipoe. In many places the valley is a canyon, but here and there are level places of one, two or three acres suitable for crops. 

There were 500 coffee plants on one 'flat'. 

Another had been planted to vanilla. This we gave to Taihai. There were bananas here and there in patches, tangles of wild guavas and mangoes, some limes and oranges, and farther up the valley were fei by the thousand.

Harry chose one flat for watermelons. Friday worked with him for a share in the crop and Saturday worked by the day when he felt like it. A native family wanted to make charcoal so we let them use the wood free to get it cut and

cleared away.

The smaller stuff, not suitable for charcoal, Friday and Saturday cut with their slasher knives. They always use knives in preference to axes, and it's amazing what large trees they chop down with knives. There was brush burning and weed pulling for several weeks. 

All hand-work. Harry was ready to plant, but 

Taihai begged so desperately for him to wait till full moon, that Harry gave in. "Dark moon no good, Harry. No crop come."

Plantings, fishing parties, weddings and all sorts of things are determined by the moon here, according to ancient superstitions. The morning that Harry did plant, Taihai stood and prayed in Tahitian, 'long and loud', to some ancient god of pagan times.

The vines needed constant care and weeding but the time came when we could see melons swelling. Just before they were ready to pick they began to disappear. Every night during full moon, they went by the half dozens, although they were three miles from the road and the nearest house. The gangs of boys who parade about all night, every full moon, didn't find the six mile walk too much when the reward was stolen watermelon. By the time we found out who was getting them, and Harry stayed all night to guard the patch, the moon waned and the boys slept at night. It's just another old Tahitian custom to wander under the palms, till dawn, when the moon invites to mischief or love.

Taihai brought melons down and set up a little

Taihai and Saturday just returned from a mountain trip with their loads of fei. Taihai (Friday) is just in from the trail. Saturday is untying his load. The framework is for a new cooking shack that Taihai is making. Our two-wheeled cart, the ex-breadwagon, rests in the sunshine, having a day off. In the extreme left foreground is the latest watermelon patch trying to survive a drouth. The great mountain and valley in the distance is part of our land.

table next door where he sold them to the dancing crowd on the store porch. A franc a slice. We sent some in to Papeete where our little Chinaman sold them at his store. After all expenses had been paid and Taihai had received his share, there was a net profit of $33. Not much. But enough to buy a horse and cart.

The little island horses sell from $9 to $15. We got a buckskin blond with zebra stripes on her forelegs and a darling black stripe from her mane to her tail. A plume of a tail which swept the ground. Zebra turned out to be a good worker on the plantation, but was deathly afraid of bridges, so it was difficult to ride or drive her far on the road. Spurs and whips meant nothing when she made up her mind not to cross a bridge. 

There was nothing to do but humble ourselves, get down and lead her over.

Zebra made many patient trips from the store to the melon patch, walking carefully with two sacks fastened across her back, with four ripe melons in each. But Harry wasn't satisfied with that, so he felled trees, widened the trail, bridged a stream and made the first wagon road through the wilderness. Pioneer stuff! 

The next purchase was a cart, an ex-breaddelivery cart, like all the other little two-wheelers on the island. They're popular here because they can go on rough roads, on the plantations where there are no roads, and because carts are taxed by the wheel. Two wheels cost only half as much as four. Harry collected a set of harness from

the four ends of nowhere. A neighbor gave him some pieces from an old harness; Harry used rope and tire casings for other parts, sewing them together with purau fibre. The traces were parts of a broken chain. One day I saw his leather belt getting sewed into the outfit. 

After we got the cart we didn't know how we had lived without it before. It hauled our coconuts to the rack, and hauled our copra to the Chinaman. It took us to Taravao, a sixteen mile drive, to get Parcel Post packages from the Post Office. 

Think of that next time the postman brings your packages to the door. It took us on picnic trips on holidays. It transported pigs, calves and coconut husks for fuel for the whole neighborhood. The natives borrowed it to peddle fish along the road and it took white ladies on afternoon calls to distant friends. The latest wrinkle here is to mount your cart on auto wheels with balloon tires. Makes very smooth sailing, but our cart is not so up-to-date as that. 'Tis a proud brown boy who can fetch his girl to a rooster fight in one of those! 

Melons came down by the cartload until a fiveweek drouth came right in the middle of the rainy season and shriveled the vines. That was that until next melon season. But the land was cleared. Good cow feed came up, and papayas grew by the thousand. They spring up whereever land is cleared. The seeds are in the ground waiting for their turn at a place in the sun. 

Harry let about five hundred trees grow, to add

SAVED FROM THE CURSE OF TAPAPAU! 213 

to the cow feed and supply our table. 

Both our cows had "got balloon" as Taihai expressed it, and in due course of time presented us with their offspring, one calf we gave to 

Peggy and Polly, who promptly christened her with both their names combined into Poggy. 

It was a source of constant amazement to the natives to see Harry milk the cows and feed the calves from a bucket. It is the island custom to let the calf suck while the cow is being milked, with the result that the cow refuses to let her milk down if the calf isn't handy to butt a little.

Under that system the calf is called in to help at the milking until it is full grown. Natives have never heard of weaning at three months.

They think the calf needs the milk until it becomes a mother or father itself, or until it is butchered. 

They let the calf run with the mother all day, then pen it up at night so as to have a little milk in the morning if they bother to milk at all.

Under such treatment the cows never develop their full milking possibilities, but no one worries for children here prefer their coffee. Most natives take a little milk as medicine when ill, but rather scorn it at other times. COWS.

The brown brothers are mortally afraid of 

They would rather break a wild horse from the mountains than train a cow to milk. 

They do well at breaking horses, bareback, with a bridle of purau bark, but they will run from a frisky calf, and use chains, ropes and hobbles on a comparatively gentle cow.

We soon had a small but steady income from the sale of milk. We exchanged milk for honey, coffee and fish. We had all the drinking milk, cottage cheese and milk puddings we could tuck away. But we found butter making all wrong without ice. Cream wouldn't rise and mold grew too fast.

Harry had bought a couple of little pigs when we first got the land. They became proud parents and raised four little sows for us . . . "pigs is pigs" and pretty soon we had twenty little piglets squealing around. Harry built a rock wall across a narrow part of our valley, fenced the stream and turned the pigs loose. He had to fence a few other places, but most of the valley walls were so steep that the pigs were secure in a pen ten or more miles long. They throve well for a time on nuts, water grass and api, a kind of wild taro, added to the tarua which Harry fed them every day.

Harry brought the sows down to the plantation to litter. Many of the mothers had a careless habit of lying down on their babies and killed half of them, but the survivors were turned up in the valley when they were three months old. Dogs killed a few, some were stolen to grace native festivals and five were buried under a landslide, but some lived and got as fat as little pigs should.

Our herd reached a peak of one hundred and five, but we weren't to get rich on pigs, it seems, for when everything looked rosy they began to die mysteriously. A pig would turn purple along

SAVED FROM THE CURSE OF TAPAPAU! 215 

his sides, gasp for breath and within a few hours pass on into the great beyond for pigs. We tried to doctor them but couldn't find the cause of death. So we gave up, hurried the remaining pigs to the market while they were still healthy, and sold all. Taihai thinks they discovered and ate a certain poisonous plant. 

While the pig herd had been increasing Harry had bought a plow and cultivator, (more wonders to the natives) and patiently taught Zebra how to plow. Taihai was the proudest man on the island when he could follow the plow with Tahitians looking on enviously. They all wanted a try at the new game. The rest of Nuuteri was soon cleared. Harry planted 7,500 tarua stalks so as to have plenty of pig feed. At the time he sold the last porker, he had a bumper crop under the ground. It was and is the largest tarua plantation on the island. Well, thank goodness, tarua is something that human beings can eat, it being an imitation of an Irish potato, so we didn't lose on it, although the pigs were gone.

We sold the tubers and the tops, 17 tons in all, getting in a net profit of $125. 

Once the land was cleared and plowed Harry decided to keep tarua coming on, so as he digs the fruit from one section, he re-plants young stalks. There is a constant demand for it in 

Papeete, and much is shipped to the low islands where the coral soil will not support anying but coco trees. There are troubles, but what farmer hasn't his trials? Several weeks of drouth will

put back the growth. Pigs get in and raise havoc with the tubers and tender shoots. Natives steal a little, but on the whole it is an easy crop to raise. The big leaves of the plant, head-high, shade the ground so that few weeds grow, but water grass thrives under it and between the rows, and makes excellent cow feed. So we have a coconut crop above ground, a tarua crop underground and cows on the same land. Three-layer farming!

Harry and Cream Puff cultivating the taro and tarua, the plants with the big leaves like 'elephant ear' at home. Take a good look at the cultivator. It's the only one for miles and miles. The harness is made up of all sorts of odds and ends. Coconuts, wearing their big outer husks, have fallen right into the picture from the trees above.

Adolescents in Tahiti are a problem. Peggy has a strong gravity. Their first "smooth" 

They begin to "plump"-and write their memoirs. ride. 

Author's note to reader: "Dear reader, I did not mean to write such a spasm about our school work, but I am so interested in the unit as outlined here I couldn't say it in fewer pages." 

When we decided to stay longer in Tahiti I outlined a course of study for the twins on a four-year plan. It would consist mainly of world history with a background of geography. 

Four years . . time enough for a leisurely progress down the ages. We would begin a few million years ago or so, make the sun of gasses, nebula, star dust and burning material. When it got to revolving at a terrific pace, we would watch the little spark of our world flung out of it, 93 million miles into space, see the world start on its own little bonfire career through immensity, hear it hiss and sizzle and watch it cool slowly as the clouds poured rain on it. We would leave it steaming and shrinking for centuries while we went on

to the earth's eight sister planets and got them built and started, and the moon running around properly on its business. Then back to earth we would skip and see how it was getting along without our personal attention. If everything is going well at that stage we may work out a few comets and constellations before we settle back to earth permanently.

Then we will watch our old globe through the barren ages and when it is ready for the beginnings of life we would see a tiny, one-celled creature come to life in the warm shallows of some salty pool, watch it make its own food from the sunshine on the green matter of its body; see a crease form 'round its middle till it divides into two cells and starts the making of millions upon millions of other tiny mites that represented ‘life' for eons of time.

Then we will see those single one-celled animals of green matter change to the creatures that are half green and half white, and we will struggle with the white ones, who can't make their own food, but catch the green ones who can. 

We will thrill over the first plants that learn to put part of their cells in the ground to bring up water, leaving part out in the sunshine to produce food by combining sunlight, chlorophyl and water; see them change and develop through the centuries to real plants and ferns and trees of various types; watch the two-celled mites grow tails to swim with and become jelly fish, and lament with the sponge that he has such a long road to travel

before he will ever get legs, arms or a brain. Then we will rejoice with the mites who spread wings like insects or birds, and also with those who raise legs, tails and scales, and become lizards and other crawling creatures of the great forests of ferns.

We will see the fern forests become layers of coal that were carefully carefully hidden away for thousands of years before man discovered its uses, and Uncle Hank began buying and selling it. 

Then we will suffer with the lesser creatures during the age of the terrible monsters, the dinosaurs, thunder lizards, double-beams and brontasaurs, till we kill off the twenty-ton monsters in favor of the mammals, and see those in turn develop through the thousands of years, through the ice ages, till we produce the distant forerunner of man.

We will watch that poor animal struggle for more ages, through disease, cold, dirt and famine till it begins to resemble a cave man in a distant way.

Then we will watch it creep up through more terrible ages, battling flesh-eating animals, seeking security in the tree tops, escaping forest fires, shivering through bitter winters till at last it emerges out of the past a really, truly cave man.

We will watch the cave man scratch pictures of the animals he hunts on his cave walls, for his evening's entertainment. He will capture his wife by the 'knock-down and haul-in' system. Then he will begin to chip stone for arrow points, and then

he will die and leave his skull for eminent modern scientists to dispute over.

Next we will see the cave man's children live a shade better than he, with sharper spear heads, ⚫ and a bright lad in the clan will discover that a bit of the forest fire can be brought into the cave without causing death, and then there will be more comfort all around and the babies won't die off quite so quickly during the first snow storm. 

Centuries will pass. and another genius will discover how to make fire by rubbing sticks together, so when the cave women carelessly let the fire go out, the coming blizzard need not finish the family off.

Then a few landslides in the mountains will seal up a half dozen caves so that the people in them, their weapons, the bones of animals they had been gnawing and all their paraphanelia will be preserved intact for the curious people of today who go to museums.

We will watch some of the cave people spread along the rivers and build huts where they cannot find caves, and the lake-dwellers will be smart enough to hoist their hovels high on piles to save themselves from less 'civilized men' and from animals that think man a very toothsome morsel.

Then the lake and river people will invent shell money, presaging the time when too many people will put money-getting before decency and honor.

Then the wild hunters of the forest will discover that some animals can be tamed to help carry man and his burdens, and the wild cows will learn to

stand and be milked, and, slowly, the hunters will change to herders with flocks of sheep to supply them with food and clothing. 

More ages will pass and the herders will learn many things as they move about with their flocks. 

At night they will notice the stars and name some of them, and begin the first crude study of astronomy.

Then we will witness an incident that marks another important change in the survival of man: 

A woman picking the heads of the wild wheat that grows on the graves of the dead, noticing that it is thicker where the warriors are buried, remembering that a handful of grain was put in the graves for whatever use it might have in the warrior's future.

With that discovery we will hear the woman argue: "Why not put a handful of grain by the tent door, then when it comes up thick and close I will not have to walk so far seeking the scattered grains of wild grasses." We will see the results of that first planting so successful that others are quick to try it, and the herders will quit roving and become the first settled farmers of the world. 

Next we will settle those farmers in the valleys of the Tigris and the Euphrates in Asia, and along the Nile in Egypt, where they will enjoy the gentle yearly floods that bring rich loam to fertilize their grain. The Egyptians will tame the barley, oats, corn and cotton, learn to weave cloth of cotton, and linen, and use the fleece of sheep, too.

1

In their anxiety about the yearly floods the Egyptians will learn to measure the water that means life to their crops. In their effort to time the floods and fix the date when crops will be ready to harvest, they will learn to count days and years and make a calendar. 

Their priests will seal their king in a mighty stone mountain, called a pyramid, with his horse, his hound, his wives and slaves, his butcher and baker, his priest and candlestick-maker, so he will be well attended in his future life. 

The trinkets and jewels that are sealed away with him will lie quietly for thousands of years till the tomb is opened by impertinent moderns and the trappings of a king are imitated in ten cent store jewelry, and gas stations are built in imitation of his tomb.

Then we will travel with the Israelites from Ur to the promised land, sail the Mediterranean with the greatest of ancient traders, the Phonecians. 

With them we will discover the secret of making glass, and learn how their coppersmiths make intricate ornaments.

We will go with them to England still the haunt of savage people and get tin, the metal that makes copper into enduring bronze, its possession making rulers of the world, because bronze spearheads and arrow points kill quicker than stone or copper.

We will risk our lives with these traders in tiny boats on the stormy Atlantic going to Norway and Sweden for the drops of petrified pine

sap that we call amber. We will guard the precious stuff against pirates on the seas and robbers on land till we get back to Egypt and Syria where kings will pay for it in shining gold, and pay heavily, for they desire its beauty and color in their crowns and want it near for they believe it will ward off evil spirits, starting a superstition that still exists. Many people today believe that amber worn 'round the neck will prevent goiter.

We will watch Greece rise in a greater civilization, see it weaken and fall as Rome becomes powerful. Then we will fight with Roman legions in Britain, Algeria, Gaul and through the Balkans, pushing the great Empire of Rome farther and farther out from Italy till its boundaries almost encircle the known world of that time, and we will see Rome decline, luxury, greed and treachery destroy in a few short years what it took decades to build. Then we will witness the beginnings of the present European nations, before they are caught in the gloom of the dark ages.

We will see Venice come to the front, become 

Mistress of the Mediterranean with her trading galleys: see her plying the seas bringing wealth to princes, kings and merchants. We will see 

Venice fall, too, and Genoa rise in all her glory.

Tramping with the three Polos we will scale the great wall of China, and be the first white men and women ever to step foot in that sacred empire. By great luck we will pass through unhurt to the throne of the great Ghengis Kahn, and

be the favorites of an Emperor for years.

Returning slowly to Italy we will carry the ransom of a dozen kings in our money belts, but disguised as ragged beggars. Then we will tell the home folks how silks and brocades are made, tell them stories of wealth that will start merchants and great caravans across the desert to make that wealth theirs. Some will be slain by robbers, some die of thirst, but some will get through to establish the great caravan routes of the Orient that connect the Western and Eastern worlds for the next three hundred years.

Then we will sail with Vasco de Gama 'round the stormy cape of South Africa for the first time and connect the West with the East again by water, and we will try with Columbus to find the short way through Asia, only to haul up short on the shores of America.

We will fight with Cortez in Mexico, explore with Coronado in the Southwest, and see French, Dutch and English settle on the East coast of the new land. We will hear the war whoops of Indian tribes trying vainly to stop the westward advance of the white man. 

Driving in covered wagons, wading fords, crossing rivers in flatboats, plodding across prairies and struggling over mountains we will finally reach 'Californey' and her gold diggings. We will send more Indians to the Happy Hunting Grounds, push the rest into reservations, and take the whole expanse of land from the Atlantic to the Pacific for our own.

Then we will people the new land with a few more pioneers, allow swarms of Europeans to come in, take land, build railroads, tunnel mountains, cut prairies into wheat farms, build cities, and dot the peaceful landscape with noisy factories, rumbling trucks, automobiles and shrieking sirens.

Finally, we will dig under the cities, put down subways, start roaring trains, fill the air with planes, see traffic take such a toll of lives and political and economic systems so distort the truth, that we will flee to a South Sea island for peace, and there in the quiet of our niao hut we will study history in detail. 

The twin's interest in it all is at white heat, and when they have studied this broad outline and written in all of the detail, I believe they will have a history book of their own making that will give them a wholly satisfying picture of the history of the world. 

But hear the girls' own reactions to their lessons as told in letters written when we first began our long climb up the staircase of time: "We love our lessons very much," Peggy writes, "arithmetic, reading and long ago stories. We know a great deal about soil, snow, rain, clouds, stars, space and other planets. "We know about other countries around the world because when mother told us about travelers we didn't know where they went so she showed us on the maps. We would rather hear about that from mother than from books. We know all about

the stars. Mother told me when she was my age she didn't know about them, not until she went to college. "The moon has only a little gravity. If I were in the moon and a little breeze came, it might float my hair. I must wear a playsuit or my dress might float up to my head. But there wouldn't be a breeze because there is no air on the moon. 

If I washed the dishes, when I dropped one, it would fall slowly and I could catch it before it reached the floor, because of only a little gravity. 

I could lift a kitty by one of his hairs and carry him around, he would be so light. I would drop him slowly until he turned over and landed gently. 

I'd like to go there but it isn't possible because 

I can't fly."

Polly's interest ran to the sun and then down to earth: "Peggy and I learned about our mother sun and her nine planet children. We are in the sixth and seventh grades, I guess. Some of our work is in college. Soon after the earth was made it began cooling off. That was millions and millions of years ago. "The earth was more brilliant than our brightest day for it had just stopped burning! For years the heat was so terrific that clouds could not go near the earth. The colors went from golden to scarlet and red then red-purple, blackpurple and then inky because the thick clouds covered the world and shut it away from the sun. "The earth got cooler and all the fires were out but then volcanoes bursted! The rocks and

ground began to tremble and quake. Ashes, gasses and lava flew out so terrifically that it might have killed people, only there weren't any people, no animals, snails or birds or worms. No one was alive and mother nature was asleep!"

Books for our evening reading would be historical novels, to give color and thrill to the pageant of the past. "Story of Ab" to show the cave days; "Ben Hur", to tell the story of the gentle Christ; "The Sisters", "The Little Queen", "The Last 

Days of Pompeii", and "Quo Vadis" to tell of 

Cleopatra, doomed Rome, the might of Vesuvius, Nero's cruelty to the Christian martyrs. 

Tennyson, Chaucer, Shakespeare, Scott, Reade, Dickens, Thackeray, Meredith and Eliot would teach us of high ideals and noble endeavor. 

The girls should also have books all boys and girls enjoy: the Alcott books, "Tom Sawyer and 

Huckleberry Finn", "Gulliver's Travels", "Swiss 

Family Robinson", "Arabian Nights", "Robinson

Crusoe". We had brought some of them along. 

Others were borrowed from the one circulating 

English library in a Papeete barber shop. And 

God bless the gandmothers! They sent us two or three books on each mail steamer. Our box cupboard is filled to overflowing but we can always add more boxes.

The girls drew diagrams, maps and cartoons and painted pictures to illustrate their stories. 

Polly wrote grandmother: "When mother thinks we are reading too much and taxing our eyes, we go to the plantation and work. But we are not

complaining for we are dreaming about the Greek heroes, the gods and lovely goddesses and we imagine ourselves with them."

So for a time, Tahiti was Mount Olympus, or we were noble Romans in flowing togas, or kings and queens of medieval Europe. Just now we are colonists, subduing the wilderness and Indians. 

We suffer with the Pilgrims at Plymouth in the daytime (when we help clear the jungle) but in the evening we rest and enjoy the story of John Alden and Priscilla. We are marching steadily up from the dim past to the present. 

However, we don't live entirely in the past.

We take current magazines and get a daily radio news sheet. Polly wrote recently: "I am well satisfied to hear that America is going to make the Panama canal bomb-proof so Europe or Japan cannot stop my country from moving its navy quickly in case of war. I saw a cartoon where death was drawn as a skeleton, wearing a tin hat. He was beating a drum which rattled. "Prepare, prepare".

Two white girls far removed from their own kind! How will these years in Tahiti affect their lives? They have developed a new scale of values here. How will these values change when they return to America? When they came here they were just entering adolesence often a trying time under the best of circumstances. Are we making this period easier or more difficult for them by transplanting them to a simpler life?

Peggy and Polly believe they are better off here

than in the whirl of American civilization. Are they old enough to judge? Or is their attitude simply a reflection of Harry's and mine? In 

Tahiti they have gained a father and mother in a setting of home life, but they have lost the companionship of white boys and girls. 

Peggy writes: "I have two girl friends. Marsaline is a Rurutu girl and Marie is half French and half Tahitian girl. Marie is very beautiful and has light brown skin, like me. She is a little taller than Polly and me. Marsaline has dark brown hair and skin, and is a little shorter than 

Polly and me. I love Marie better than Marsaline because we are nearly the same size. We always play with each other. We taught them a little 

English and they taught us a little French. Mother says they have beautiful manners.

Shura, Marie's big sister is the teacher in the 

Vairao school. Polly and I love her very dearly. 

Her father was born in France. Her mother was from the island of Rurutu. When Marie goes back to school in Papeete, we will miss her. Then 

I will love Marsaline more. When Marsaline goes away to school, then my two companions will be mother and Polly, but I am happy here.

In the afternoons Polly and I go to the hillside and play among the old dead trees which have been cut down. Sometimes we sit there comfortably and talk about our "far-away land," friends and family. Sometimes we play on the coconut plantation, or get some corn leaves for the cows. 

As we carry them, the big cow comes walking

fast to get them. Polly drops hers as I slip around to give my leaves to the small cow. The big cow is selfish and tries to drive the little one away. 

Sometimes I ride horseback, but my horse, Zebra, hates Polly and me and wants to bite us.

We don't pay any heed to her and get on her back. 

She likes mother and daddy, but I don't know why she doesn't care for me. 

Polly and I ate ice cream four times in two years: once last year and three times this year.

When we return to America, first of all we will go to a drug store and buy ice cream for our taste. Ice cream in Tahiti is very different from 

American ice cream. It is soft and tastes of coconut." 

Polly wrote to grandmother: "Sometimes I want to go swimming but Peggy doesn't want to go, so I go alone, and then Peggy changes her mind and wants to swim with me. Or if she wants me to walk with her up in the plantation and I refuse, and she walks away alone, it is as if she has a strong gravity to pull me along with her, so I follow wherever she pleases to go. Peggy and I became "friend-sisters" better than before.

In America we quarreled and liked each other pretty well, but we have changed and wonder how we are such good friends. So I asked Peggy, “did you hypnotize me to make me like you so very much?" She would only say, "No, but you and I are both without other white children, and we are together so much of the time."

Sometimes Peggy and I climb up in an old dead tree. We sit there in the sunshine very quietly, and there is not a sound until our skins get pretty hot. Then we go swimming, and race and play tricks in the lagoon. We had a bright new imagination that people never swam just like us before. 

We return home and take a bath with a great deal of cold water in our shower. We hardly ever have plenty of hot water-four or five times in two years, but we don't care. Mostly we use hot water for our faces.

When we ride Zebra she is just as lazy as a shark, for the shark loves to float at the surface with the sun shining on his back. I would like to have one of the horses who loves to gallop fast. Daddy borrowed another horse for awhile.

I noticed that he was sturdy and much stronger and peppier than Zebra. We named him Cockeyelo-co for he was a bit crosseyed.

I begin to realize about my future in America, and I wonder if I will be unhappy there. I notice that many people become unhappy when they leave Tahiti. There is a rich man who has traveled around the world many times. Whenever he leaves Tahiti he always wants to come back. Tahiti is a siren with a magnificent magnet to pull people. It is difficult to fly from the beautiful siren, the Polynesian islands. We planned that if our "someday future" husbands couldn't get any money in America, then we will come back to beloved Tahiti. It's a very easy life down here.

Grandma, please save my letters of the past and future so I can have the pleasure and considerable enjoyment of reading them in the future, just to remember my experiences. I will laugh over the silly descriptions in those childish letters!

It is very kind of you to ask us what kind of dresses we want for our birthday. We want the latest style white dresses -twin dresses. We have been wishing for white dresses for a long time. We don't know the latest style in America. 

With loving from your granddaughter, Polly."

The flightiness of the 'teen age caught them, and Polly describes it: "Grandma, you said that you missed my letter. I had unconsciously put my letter concerning my expeditions and the sights of Tahiti, into the envelope box, and there it lay. Ave Maria! What a stupid girl I was. The fact is that I am in adolescence. Peggy and I go wool-gathering a great deal like other girls. You know that girls in their 'teens are different in their minds. 

I find myself carrying a toothbrush into the kitchen while it is supposed to be put away in the bedroom! Once I carried a towel to the living room and laid it on daddy's lap! Then the realization of my movements woke me up. I snatched it from daddy who stared at my queer action with astonishment. It was difficult for me to hide my embarrassment. Worst of all, most every day, I find myself smiling when alone, according to my funny imagination. Paugh!

My friend Cooky, and her husband Steven, came to visit Mother Angel, so we had a dance. 

It was my first dance with boys. I danced with 

Cooky, then Steven, then Laisa, then George. I was nervous for I had never danced with boys, but soon I didn't mind about that. Peggy told me that before she hated to dance, but now she likes it and asked me if I were the same. I told her I liked to dance but it wearied me soon. Shura told me that if I danced more my legs wouldn't get tired. But when I tried later I didn't like to dance at all. Funny how I change my mind. Perhaps

I am still too young to get interested in dancing. 

I'm only a kid!

Before Cooky and Steven left we all had a canoe trip down to Zane Grey's wharf. We saw his fine boat and his son Lorin who had just caught a mere baby sword fish weighting 200 pounds. They gave us a piece of that fish meat, and we paddled back with sunburn on us, but we were very happy. 

We went to a fair in Papeete and it was' such a pretty one. There were only a few people wandering about looking at the school work, beautifully sewed clothes and queer dead fishes, which are strange to you but not to us. We couldn't find the cat show because there weren't any cats. But we saw birds. 

We rode in a taxi -the first "smooth" ride we have had in three years, 'cause we always ride in the truck which makes noise and goes bumpitybump from Papeete to Vairao. When we return

to America we will enjoy our rides -until we get tired of them. We will get spoiled again in 

America, I know. We never went to the movies here. Maybe when I go back to the States I will go to the movies and get a terrible headache!

After the ride we went to a cafe and thought we would have a nice Chinese dinner, but we didn't like the food which was all dry -bacon, rice and some other food which we never ate before. I gave my food to a wistful dog who was sitting beside me and Peggy.

We walked through the lonesome streets and mother bought us pineapple pie and ice cream. 

For two years we didn't have ice cream. We met mother's friend who told her a story about two men and a "tupapau" which means a ghost or spirit or devil, and there they talked for two hours. That made Peggy and me complain, but we had some fun throwing water on each other. I did more than Peggy and she became enraged when I threw water on her new dress. But I let her throw water on me, so then we were both hilarious. 

Peggy and I are getting plump. Our bones are hidden now. We started to 'plump' from the heads. Next our necks and backs, next arms, then hips and rather to our knees. But someday our lower legs will get plump, too.

Peggy and I were thrilled as we opened the packages, and love to see many things, and we are happy to have the sugar spoon, butter knife, salt shaker, cream pitcher and little coffee pot, for we haven't things like those here. Sometimes,

when mother was away, we were a bit naughty and took some of the candy you sent. Daddy was delighted to get the shirts and wanted to wear each shirt every day, but mother wouldn't let him because we would only have to wash them, and mother will let daddy use only two or three shirts until they are worn out. Then she will give him another two or three. So she can save them for him. Millions of thanks for the candy, we don't get that kind down here. We put it in the food cupboard where the breeze comes by so it won't melt. Mother put the flowered material on the bookcases, chairs and sofa. They look nicer than before because the boxes are hidden. 

Mother put the flowered pillow cases on the pillows. I am keeping the hairbrush until mine is worn out, then I will use it. It was like Christmas when your packages came, we don't care a bit because they are all old things. Down here we like every little thing. In America we were "fussy" and wanted new things. 

Mother told us about the marriage of Henri, a Frenchman, and his English bride. They had a 'civil' marriage and then a church wedding. I gazed at mother awhile and then slowly spoke, "I will not marry in a beautiful gown but in riding breeches the way you did, because if I wore a dress the elastic might break by accident and I would be terribly embarrassed to have the spectators see me squirm." "The elastic what?" asked mother. "The elastic which holds up my bloomers. The bloomers might drop to my feet

before people- ""Bloomers" mother echoed. and burst into a laugh. "Oh, you have gone lo-co I hadn't thought of that- 99 

Goodbye. SMAK!!!! 

Forever yours, Polly Lo-co."

What problems youth has that we grown-ups don't realize!

Peggy must have been feeling especially old and intellectual on the day she wrote this: "I read mother's letter to you and it said that Polly and I were missing the frills, fads, boys and thrills of the 'teen age. Pshaw! I'm not anxious to have beaus, and I almost always think of them as reckless, boastful, foolish boys. I am glad I am not frivilous." 

Easy to say when there are no beaus. How will matters stand when she gets back to America among her own kind? 

Another day Peggy is feeling particularly young and writes: "Gee, I hate to give up calling you Ma-ma-ma. I love it and always think back to the days of our baby-sisterhood, calling you Ma-ma-maaaaa." 

She realizes that her home life is nearer normal than her life in America. Her journal records this: "I know my daddy better than I did in 

America. He is still handsome, especially when his hair gets long and needs cutting, but he is not a stranger any more. Every time native women pass our house they walk very, very slowly, hoping daddy will see and admire them.

Silly! 

Daddy has no thought of them. 

He is busy playing puzzles with Polly, or talking with mother, or reading some magazines or sleeping.

But the women think they can attract him. 

When daddy finishes shaving, his face is smooth and smells nice with powder, so he feels like kissing. He kisses mother and she kisses him. Then daddy comes and kisses me and I kiss him, then he kisses Polly and she gives him a kiss. Too affectionate dad-dee, isn't he?

Last month I didn't get any letters and my feelings were almost hurt. I tried to buck up and not be babyish."

A year after asking for the new dresses in the latest style, Peggy's ideas have changed for she tells grandma: "Please don't send any more new dresses, just old ones. I am growing very fast and might outgrow them, and then the new ones would be wasted. I think when I return to

America, my growth glands might almost stop working, and then I could wear new dresses for a longer time. Polly too. People here don't know anything about new styles, so it's no use to have new styles.

When we started having vacation the days just staggered. We told mother we wanted something to do to make them go fast, so she said we could work easy arithmetic every day. Then Polly and

I thought a fine idea. We would write our memoirs. Polly calls hers "Recollections Of Our 

Early Childhood" and I call mine "Life And Memoirs of Peggy Parsons". We will enjoy reading

them when we are older, when we are ladies, oh, no, better say 'women'.

I think you would like to know about our memoirs, so I will copy a part in this letter and I won't change the sentences for you will want to know it as a story: At the time we were seven years old we were wearing Pilgrim costumes with aprons and little white caps. To everybody we were cute. Polly asked me to be ladies who doted on telephoning. I agreed, and we clambored up on the woodshed, but we were cautious because it was old. It would break any moment if we walked boldly and d-d-down we would go to the place which was full of snakes. 

We got small pieces of bicycle tire each to represent a telephone. We tied strings to a telephone pole which was close by. I demanded to be near the pole, which was close to the front of the shed roof, and Polly submitted. I sat “ladylike” and began to jabber. I was so busy with it, gesturing and pointing with the telephone receiver that I never noticed Polly leaving the roof to go in the house. Between the jabber, I said: "Oh. That's too bad. No. Yes. Smackra. 

Oh, oo. No. Yes. Yes" and nodded and shook my head violently. 

I got a strange feeling I was being watched. I glanced down and there were eight or ten people looking at me! 

To them I was laughable, but I was dreadfully embarrased and determined to leap from the high shed. I jumped up and ran, forgot the decaying

shelter, then remembered the weak roof and lost my courage. Down the ladder I went, scurrying off like a wild goose.

The other people thought it comical, but to me it was horrible and unforgivable. It impressed my mind deeply, and I never forgot my discomfiture.

We have been writing our memoirs for three weeks now and I feel the days are flying very fast. 

I am afraid I will have a short life when I am an old woman for my young days are going swiftly. 

Your loving Piggly-Peggy."

TAIHAI. 

and their 

All-day suckers for sirens. Taihai steals a wife. Husbands have their rights lefts.

Since Taihai had become a salaried man he had enjoyed a sudden popularity among the local belles. For two months he had us hold back half his wages to save them. On the night we paid him the magnificent sum of $14.40, in a delightfully large number of francs, he made a trip to Papeete. 

He returned three days later with an aching head, a seaman's chest and an outfit of clothes which included patent leather pumps two sizes too small for him. When he blossomed out in lavendar trousers at the Saturday night dance, the girls vied with each other for his smiles and francs. 

He was a fascinating gentleman indeed when he bought fifteen all-day suckers and passed them to a waiting line of sirens. Finding him irresistible, one of them went home with him.

She turned out to be a Tahitian gold-digger

TAIHAI 

243 and Taihai's modest wage of 36 cents a day melted away for her cigarettes, candy, ribbons and dresses till there was nothing left for sugar and bread. If Taihai stopped work to fish or gather fei in the mountains his wages stopped, so the food cupboard was soon empty. The girl returned to her father's house for meals, but showed up at Taihai's home every pay night to do a little chiseling. Taihai wearied of the arrangement and returned her to her father who couldn't forgive that insult for many a day.

Then our Friday looked about for a more settled woman to manage his house. Unfortunately he was attracted by the wife of Teata, mother of six, who lived on the adjoining plantation. She was plump, golden-skinned and still youthful in spite of her 25 years, when most native women are middle aged. "She come live with me soon," Taihai told us. "Me get new mattress, table, then she come." "What about all those children?" "Four stay papa. Hutia and me take two. 

Soon new baby come. MY baby," and Taihai puffed his chest out.

All might have gone well except for the fact that Teata and Hutia are quite legally married, and the outraged husband threatened beatings if 

Hutia dared leave him. Harry tried to persuade 

Taihai to choose an unmarried girl, but in spite of everything, Taihai planned a blissful future with Hutia.

He was concerned about "prettying up" to

YOU COULD DO IT. TOO! 

please Hutia, and spent several days at our home using Harry's tools in fashioning footwear for himself. He had found it impossible to walk in the patent leather pumps, so they stood on a shelf in his house, a highly prized ornament, shining darkly between a broken clock and a toy horn.

The sandals he made were serviceable, but not streamlined: soles of old tire casings and straps of inner tubing.

At the same time he began to talk of getting a gold tooth. "Have you ever been to a dentist, Taihai?" "No." "A gold tooth will cost a lot of money." "You give me half wages. You save half.

Bime❜by plenty money, me get gold tooth, here.” 

He touched a perfect looking tooth. "Does it hurt?" "No." "Then what in the world do you want a gold tooth for?" "Hutia like." 

Ha! A lure for Hutia. More prettying up. "Harry, you and me go doctor."

Harry refused to accompany him on such an errand.

A week later Harry was going to Papeete on business. Taihai appeared with a bath towel about his head, complaining of toothache. We suspected a trick but couldn't be sure. "Harry, you and me go Papeete tomorrow. 

Tooth no good. Plenty hurt.”

TAIHAI 

245 We peered in his mouth, couldn't see any cavities, but he groaned so well that Harry said Taihai might go with him. They got on the truck at 

2 A. M., bath towel and all, in a wind and rain storm. 

Next morning the wind blew many of our bananas over and coconuts were thudding down all around us. Our thatch roof was tangled and blown away in patches. The truck returned early, unable to get to town. The road was under water for miles and a bridge was washed away. 

The stream near us was a torrent which muddied the lagoon a mile out. Harry had seen a native house spin past and one boy was drowned. There was no hope of getting to town for a week at least. Taihai was frightened white.

Our house swayed in the wind and we were glad that some of the posts were green purau which had taken root in the ground. When the worst of the blow was over Harry went to see what damage had been done on the plantation and narrowly escaped a falling cocotree which was too old to stand against the gale. Taihai feared to stay in his little house for it was in the shadow of two other old trees. He spent the day with us, running outside when our house shook. 

We heard nothing more about toothache for two weeks, but when the bridge was re-built and trucks were running again, he appeared groaning, with the bath towel to bear witness for him .. Harry and Taihai finally reached town and arrived at the dentist's doorway just as a native

came down the stairs with blood running from his mouth. Taihai took one horrified look and bolted up a back street.

Harry never saw him again until time to catch the truck back to the country, and Taihai returned with all his teeth intact. A week later 

Harry got a toothache and decided to have an extraction. "Now's your chance, Taihai. Go with me if you want to."

So Taihai trailed along with his mind made up to bravery and a gold tooth. He trembled in the waiting room and turned grey when Harry's tooth came out. Harry had to be a good example, and didn't dare let out a murmur, but Taihai fled.

Harry and the dentist caught him at the top of the stairs, dragged him to the chair, and between them, the tooth came out. Taihai cried like a child and was outside before anything could be said about a gold tooth to fill the gap. That night Taihai told us over and over, "Harry big man. Harry no yell."

Several weeks passed and Taihai asked for the back wages Harry had been saving for him. "Going to get that gold tooth now, Taihai?”

He assured us he was, so we were not surprised to find him gone the next day. But the news was soon over the neighborhood that Hutia and her two youngest were missing. She had slipped away in the night, carrying a couple of babies and crawling over her sleeping husband and four children to elope with our Friday.

TAIHAI 

247 The husband descended on us in the belief that we were hiding the sinners. After a search of our small house he decided they were not with us, but still insisted that Harry had aided and abetted them in some way. It took a heated argument and a threat to call the police to get rid of him. We were put out with Teata, but amused when we recalled him as he had looked under our beds to find his wife, babies and rival. 

We believed that Taihai and Hutia had caught the boat for Bora-Bora since Taihai had an uncle there with much property. But Teata knew his own kind better than we. He believed the honeymooners were close by. Borrowing a bicycle he rode ten miles to the home of Hutia's aunt and found them in the midst of a wedding feast, or perhaps it should be called elopement feast. Teata and Taihai fought for the lady's favor. Taihai, fifteen years older, and a slighter man, was soon out. Teata made free with the feast, then put his strayed family on the return truck and had his flock together by nightfall. Taihai remained away until his eyes were open again and slipped back to Vairao crest-fallen.

Taihai had spent all his money on the elopement feast. He was broke, without a wife or gold tooth, either. He didn't dare go back to his own little house for it was close to Teata's home, and Teata was threatening further thrashings.

Taihai moved in with papa Poria, the witch doctor down the road. He had adopted a small boy from some family near the scene of his battle, whom

he kept with him night and day, whether for protection or consolation we didn't know.

Such was Taihai's fear of Teata that he wouldn't go to his house to get his belongings. 

Friends took some of his bedding and clothes to him. He asked Harry to take the new chest, table and bench for safe keeping. The lavender pants had been hopelessly ruined with mud and blood stains during the fight. The patent leather shoes were saved from the general wreckage and became a prized ornament on the mantlepiece at papa Poria's house. Taihai's adopted boy tries them on surreptitiously and hopes he will grow to meet their size. 

When Teata goes night fishing he doesn't trust his wife at home but marches her and the six children with their bedding to his father's house, a mile away. There they are lined up on the floor in the rear of the sleeping hut, flanked by brothers and sisters-in-law, with papa and mama on guard across the doorway.

Taihai was downcast for several weeks, but when another 'back wages' pay day rolled around, another girl moved in with him and his adopted boy. He cheered up. against the gold tooth. money for a phonograph and Christmas wine. 

He told us he had decided

Mother is stranded. A game their ancestors played. Peppy tells that "damned business" where to go-but she always comes back. 

No bread for eight days. 

Christmas passed with a firecracker celebration at our house, then natives began their preparations for the New Year festivities which mean a great deal more to them than Christmas. 

There were group fishing trips with nets. The extra fish supply was kept in bamboo cages in the lagoon, to live until needed. There were many trips to the mountains for fei. Banana beer was brewed by the gallon.

Our brown neighbors invited us to go in one of their large outriggers to collect shellfish on the reef. We got separated, Hina and I going far down the reef. A stiff breeze sprang up, helped the incoming tide and big waves caught us. 

Peggy and Polly, watching from the big canoe were horrified. Peggy wrote in her journal later: "The waves rushed higher and higher, and

knocked mother and Hina down. 

First mother fell and Hina picked her up. Then Hina fell and mother picked her up. Then they both fell and picked each other up. A boy in our little canoe tried to get them off the reef, but the strong backwash drove him on a clump of coral and broke the outrigger. The canoe upset. The boy swam and tried to tie the outrigger back in place, but in vain! Mother wanted to dive off the reef and swim to us, but Hina held her wrist tightly and wouldn't let her go for fear mother would be dashed on the coral. Daddy, Taihai, Polly and I paddled very fast to rescue them. I became so weak with fright I could hardly paddle. We reached the reef and mother jumped into the big canoe. There was blood on her arms and legs from coral cuts. But we got a lot of shellfish and had a grand trip!" 

New Year's day the drinking and singing started in the morning and didn't end until the fourth day following. There were nightly dances on the store porch and gambling on the road. 

Games were played. Among them one that their ancestors indulged in generations ago. A coconut is fastened on the top of a tall pole set up in an open space. Then men, in competing teams try their skill at hitting the nut with spears. They tried once too often on New Year's night. A spear missed the nut and came down in the skull of a boy who was darting across the field. 

Old Amaru had thrown the spear. He rushed forward and gathered the unconscious child in his

HOLIDAY DOINGS 

251 arms only to find that it was his own son. The father pulled out the spearhead and started to the hospital, but the child died on the way. Back at the house wailing songs of the 'Himini' went on all night. The next day a simple board coffin was made, loaded with flowers and carried to the cemetery by Amaru and his friends, with most of Vairao following silently on foot. An all night wake followed with coffee, bread and quantities of wine. As the wine went around the songs became more cheerful, and the New Year festivities went on without pause. 

We saw our man, Saturday, blissfully intoxicated, being supported home by his best friend, while his wife scolded and screamed. Saturday seldom goes under the table, so his wife is indignant when he does. Two hours later, we saw the wife, drunker than Saturday had been.

She had captured another man and with arms about each other, they reeled down the road singing. We never see sights like these except at the holiday season. Usually there is no public petting. The brown brothers are decorus in their own way.

We received a New Year gift from the policeman: nuts which he had gathered from our valley. 

They looked like black walnuts, rich and oily. We ate them freely with disastrous results and found out later that the natives use them sparingly as a purge.

The little Chinese baker, 'Peppy' has been having more trouble than usual through the

holidays, with his half-caste wife, Paipe. Peppy speaks good English and tells his difficulties to 

Harry. Peppy hates the gatherings on the store porch because his wife is in her glory there, dancing and flirting. Paipe stays up most of the night singing and dancing, then sleeps the next day when Peppy wants her to help him make the bread.

Peppy tries to make her work. A quarrel begins. 

Paipe stamps, screams and bangs chairs on the floor. Peppy tells her where to go in Tahitian, Chinese, French and English. She advances with a chair upraised. Peppy dodges. The chair crashes against the wall. Paipe abandons it and runs for Peppy with tooth and nail ready. Peppy keeps a table between them, for she will make two of him. The sister-in-law, Hina, enters and tries to make peace and save the furniture. Paipe turns on her and tears her clothes. Hina pulls hair and scratches.

Ah Lam, the proprietor of the store, tries to do his bit for peace, but is soon drowned out by the three who are now fighting each other indiscriminately. Ah Lam sends a boy to bring the policeman. He enters in a lordly way, but is put to flight by the screaming women, for he is a mere man. The policeman retires and advises Ah Lam to let them wear themselves out, which they do after an hour. Paipe and Hina go through a course of hysterics and lapse into fatigue. They sleep off their fighting jag. Upon awakening their troubles are forgotten.

HOLIDAY DOINGS 

253 But Peppy can't forget so easily. He gives his wife money and sends her back to Papeete to her mother, "to stay", he tells her. The next night she comes back. Peppy moans and sinks.

The contrite wife declares, "Peppy, I can't live without you. I can't eat. Can't sleep," and almost chokes Peppy with large, fat caresses. Peace lasts three days, then it is all to do over again. 

When Peppy talks to Harry about Paipe, he never calls her by name, or speaks of her as his wife, but always as "that damn business."

Vairao has no sooner recovered from the excesses of the holidays than the Chinese New 

Year comes with its complications. The shops and bakeries close for four days. The Chinese men gather in large groups in different parts of the island for games and gambling. There is very little drunkeness. Their fun runs to firecrackers and opium smoking.

We knew better than to expect a bread delivery for four days, but when the time stretched to eight days, we began to worry over our little Sam-mee because he owed us 500 francs for copra, as well as being our bread man. Harry got word that Sam-mee was at Taravao and pedaled over. He found him with other Chinese men in an old frame house, set back from the road. They were in all stages of coma from opium. Harry roused Sam-mee and demanded his money. Sammee was past standing up but told Harry to take the money from his pocket. He had been lucky at dice and had several thousand francs in fat

rolls. Harry peeled off 500 and left. As he went out the yard, motorcycle police from Papeete arrived to raid the place. All the Chinese were arrested and clapped in jail. We didn't see Sammee again for three months. The rest of his roll went toward paying his fine. We felt lucky to have our share of it. 

In all, the holidays were not just what we had been accustomed to in the States, but they didn't lack interest and excitement.

Cream Puffs are better without saddles. You can't desert a Princess. Boy and Zebra are not that way about each other. Spooks! "Cowboy-all same cinema!" The tragic bulge. 

Nona, the Cannibalette! Women are more broadminded. (?) Harry finds himself in bed with a strange woman. Harry vanishes.

We were in our third year of island life and had never been on the windward side of Tahiti- 

Nui. That's the main part of the island, "Great 

Tahiti." We live on Tahiti-Iti, the smaller part, farthest away from Papeete. Remember when you were a kid in school and used to get bored or dreamy in arithmetic time? When you passed idle minutes filling in the ovals of the zeros and the loops of the eights? When you get an ‘8’ blacked in prettily you have drawn a map of 

Tahiti. Not a jittery, new-fangled eight with the finishing stroke sticking up kicking; but an old-fashioned eight built entirely on curves. 

Now put a dot at the bottom of the larger loop and call it Papeete. Another little dot, a bit off center, on the shore line of the smaller loop, and

that's our Niao house. Get out the compass and plumb line and swing your '8' around until its axis slants Nor'-Nor'-West by Sou'-Sou❜-East, with Papeete blinking out across the sunlit stretches of blue ocean in the general direction of Japan. Label that point where the two loops join 'the Isthmus of Taravao', and your map is finished. If you want to make it more fancy, put a rough road all the way around the coastline except at the end of the island beyond our house where the cliffs of ancient lava defy the road gangs to carry their work any farther. 

We planned a trip around the big loop, through

Papeete. A family party of four on horseback. 

At the last minute we found ourselves lacking one horse so we borrowed a hard-trotting bicycle. No one liked the bicycle. Its saddle was too small. 

It refused to keep pace with the three horses.

Thought it was a racer, went too fast and showed the others up. Or, if held down to a walk, it wobbled. It was the only one of our steeds which ever bucked us off. The one of us who was feeling the most self-sacrificing at the moment would offer to take a turn on the bicycle.

After my experience riding Pi Pi up the mountain we had sent for our army saddle and it came down Parcel Post, at what seemed like airplane rates. Later a friend from the states brought our western saddle down with his luggage. We gave Zebra a sea bath and new shoes for the trip; borrowed one horse, 'Boy', from the Vairao school teacher and bought another mare. On first sight

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

257 we named the newcomer "Cream Puff", both because of color and roundness. What a silhouette she had! She had been lazying in rich pasture grass for three years without a day's work. She was delivered to us wrapped up in three hundred pounds of pure fat. You couldn't have guessed she had a backbone, for her back humped up with fat. A friend suggested she might have a strain of camel somewhere in her ancestry. Cream Puff had a beautiful face, slightly dished, with the flaring nostrils of an Arabian. Zebra looked common beside her. "Will the horses find enough to eat?" I asked 

Harry. "Cream Puff will need a whole field to herself if she's to keep that matronly figure." "Sure. There'll be plenty of grass everywhere.

They can graze all night, picketed out on chains or ropes. I'll get up every night and move them to new feed if the grass seems scarce at any stopping place." "And we can stop to eat our lunch where they can get a bite at the same time." 

We didn't have a saddle for Cream Puff, but her fat was as round and soft to loll on as a feather pillow. We had a pair of old stirrups on straps. 

We hung these over the region where a horse would normally have shoulder bones. We had no strap long enough to encircle Cream Puff's tummy so the stirrups had no anchorage of any sort. That made mounting difficult. If you put any weight in one stirrup it betrayed you by giving way, while the opposite one came up. If you

disregarded the stirrups and leaped on board from the ground, the sacks over Cream Puff's back got mussed up and you found yourself sitting on large wrinkles. These refused to smooth out with your weight on top. . . it took at least two of us to get one hoisted smoothly on Cream Puff.

Once settled on her back, the rider dreaded to descend because there would be the mounting to do all over again. Through the entire trip it was, "Honey, please get my hat. It blew off. I don't want to get down." And "Do you think I'd better eat lunch up here? or get off? which is the worst, mother?" and, "Dad-dee, please cut me another switch. I lost mine and I don't want to get off 

Cream Puff." 

But in spite of the mounting difficulties, Cream

Puff became a big favorite in more ways than one before the end of the trip. Cream Puff's legs looked ridiculously short under her barrel body, and she didn't have much speed, but she had wonderful control. She paced, single-footed and waltzed with remarkable smoothness. She was easier to ride without a saddle than the other horses with. Especially as the other two were stricken with temporary insanity every time they met a bridge.

The morning we started we dashed about trying to have early breakfast, pack our bedding roll, commissary department, tooth-brushes and sweaters, getting in each other's way quite a bit. 

We dressed Cream Puff in her sacks and draped the stirrups over her shoulders. We saddled Boy

Boy, Zebra and Cream Puff, the Peacemaker.

261 and Zebra and separated them a half dozen times for they drew together like a couple of magnets and went at each other with tooth and hoof. We strapped packs behind saddles and tied sweaters on in front. "Have we remembered everything? Hats? 

Pocket combs? Sun glasses?" "Mother, where's Princess Palico? Come, Kitty, Kitty, Kitty."

And we turned our attention to finding the Princess. She was to be taken to her mama, at the home of our American neighbors. We couldn't desert a princess for five days. Everything stopped while the whole family hunted for the cat. 

She had been around at breakfast and made a nuisance of herself when we rolled the bedding on the living room floor for she got the idea it was all done to amuse her. She had played among our sweaters and chewed our toothbrushes, but turned up missing when we were ready to leave. 

We called and hunted and whetted knives together and clattered dishes for fifteen minutes.

Peggy and Polly began to argue for giving up the trip if the Princess couldn't be found, rather than leave her alone in a cruel world. Patience was wearing thin when Polly went to her box dresser to get a head scarf and put her hand on Princess

Palico's soft fur. The kitten was blandly sleeping. 

Her yawn was the utmost in delicacy and her angelic face said, "Oh-were-you-calling-me? Butit's-time-for-my-nap-now." 

We put her into a rice sack over her indignant

"meows," mounted and waved goodbye to Taihai and the group of curious neighbors, brown and yellow, who had been collecting for an hour past to see our start and yell "Cowboy. Cowboy."

Harry had long been known as "Cowboy" to hundreds of natives on the island. His reputation for handling cows dated from our first month here when he broke several wild cows to milk. After that our own cows were the marvel of the island, Then when we got our western saddle, with the bucking rolls, and Harry began breaking horses, his fame spread. Everywhere we went we were greeted with, "Cowboy, Harry Tane, Cowboy."

The first stage of our journey was to the tune of Palico's plaintive cries. It took fifteen minutes to re-introduce Palico to her mother, Pansy, who refused to recognize her as a friend, much less as a daughter; another ten minutes to say our farewells to Palico; and two hours more to reach the 

Chinese eating house at Taravao.

Several miles further on we stopped to show the children the house where Robert Keable lived while he was writing his stories of French Oceania. In one of his books he described his home as an artist's dream come true in stone and lumber. The house is one of the show places of the island and is usually rented to well-to-do Americans. 

An hour before sunset we burst upon the gaze of the village of Mataiea, where lived John, the boy wonder, primary teacher of the native school. 

They say that he showed precocious intelligence

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

263 as a child, whizzed through the district school, entered the Papeete school at a tender age and began teaching at sixteen. 

So to John we went to find a lodging for the night. We wanted to rent a couple of beds. If we couldn't get beds we would take mattresses on the floor. If we couldn't get two mattresses, we would take one and sleep on it in a row, crosswise. We thought John understood our American-Tahitian explanation and left him to inquire about the neighborhood for beds. We staked the horses out so they could graze, putting Boy and Zebra farthest from each other. Boy made little rushes to the end of his chain, telling Zebra what her fate would be if he could get his teeth on her. 

She snickered insults at Boy. 

We went to the beach for supper. It was white sand, a rarity here. A small island, two hundred yards off-shore looked romantic with palms leaning over the water. It wouldn't be romatic to go to though for these small islets have no water, are rat-infested and there's no place to walk or sit for they are covered knee-deep with broken coral. Better to enjoy them from a distance. We built a tiny fire and made cocoa with canned milk. 

We spread salmon and cheese sandwiches and dropped some of them in the sand. You know these picnic troubles!

As the sun slid down toward China we returned to see how John was getting on. Instead of beds he had rented us a deserted house across the road from his school. He had two native boys cutting

a path through the weeds to the doorstep. He had three women sweeping the place. It was an enormous old French home of eleven rooms and prodigal verandas. Five rooms across the front of the house opened onto the front porch. The house had not been occupied for years. John was a-brim with service, hurrying the women at their work. 

We told him we wanted to use only the front porch and there was no need to sweep the entire house. But they were determined to clean it all. 

The women hurried with their work, anxious to get away before dark. What Tahitian wants to be near a deserted house after sunset when a ghost or Tupapau may show himself at any minute? 

But where was our mattress? John showed us mats woven of pandannus. We wanted something with a more softening effect on the floor. We went to his home and pointed to mattresses and prodded pillows but the best we got was a thin pad from a narrow couch. We carried it back to the eleven rooms and the verandas. We made our bed and tried it. If we put the pad under our heads and shoulders, our hips fell off on the floor in a distressing way. If we wriggled higher on it to ease aching hips, our heads lolled on the floor. All night we four slipped off, and crawled back on that bit of pad, wondering how any one night could be so long. A dozen times I thought day was coming to the rescue but it was only moonlight. 

Some time before dawn Harry said he would go and change the horses to better feed, then

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

265 walk on the road a bit and smoke his pipe. I dropped off to sleep again, quite exhausted from trying to curl myself on my small portion of the pad. 

I was awakened by thumps at the back of the house. It was quite dark for the moon had set. "Is that you, Harry?" I called. There was no answer. "Hey, is that you coming in the back way dear?" I tried again. Thick silence, and plenty of it. Then a squeak from somewhere. One of the many doors swinging a little in the night breeze. I thought I had been mistaken about the thumps, turned onto the hip which was not quite so sore and tried to sleep. The door continued to creak in a most annoying way. Why is a squeak twice as bad in darkness? And when your big, brave man is not around? 

The thumps came again. No mistake this time,

Then a noise like a chain clanking and dragging up steps. I didn't believe in ghosts or Tupapau, but I wished Harry were around. More thumps. coming nearer. I sat up and peered down the long veranda. Something grey moved or did 

I just think so? The dragging chain again! And thumps! It wouldn't be a barefooted native, but could it be the lunatic Chinaman we had heard about in the village the one who was kept chained? 

"He's usually harmless," we had been told, "but they put him in chains at night so he won't get into any mischief." Was that clumping noise the sound of the Chinaman's wooden clogs?

It came nearer and I wanted to scream but had that awful feeling you get in dreams when you try to shriek and can't make a sound. If it were the insane Chinaman what would he do to us?

Now the grey thing was between us and the veranda steps. Oh, why didn't Harry come back? I closed my eyes tightly and tried to think if I had ever read about what to do when confronted with a demented Chinaman. The footsteps came nearer and I opened my eyes without having thought of one soothing word in Chinese.

The grey blur was low to the floor. Was he creeping? Did all escaped Chinamen creep or just this one? The blur disappeared with another thump or two and a little more chain dragging. 

He was beind the door which was swinging in the wind. "Getting ready to spring suddenly," I thought. "Am I going to sit quietly and let us all be murdered without a fight?" It was very still. Peggy and Polly were sleeping soundly beside me. I remembered the water bottle which Harry had filled from the stream and put at the head of our community bed in case anyone wanted a drink in the night. I hefted it. Almost full. Would it crush a skull? I rose on my knees grasping the bottle firmly. "

That brought results. Clump, clump and the grey thing appeared, answered "Ma-a-a-a," and put out a cold, wet nose inquiringly.
