I gathered up the goat's dragging chain and led him down the veranda steps. I'm not clever at swearing, so I was only muttering, "I'll tie you up where you won't wake me again," when Harry returned. "Well, what in the world, honey 99 "This goat". Really it was difficult to think of anything adequate at the moment.

"Gosh, dear," said Harry, "Don't bother about picketing out other people's stock. They'll change him to fresh feed at daylight. I just moved all the horses across the road on some good grass, and-" We had come twenty miles the first day and that was plenty for we hadn't been on horseback for a month. Peggy and Polly had played around with Zebra some when we first bought her but that soon lost its charm. In our California camp they had been accustomed to one full-sized horse and saddle each, so there wasn't much thrill to a pint-sized island horse and one saddle. At fourteen, girls have passed the stage of riding by turns or with two on one mount.

We were stiff and sore at breakfast, but some of us wouldn't admit it. The second day brought no adventures except heat, more soreness, bridges and a continuation of the feud between Boy and 

Zebra. 

Boy is mean. Even his mistress admits that and she is very partial to him. Zebra was at fault, too -nasty and sarcastic all day. She would nip Boy in unguarded spots. When they were allowed within kicking distance of each other, there were hooves flying. We tried all sorts of marching formations to try to get more harmony. 

If Boy were put out in front he thought he was the leader of a herd, and must dominate the situation at all costs. He would step out on a brisk trot a liver-shaking thing at best— calling taunts over his shoulder to the mares. Cream

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

269 

Puff was too much of a lady to heed his insults, but Zebra was always ready to show her mettle. 

Zebra had a hard mouth. She was ruined by 

Chinese-style-driving in her youth. The Chinaman pulls back on the lines to make a horse go forward, so by the time we got Zebra her mouth was calloused like the bottom of a native's foot. 

Harry carefully taught her American style: to stop at a pull on the lines, or "whoa", and to go forward at a gentle slap of the lines or a chirrup. 

But Zebra always went beserk at Boy's pretentions of superiority, and reverted to the habits of her youth. The more we sawed on the lines to stop her, the more determined she was to run up and take a tasty bite out of Boy's hip.

When Boy saw her gaining on him, he would turn on a faster trot and Zebra would hit a gallop. 

All very well if you're not too sore for such play, and if you don't meet trucks on a narrow bit of road with ditches on each side. 

Then a bridge would come in sight. Boy would begin to look uncertain. Did he want to be the 

Zebra could spy a leader of the herd after all? bridge from a long distance. She would make an effort, catch up with Boy as he began to falter, take the nip she'd been planning on and escape Boys's heels by a side jump. The bridge is coming nearer and Boy looks harrassed. As the leader he should go on. But he slows up, prancing sidewise to gain time. He knows his duty but has lost all will power. Boy snorts at the bridge, ready to turn tail and run if the bridge shows

evil intentions. It does. Boy tries to use bad language to the bridge, but loses his nerve. 

Zebra knows she isn't going over that bridge.

She fell through a hole in a bridge once, at least part of her did, and she never forgets the terror of it. She turns her back, refusing to look at the bridge. Boy begins to go around in large circles, pretending he is going onto the bridge, but turning back suddenly each time his feet reach. the first plank. The longer we keep him near the bridge the more jittery he gets. The circles become smaller and faster until he is giving a pretty good imitation of a merry-go-round steed.

Occasionally he gets dizzy, or tangled up in his feet, then he remembers business back on the road and makes a little run off at a tangent, but he never forgets himself and runs across the bridge. Zebra grows calm as soon as she makes up her mind not to look at the bridge and is quietly cropping grass. She will hoard her strength until someone tries to force her over the bridge.

The first day we were foolish enough to think we could boss the two of them with whips and spurs. We got them onto one bridge by force, but regretted it for they re-opened their feud in the middle of it. That, added to their bridge-insanity, almost ended the trip. Boy backed to the edge of the planking, then made a rush at Zebra. 

Luckily it was a low bridge so when she sidestepped, she and her rider had only two feet to drop. (We wouldn't want that to happen on a

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

271 bridge thirty feet above water). An English speaking humorist, meeting us at the moment, inquired, "Are you people going somewhere or is this just a horse picnic?"

After that we waited for Cream Puff. All this time she has been calmly on her way to the bridge, never breaking her gentle gait. She looks neither to right nor left, but single-foots onto the bridge, even if it's a bad one with wide cracks grinning.

Her manner says that there are no horses within miles of her she simply doesn't see Boy, Zebra or any of their bad behavior.

Boy comes up out of a tail spin and looks indignantly after Cream Puff. What right has a mare to take the lead? Gingerly he tests the first plank of the bridge. It holds. He tries another step and shivers at his own boldness. Cream Puff is swinging ahead at her easy pace. Boy is overcome with shame. He gets all four feet on the bridge.

He'd better go forward and overtake that presumtious mare. He goes in a burst of speed, laying his ears back so Cream Puff will know who the boss is. As he passes Cream Puff, he snarls, "Woman, how dare you?" But she, with untroubled brow, sees nothing of his temper. Zebra hears the thunderous passing of Boy on the bridge, raises her head and knows that nothing is left for her but to catch up with the procession. 

She whinneys and tries to get them to come to her. They won't, so she shakes off her bridgeterror as much as she can and follows. But never in a straight line. She zig-zags from one edge to

the other, looking for the safest places to step on. 

By the second day we had learned to stop Boy and Zebra at some distance away from bridges and let our angel-faced Cream Puff lead over.

We found the pleasantest road order with the three horses abreast, Cream Puff as peace maker, between the bickering ones. Her calmness became contagious as the other two became better acquainted with her. Once we tried the bicycle in the center to keep order, but it had no authority and didn't have the peaceful influence of Cream

Puff. When Boy and Zebra re-opened hostilities they did it right across the bicycle rider who was lucky to escape unwounded. After that it was always Cream Puff who (like the U. S. Marines) "arrived and got the situation well in hand."

We were determined to make 23 miles the second day to reach the home of a friend where we knew beds with mattresses would be available. The road was white and glaring through 

Paea for the road gang had been putting fresh coral on it. It was a relief to turn in at the Great 

Grotto, cool and dim, for lunch. Maiden hair ferns were all about the wide mouth of the cave and hanging upside down from the rock roof in a most trusting way. The pool which covered the cave floor was icy cold. There was a rock platform built out for a diving board but none of us had nerve enough to plunge in. We were chilled in a few minutes standing in the breeze which sighed from the depths of the cavern. We threw stones which appeared to fall in the center of the pool,

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

273 but we knew then that it was an optical illusion because the cave is many times longer than our throwing range. The roof appeared to be shallow and slope down to the water at a near point, but that was more of nature's trickery, for boats can go far back. An iron tank caught some of the roof drip and made a clean reservoir of drinking water.

Boy and Zebra became lunatics the moment we tried to lead them into the cave to drink. It didn't resemble a bridge in any way except for the water below, but they had strong ideas about it. Cream Puff walked in leisurely, drank her fill, and would have gone swimming with a little encouragement.

We rested in the shade of the cave mouth till the afternoon sun made rainbows in the drip from the roof. The road seemed dreadfully hot afterward, but we pushed on without stopping except for water at roadside springs or spigots. Night came and we were five miles from Jeanne's home in Faaa. We went into a family council and came out with minds made up to reach beds at any cost. 

The longer we rode, the more sore we got, and the more aches we discovered and the more we wanted soft beds. We would buy something to eat at the next store we came to something that didn't need cooking. "The moon will be up presently," said Harry, "and think how jolly it will be to ride by moonlight." Romance, eh? But the moon was delayed by something and we rode in darkness. Hours

later, it seemed, the lights of a store came in sight. 

We cheered up and arrived at a trot. A group of natives at the store porch saw our outfit and set up the familiar yell of "Cowboy, Cowboy." Boy and Zebra thought this an excuse for a little crazy cavorting, so Harry took the lead on Cream Puff. 

When almost at the porch steps, Cream Puff stumbled and fell on both knees. The sacks, stirrups and Harry shot over her head indiscriminately. Harry landed on his feet! Cream Puff scrambled to her feet and stood breathing down 

Harry's neck.

The yells of "Cowboy" were re-doubled. “All same cinema." "All same Ken Maynard." "You good Bill Hart." The natives crowded about congratulating Harry on his new dismounting trick.

It was neat. If I had managed it I would have felt quite cheeky, but I always land on my nose.

The last time I had sprawled off a horse, face foremost, Harry had proved himself a super-tactful husband, rushing to the rescue, saying, "Well, you poor darling. That's because you're topheavy in the head. Now I carry all my ballast in my big feet so they stay down. But your feet are so little and dainty that" Well, you get the idea. You see tact has nothing to do with truth, but such remarks help to smooth over bad moments. I've told Harry many times he should open a charm school for husbands. 

But poor Cream Puff! Her knees were bleeding and her velvet nose was scratched. We purchased a can of meat and some French cheese

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

275 with a 60 horse-power smell. Polly sniffed, peered about and inquired, "Did something die?" "No dear. It's just the cheese. Please carry it."

Polly held it at arm's length like a pup which has embarrassed the living room rug. We got hot bread just out of the oven and a tin of apricots.. Withdrawing from the limelight of the store, we sat wearily on a low stone wall and made sandwiches.

A lively donkey came out of the night and picked a fight with Boy and Zebra over the wall. 

We hadn't tied the horses, expecting our stop to be brief and they had good grass to amuse themselves with. Before we knew what was going on the three of them were doing their best to kick the wall down. For once Boy and Zebra were unanimous, joining forces against the stranger. 

Men began to shout and come with lanterns so we mounted and trotted off, still chewing on hunks of bread and passing the can of apricots for each one to help himself as best he could. If you want to know what that was like, get a can of the slippery things, let somone bounce you on a springy bed in the dark, and then try to get onefourth of the contents into your mouth without a spoon. You may use the point of a hunting knife if you like, for we passed one with the can. 

The knife cut us, pricked us and fell on the road by turns. Then the one on the bicycle had to light matches and scratch around in the dust until it was found. 

I'm not a bit ashamed to admit that I was tired

when we turned into the driveway of Jeanne's home two hours after dark. The house had fiveeights miles of veranda around it. We draped saddles, bridles and bedding roll over the railing, took showers, got an introduction to two large comfortable beds and fell in them. At least, Peggy, Polly and I did. Harry took the horses out for water and picketed them on grass and bicycled in to Papeete to try to buy a gallon of ice cream. He promised to wake us up if he got it. He couldn't get any in a container to bring out so he ate cones on the street corner: eight of them. Knowing how we loved ice cream, he ate two cones for each of us. Thoughtful man! 

Peggy woke me up next morning with, "Daddy says, 'come and get it'. Do you know what that means?" "Yes, child. It means that he's still spoiling me by cooking breakfast after all these years." "Mother, I want to marry a man like daddy. Do you think that any modern boys in America are learning to cook breakfasts?"

Later the children were making the beds while Harry and I went out to gather the horses and it occurred to me that there is one advantage in a bicycle. It doesn't have to be staked out and moved to fresh pasturage during the night. Zebra, kneedeep in water grass, looked calm for a change.

Farthest away, toward the beach, was Boy, beginning to dance in a double-jointed sort of way, thinking up evil remarks to whinny down at 

Zebra. But where was cream Puff?

277 "She was over there," said Harry, "half way between the two idiots, so they wouldn't shriek at each other all night." "Oh there she is, lying down. That's strange, so late in the morning. Maybe her knees are sore. That was a bad fall she had-bad for a fat lady." "Look at the queer way her neck is stretched out. And birds pecking at her. Why, she's dead!" "Dead! But I couldn't help it. I sobbed a little on 

Harry's shoulder. His shirt smelled of 'horse' and his pipe. It was comforting. "Cream Puff was so sweet." I recounted her virtues. "But the good are supposed to die young, aren't they?" Even when she was sweaty, I liked her smell. I-I-feel better

now. I'll go with you and help bury her. We'll have a funeral and flowers, and-" 

At that moment two more saucy minah birds flew down and lit on Cream Puff's bulging side. 

They perch on the cows, pigs and horses here all day, hunting for ticks and getting free rides. We had seen them a million times on all the Tahiti animals, but now we thought only of death and scavenger birds gathering for a feast. Suddenly

I was furious at the birds and rushed from 

Harry's arm, yelling, "Get out. Oh, get out, you nasty things."

I bore down on them with large gestures. The birds rose in startled flight, and Cream Puff clambored to her feet, blinking. "Oh, darling. She was just sleeping, after all. 

The bulge was only her night's feed."

Two hours later we were entering Papeete to more yells of "Cowboy." Almost every native on the island knows two expressions in English, or rather American: "O. K., boss" and "Cowboy." 

The children were eager for ice cream cones.

Harry told them to eat all they wanted. They don't grow in Vairao, and we go to town so seldom that ice cream is a rare treat. We sat on our horses and ate cones. Then we rode up the block to the next pushcart and ate more cones. Whenever we began to slow up, Harry would remind us that it might be six months before we got any more, saying cheerfully, "Have all you want, girls. 

We don't go on a picnic every day. Shall I order more?"

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

279 We reached a point of saturation at last and rode out of town. Polly grew silent and a little pale. "Mother, let's not trot," she said presently. "Daddy spent so much money for cones. I don't want to lose them." "Daddy was glad to get them for you, dear. 

Did you enjoy them?" "Well, I liked the first four, but daddy forced me to eat the last five."

Several miles out of town we came to the home of James Hall, co-author with Nordoff, of Mutiny on the Bounty, the Hurricane and others. We stopped for a chat. Mr. Hall has shown a kindly interest in Peggy's and Polly's education ever since we appealed to him for some books from his library.

Soon we were pulling up Tahiti's stiffest mountain grade. Most of the roads here are level, following the beaches, but as we got around on the windward side of the island, we found that the mountains had been shorn off, making steep cliffs. beaten by pounding waves. There is no protecting reef or calm lagoon. Rainfall is greater on that side, streams are larger and the fresh water pouring into the ocean discourages the coral growth. The sea for miles is ruffled and turbulent with cross currents. Here and there are sunken reefs, rocks, arches and tiny islets, all picturesque enough to see from the safety of the shore, but these are dangerous waters for craft. Few people live in this region and only the boldest take canoes

through these troubled waters. Frequent wind storms and squally weather are the expected thing, a great contrast to the calm of the lee side where we live.

There are strange water worn caves at the base of the rock cliffs. The surf boomed and pounded through the hollows making unearthly noises. 

There were long drawn sighs and groans until you could imagine almost anything going on in the caverns two hundred feet below. The Tahitians call this part the "shouting road." We heard the story of one of these caves from an American who lived near it. He had the tale from natives: 

Long ago there was a woman named Nona who lived in a little house near one of the caves at the base of the storm torn mountain. Nona was not as other women: her teeth were long and sharp like those of the wild boar. Her fangs had a real use: she was a cannibal. Her bodily strength matched her teeth and her taste in meat. She waited for unsuspecting travelers, men or women, sprang upon them and held an orgy of human flesh and blood. Nona's neighbors did not suspect her of cannibalism for many years, for she kept herself to herself and hid in a little out-house for her secret feasts. She warned people never to go to that little hut telling them that the "Tupapau' of her ancestors haunted the place. The superstitions of her primitive neighbors made her retreat safe, for in Tahiti one doesn't spy on one's friend's "Tupapau'. One goes far around the dangerous place with averted eyes.

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

281 

Nona had only one fear that her little daughter would discover her vice, for in Nona's time cannibalism was frowned upon. Centuries before it had been the mode, but times were changing, and Nona was clinging to an outworn custom. The little daughter was the only creature whom Nona loved; a bright-eyed child of sweetness, named 

Hina, in honor of the pagan moon goddess.

Hina was a gentle child who grew up in her mother's secluded home scarcely seeing anyone from year to year. She contented herself with flower-gathering, fishing and wandering along the base of the cliffs at low tide. When Hina had grown to be a lissom young girl she found a cavern which was unknown to her mother. It proved to be a magic cave with a rock door which would open to a password. The moon goddess whispered the 'open sesame' to her namesake.

Hina loved to go there, shut herself in and dream away idle hours, lulled by the wash of waves outside the stone door.

When Hina was sixteen and ripe for marriage, a handsome young man saw her, fell in love with her and begged for a tryst. (Some native stories couldn't be more pat if they came right out of a modern movie.) Hina led her Romeo to the secret grotto and there he lived. The girl took food to him and was very happy with her lover for several weeks.

The mother, a thrifty housekeeper, began to wonder where the food was disappearing. Every day she went fishing but couldn't keep the larder

full. Could the daughter be eating so much? Nana began to lie awake at night listening, for Hina had a bad habit of talking in her sleep, revealing her innermost thoughts. Nona soon heard about the young man, the food and the cave. She spent hours eavesdropping on Hina's sleep but couldn't find out just where the cavern was. 

One day Nona feigned illness and did not go fishing. Through half-closed eyes she watched 

Hina wrap poi and fish in fresh leaves and slip along the beach. Nona followed, heard the password, saw the magic door open and caught a glimpse of the young man inside before the great stone door grated closed again. Nona wasn't disturbed because her pretty daughter was meeting a lover. She was concerned only with getting the young man to eat.

The next day Nona sent her daughter fishing to get her out of the way. Nona went to the cave and called to the young Romeo inside. He knew it was not the voice of his beloved and trembled in his hiding place. But Nona knew the password.

When she spoke it the door had to obey. It opened. 

Nona ran in, caught the young man and killed him. She was so eager for the taste of human flesh she couldn't wait to cook him for this was her first victim for several weeks. She thought he had an excellent flavor, though raw. In her hurry she mislaid the heart.

When Hina returned from the sea with her catch, she found her mother in bed, still playing sick. Or possibly she had a real tummyache from

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

283 gulping raw Romeo. The beautiful girl hurried to the cave thinking her lover must be hungrybut hunger was no longer one of his troubles. Poor 

Hina! She found his heart only. But it was still beating strongly with love for her. (That touch of the detached heart still beating reminds us of some of the modern stories that find their way into certain magazines today.) The heart spoke: declared undying love for Hina and warned her against her mother. "Your mother will hate you now, my beloved, because she has injured you. She will try to kill you tonight. You must flee at dark." "Where shall I go?" asked timid Hina who had never been away from her mother's doorstep. "Go to the next district, called Haapape, 'Shallow Water,' and at the first house by the stream, you must beg for shelter of a hairy man. You will be welcomed." Romeo had been out in the world and knew the value of Hina's beauty in getting her another man and a home. "Oh, my love, I cannot leave you." "As you value your life, you must." You see

Romeo knew all the old melodramatic phrases. "But before you go, take dead banana leaves and fashion an effigy of yourself. Put it on your mat and cover it with your bedding to deceive your mother. She will not discover the trickery until dawn when she goes to kill you, and by that time you will be far away." "Oh my beating heart," cried Hina, referring to the one she was speaking to, not her own, "how

shall I ever leave you?" "Think not of me. You shall find happiness with another. Go now and prepare. One kiss before we part." The story doesn't state whether Hina shuddered at kissing a raw heart or not. She went home, made a model of herself and put it in her sleeping place. At dawn the unnatural mother crept to Hina's mat, intent on killing her daughter. She pounced on the effigy, bit through it and almost choked herself before she discovered it was old leaves.

During the night the daughter had fled to Haapape and gained the protection of a strong, hairy man just as foretold. Of course the hairy man fell in love with Hina at first sight. When he heard that it was Nona who had caused the mysterious disappearance of travelers, friends and family members, he gathered a war party and quickly dispatched the cannibalette.

Upon the return of the heroes, Hina married the hairy one. "They say" that Hina is a popular name in the district today where the descendants of the original beauty live. But the name of

Nona is quite out of style around here. We wanted to inspect the cave and see if the heart were still there and still feeling conversational, but the tide was too high to let us get in the cave door. And by the way the magic door is gone. Hina, the moon goodess, took it up to her domain.

We had wind and rain squalls all afternoon with bursts of sunshine between. Most of the showers we rode through, but seeing a half-built Church

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

285 nearby, during one downpour, we turned off the road for its shelter. It looked unfinished, but had a roof. We worked our way through head-high weeds which covered us with burrs. We dashed up a flight of cement steps to an open doorway and-space. It was the jumping off place. A deep, damp cellar lay nine feet below us, without a joist or bit of floor to stand on. We rode away in the rain and picked burrs from ourselves for the next two hours.

Some time before sunset I began to put in my arguments for finding beds for the night. "But it's pretty early to stop, honey." Harry objected. "I know, but I want to be sure to have a fullsized mattress tonight. I'd like a couple of them." "Why all this stir about mattresses? You used to sleep on the ground with just a blanket when we went hunting in California." "Yes, but ground is different. You can scrape out a gentle hollow for your hips to rest in, and build up an earthen pillow and get some shape to an earthen bed. You can't do that to a wooden floor. They're so terribly flat-even the best of them." "Well then," said Harry, "what about that time you slept on that cement floor, at Miami Beach, for several weeks. Was that a floor with hip holes. in it?" "Of course not. And the first two or three nights were awful, but after a while I got used to it. Your hips get sort of calloused or some-

thing if you stay with it long enough. But this trip will be only four nights out and I don't want to spend them all getting hardened up and calloused, and then when I get tough as nails we'll be back in our own beds again." "You're right, honey," said Harry. "I wouldn't want you to get all calloused. Not pretty. We'll ask for mattresses at the next house." So we did. 

I let Harry do all the talking with natives for he has outstripped me far in speaking Tahitian.

He learns new words all the time, working with 

Friday and Saturday, while I never got beyond the vocabulary I learned in the first few months down here.

A big, hairy man came out wearing a pareau. 

He was followed by several women and an assortment of children. When Harry offered to rent beds or mattresses, the man waved him down and told us he had nothing for rent, but that we were welcome to use two full-sized beds-the guest beds -with two mattresses each on them. "It's a bargain, honey. Take it." So Harry closed the deal. "And ask him if this is Haapape. He looks like the hairy man in the story." It turned out that the little stream flowing through the side yard was Haapape itself, so this must have been the very place to which Hina fled for protection, following the good advice of her lover's heart. "Quick, honey," for I was getting excited, "ask him if some of these girls are named Hina.” 

There were two cousins in the group, both

287 named from their ancestress. "Ask him if there are any Nonas." No, not one

Nona on tap. "He must be a direct descendant, don't you think? Most Tahitians have plenty of hair, but this one has something special in the hairy line. Look at that chest. Those legs."

We liked these Hinas. Their sisters and cousins, made themselves as pleasant as possible, helping unpack the horses, bringing drinking water and showing us to their cooking shack where we prepared our own supper of wheat mush and cocoa. 

Harry pedaled to the nearest store and bought eight loaves of bread and two kilos of sugar as a gift for the family since they wouldn't take money for our lodging. It is quite the style here for guests to take bread and sugar when they go visiting.

We had just finished our supper when a horseman, young and very English, rode in the front yard. He tried to look like a well-seasoned traveler; to act old and responsible, but nineteen was in his face. He was touring the island, he said, and seeing our horses and saddles thought this must be a regular stopping place for passersby. He was welcomed by our hairy host who got it in his head that the stranger was another member of our family, arriving a bit late. Since we all spoke the same language and came on horseback, how could it be otherwise? He showed the Englishman into the room where the two beds were, and asked if the five of us could put up in the two beds. The young man seemed satis-

fied and we could scarcely complain if our host wanted to be cordial to another guest. 

The Parsons group withdrew to consult about bed space. "I suppose the girls and I will have to squeeze into one bed," I said, "and you will have to share the other with the Britisher." "Don't want to share with a Britisher," Harry muttered. "I thought I was going to sleep with you." "Mother, why does daddy look so disgusted?" 

Peggy inquired. "He doesn't like to sleep with another man," I explained. "Don't men like to sleep together?" Polly asked innocently. "Well, not exactly. A man would rather sleep alone, or- "But we three are going to sleep together and we're not complaining," said Peggy. “I guess men are more fussy than women." Polly put the final touch on the matter when she stated sweepingly, "Well, Peggy you know women are more broadminded than men, anyway." 99 Eventually we got the beds portioned out and turned in.

Some hours later I awoke feeling cramped between Peggy and Polly. The mattresses were soft, but no matter which way I turned I ran into some portion of twin anatomy. I am not a fat woman, but in one way or another there is a good deal of me-too much for one-third of a bed.

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

289 Being in the middle I was too hot, though I knew the night was chilly. I sat up to cool off for a moment. Moonlight was streaming in a Western window near the other bed and the other bed was empty!

I wondered about that: "Probably it's almost morning and the Englishman has gotten off to an early start. I suppose Harry is out getting the horses watered and grassed. Maybe he won't come back to bed. He'll probably start the fire and cook breakfast. Well, here's where I get a whole bed to sprawl in for a half hour or so." I rose gently so as not to awaken the girls, slipped over to the empty bed and flopped in it, grateful for enough room to spread out.

The next thing I knew the room was quite dark and something was poking me in the ribs uncomfortably. A voice said, "Damn. Are all Englishmen such pigs with a bed? Get over on your own side." I was too sleepy to resent the prodding or to worry about being called 'pig' and 'Englishman'. I turned away from the elbow that poked me and slept again.

I don't know how long it was before I got a smart kick from the other side, and another voice said, "Damn. I cahn't get any sleep you know unless I have at least a small portion of bed to sleep on." A foot placed itself on my widest outspread leg and shoved it to the middle of the bed. 

I thought hazily, "Something's wrong here. This bed is getting just as crowded as the other one was. And it kicks more." But I rolled obligingly

in the other direction. A hoarse whisper there said " what's the matter with you? You 

Englishman!" That brought me wide awake, for in our seventeen years together, Harry has never been one to swear much around women. Was that Harry putting on such a lively show of cursing? If so, this was interesting. I would say nothing and see what happened. A hiss came back, "You 

Yankee, what the me all the do you mean rolling on night?" Now I was learning new words with an Oxford tang to them. "Get over on your own side of the bed." "Get over, your self, before I throw you out." So Harry could swear, and put his heart into it, too. We never know other people as well as we think we do. 

Then Harry's hand took me roughly by the shoulder and pushed hard, but his fingers caught in my long hair. "My God, it's a woman! He wasn't a woman when he went to bed last night." "What's that?" whispered the other side. “A woman? How many are in this bed anyway?" 

God knows," said Harry plaintively.

291 "Not so loud," warned Harry. "My wife is in that other bed. 

You'll wake her up, and she'll think —oh, my God! Get this native woman out of here quick. Take it away." Harry jumped out of the bed as if it had a snake in it. Harry stood silhouetted against the window where the first faint light of dawn was showing. 

His mouth sagged open. He looked helpless. 

Afterward he told me he was thinking, "If I wake the woman up and put her out, she'll make a fuss and wake Hester up. If I just run away,

Hester will wake up after a while and find the thing in my bed. A fine situation for a faithful husband." 

We heard the clatter of the Englishman's horse as he took to the road on a gallop. Harry gathered his courage and leaned over the bed. He touched me gingerly on one cheek with one finger. "Aita parau parau. Hare! Anita hinaara oe. 

Get out. Don't talk. I don't want you."
