But say, dear, how did you get over in this bed? 

You went to bed in that " "Oh, let's not talk, darling. Let's get a little more sleep before daylight. I'm so sleepy and we've only a few more minutes in a good bed where I can stretch out, and-"

Our way led over lesser cliffs the next day. All the windward side of the island had a rugged beauty more like that of a northern land. There are fewer coconuts and bananas to remind you of the South Seas. The wild breakers, rocky islets and gusty storms take you far from the Tropics.

We had worked the marching order down to a system which gave Boy and Zebra little chance to get within fighting range of each other. Bridges were no problem so long as Cream Puff took the lead, so our day was uneventful until we reached the river at Mahina where we met a flood which forced us to stop. The stream has no bridge, for the government has become weary of putting in bridges which get themselves washed out every year. A cement ford dips under the river for the accommodation of the trucks. The river, one of the largest on the island, was roaring over the cement which lay under three feet of swirling brown water.

We drew up on the bank, wondering whether to put our horses through the flood, when a Chinaman came from the other direction with his twowheeled cart. The horse tried to balk, but the driver lashed him into the water. At the deepest point the horse lost his footing and went down in

YOU COULD DO IT, TOOI a heap. The cart was swept downstream, dragging the struggling horse. The Chinaman cursed and called for help. The wagon grounded fifty yards downstream and natives set off in canoes to rescue the driver. The natives peeked into the bread box to see if the bread was ruined before they would help the man. The Chinaman climbed into the canoe, deserting his horse in midstream. 

Two daring men got ropes and let themselves out bit by bit with friends on shore holding the ends. They reached the horse and towed him to the bank as the friends pulled them in, hand over hand. The danger was not from the depth of the water so much as from the force of the current and the logs which whirled by on the flood. 

One such object lesson was enough for us: we decided to wait until the flood went down. There was a lean-to by the side of the road, the work shop of a native woman who wove pandannus hats. The next rain was upon us, so we retreated to the little shelter, and sat on the bamboo floor. 

The owner of the shed appeared and we arranged to rent it from her, plus two mattresses for the night. We stacked saddles and bedding in the shelter and felt quite snug.

When that particular rain was over we all started across a meadow, knee deep in water grass, to tie the horses to trees at a little distance. 

Harry was in the lead with Boy. The twins and I followed with the mares. A shout from some natives made us look around. 

They were yelling and gesticulating but I couldn't understand what

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

295 they were trying to tell us. I turned to ask Harry if he had understood, but to our surprise he had disappeared. The field was open without a tree or hill for a hiding place. Boy was dancing and snorting. I hurried up to catch him, but couldn't see Harry. "Where's daddy?" asked Peggy. "Honey, where are you?" I was calling. 

Polly arrived. "Where did daddy go?" "Look, mother, there's daddy's face. Where's the rest of you, daddy?"

Daddy's face was just appearing among the water grass at our feet. "For Heaven's sake, what are you doing down there, dear?" "Trying to get out of this confounded hole. 

Don't come too close or you'll be in it, too. The natives were yelling about it when I stepped right in it." Harry didn't swear -much-but he had the look of one who is thinking bad words.

It was an old well, not very deep, but plenty wet and so overgrown with water grass as to be invisible. Harry struggled out, muddy and dripping, with grass decorations on head and shoulders. We got a fire going and started a drying process which lasted until dark with Harry wrapped in a blanket.

The flood was lessening a little. These short mountain streams get excited easily but soon calm down again. As the afternoon wore on native men began to cross the stream on foot, leaning against the sweep of the water. Presently boys

came for play. They launched themselves in the current holding to pieces of driftwood, and bobbed down the stream, splashing and shrieking. They found footing on a gravel bank built by the stream near the sea beach, and made their way to shore. Racing and yelling they ran back to the cement and plunged in again. 

When the owner of the shack saw that we were cooking supper she brought us a cooking pot, a lantern and a china pitcher painted with large pink roses. We had no use for any of them, but she insisted that we should use them so we did the best we could. We filled the pitcher with water from the stream and then boiled it in the pit. When it cooled we used it for washing feet before going to bed. So sanitary-sterile water for foot baths!

When we were ready to serve our supper, the woman came with a small round table and a flour sack tablecloth. Her daughter brought two chairs, apologizing because she didn't have more. The woman and her husband came, almost out of sight under two huge kapok mattresses. Our little shack was so well furnished there was scarcely room for us to sneeze in, but we slept soundly without anything worse than a friendly black cat that thought we were there for the purpose of giving him a good bed in the bend of our knees. 

But a cat was much better than stray goats, maniac Chinamen or Englishmen. 

It was just a day's ride to Taravao then down our side of Tahiti-Iti to our home. Cream Puff

ISLAND MERRY-GO-ROUND 

297 had lost fifty pounds of her cream and butter-fat, but her waistline was still something better than a 'stylish stout'. We came in on a brisk trot feeling like people in a Zane Grey novel, tanned, lean and cowboyish. We were tempted to yell, "Yip, Yip, Yi! Get along little dogies!" and make wide motions like throwing lassoes and shooting up the town.

We had got ourselves hardened up to a real journey. All soreness was gone from our muscles.

We talked yearningly of a month or so in the saddle, but when you live on a small island it's no use to yen for thousand mile trips. As Harry reminded us, that would mean going around the loop we had just completed like a merry-go-round. 

Fun the first time, but deadly after a dozen rides.

PAPENOO VALLEY. 

"Cowboy Wanted!" Bow and arrow gunmen 

The "Clingers" had a union. Pagan gods demanded "long pig." Cracker box houses.

The water wall. Do we want to live in Papenoo? 

Harry's fame as a buckaroo spread among the natives. Eventually it reached the ears of an American who has lived here many years and become a French citizen. He wished to buy Papenoo, the largest valley on the island. The valley had cows in it and the buyer would need a cowboy, so Harry was sent for and propositioned.

This was the situation: The American was bargaining for the valley and all the stock in it for a certain price. He didn't know how many acres the valley comprised, neither did he know how much livestock was in it. It would take a great many days to go over the entire valley and all its branching valleys for they are a tangle of purau and jungle growth of all sorts, with comparatively little open range.

The whole thing, roughly called Papenoo, makes up about one-fifth of Tahiti-Nui: a great basin surrounded by majestic peaks, an amphitheatre in the mountains. The ancient name was Vai- 

Tu-Oru, "Waters-that-are-swollen," but the modern name is almost as good, meaning, "Confluent Waters".

Such is the valley. The stock is cattle and pigs,

PAPENOO VALLEY 

299 once domesticated, but long since run wild and "gone back to the brush". The cattle crash through the thick growth and disappear at the distant approach of man. One may be in the valley for weeks without catching a glimpse of the wild cows and their calves. The aim of the prospective buyer, was to clear out all the old stock in Papenoo, restock the valley with a good breed of cattle and have them cared for so they wouldn't "go native". "There's feed in Papenoo for a thousand head," the hopeful man told Harry. "How many are there now?” "Maybe 300. Maybe 500. Nobody knows. And they've been in there so long they don't look like real cows any more. Something like bison. Heavy, strong shoulders for climbing these mountains and light, spindly hind quarters without any beefsteaks on 'em to speak of. They're all undersized from years of inbreeding." "It must be a sight," said Harry, "when they go stampeding through the purau thickets." "It's a sight if you can get close enough to see it. But don't get in their way. And don't walk around alone. They'll attack a person on foot.

Natives go in bunches and take their half-wild dogs with them." "Got any corrals up there to run 'em into?" "Not a thing. The natives just go slipping around till they locate a bunch and get their dogs after them. Then they drive 'em up in one of the blind canyons. The dogs drag the cows down

and the natives finish them with knives. Not a pretty system. Your job would be to build corrals, get natives and dogs to help you run 'em in, then shoot 'em. The Papeete market could take about five or six a week until we get 'em all cleaned out." "I'm used to working from horseback," said 

Harry, "on open range. Working cows through a jungle would be different, I guess." "Well, go up and look the place over anyway. 

Let me know what you think of the proposition. 

There's a house for you up there, 16 kilometers from the road. Every board, nail and piece of tin roofing was packed up on natives' backs-foot trail all the way." "Could my wife and I make it up there on horses?" "I guess so. I went up on foot once, as far as the cabin. But go while the dry season's on or you might get caught by a flood and held in there two weeks. You have to cross the river 28 times." "How deep is the river?" 

"When it's feeling tame it comes about to your waistline and deeper in the pools. When it's mad you can't tell how deep it is it goes too fast!

The valley used to be the hide-out for the outlaws of the island in the old days. The river floods were a protection to them because the local policemen, in pursuit, wouldn't know the best places to cross. There was a whole district up there with a chief "king" they called him. All the bad eggs 

---

PAPENOO VALLEY 

301 gathered up there, criminals wanted by the government, deserters from the army and slackers escaping conscription by their chiefs." "But look here, man! I can't take my wife and daughters up to live in a place like that." "Nobody lives there now. Your neighbors will be wild cows and boars. Old King Teta has been tucked away in his rock tomb for many a year. 

The natives around Papenoo still tell the tales their forefathers told, of the great feasts when old Teta died. He had been the king of the rogues for a long time. His gang of cut-throats was afraid of him for he was heavy-handed on the punishments, but they respected him 'cause they knew what a job it was to keep the bunch in order. 

And old Teta wouldn't stand for any foolin'. But they say he was good to poor people." "Something of a Tahitian Robin Hood, eh?" "I guess so. They called the place "Te Piha Ia

Teta'. That means "The-Room-Of-Refuge' with king Teta. It might of been a refuge from their own rightful chiefs, but there was no refuge from Teta. He was the boss with a capital 'B'. He had a few faithful lieutenants and some husky strong arm boys who used all the force necessary to run things the way Teta wanted. Called themselves "The Clan of the Strong'. Sometimes old Teta killed a fellow who wouldn't buckle under to his rule. Sometimes he just escorted him back to his own chief in disgrace, saying, he wouldn't have such a blackguard in his mountain outfit. That was pretty hard for the man who had walked out

on his chief, because the chief took revenge and took plenty. The culprit was turned over to the pagan priests and they knew what to do with him. They always needed 'long pig"." "Long pig?" "Men to be sacrificed to the Polynesian gods." "You're sure they're not up there yet? Or their grandchildren? I'll take a chance on wild cows and pigs," said Harry, "but I don't want to get mixed up with wild men-nor any wild women either." "All that Papenoo stuff ended when the French government got a good hold on the island." "What happened to old Teta?" "He died, and was embalmed native style, then wrapped in tapa to preserve him like a mummy, and hoisted up into one of those tombs along the mountain cliff where they used to keep their important people. When you go to Papenoo valley take a look high up along the ridges. You'll see little black dots that look like pigeon holes from a distance. Those are the old burial caves. 

You can't get up to them though." "Gosh, I don't want to." Harry was emphatic. "Mummies and old kings and their "Tupapau' can just stay right where they are. I'll never disturb 'em. "The natives 'round Papenoo village down on the road still talk about the old days-especially the food. Of course they weren't there to know about it, but the stories are passed down in the families. They had a saying that in Papenoo

PAPENOO VALLEY 

303 'breadfruit was food for rats' because it was so plentiful in the great valley. But there's scarcely a tree left now. The old ones have died out and young ones never get a chance to grow with cows around. The cows eat the young shoots coming up from the old tree roots just as soon as they put a leaf above ground." "Who put the cows up there?" "I don't know. It was all a long time ago. 

Maybe the first cows strayed from the village and couldn't be found and started the herd. Some natives will tell you that the first cows were "Tupapau' cows-spirits, you know. Sent there to kill the breadfruit so people couldn't live there any more. Sort of a punishment to the valley for harboring all the desperate characters in the old days. "We'll go and have a look at the place next week if it doesn't rain, and see how we like the ghost cows." "O. K. I'll tell a couple of native fellows to be on the lookout for you in the village and show you the way up the mountain. You couldn't find the trail by yourself. And say, be sure to have them take you to the old cave." "No thanks! I'm not going to stir up any mummies." "Not the burial caves. I mean another one, close to the house where a bunch of the outlaws used to sleep, guarding the valley. It's where you come up out of the river for the last time and get into the more open part of the valley."

"You say I can't get to those other caves. don't want to, but why couldn't I if I wanted to?"

Harry rather fancies himself as a mountaineer. "Well, those burial caves were discovered centuries ago by the rock-clingers, and-" "Who the heck were they?" "The fellows who were especially trained to climb the cliffs and precipices. They had the job of gathering little red feathers from the wild ducks that lived in the lakes up among the peaks. 

You see the kings and chiefs wanted the little feathers for their special court robes. They were used on the altars, too. But the ducks wouldn't come down to be plucked so the rock-clingers had to go up. A sort of rock-clingers' union was organized. The fathers trained their sons to follow in their footsteps. The young hopefuls were freed from all other kinds of work. Their job was just rock-clinging, but pretty hard clinging sometimes. The experienced men trained the youngsters on the easier climbs first. Then gave them more difficult clings as their skill increased. There were some fatalities of course, but life's like that everywhere. A youth became a full-fledged rock-clinger when he could make the grade up the highest peak of all, old Oro-Hena. That cling was Tahiti's highest scramble. It's more than 

7000 feet to the top of the crater, and a lot of it straight up. Easy for the ducks, but a stiff climb for a youngster even after being trained from babyhood. If he reached the top of the old cone he was graduated from the clinger's school. If

PAPENOO VALLEY 

305 he couldn't make it he was sent back on some easier 'clings' to learn more and then brought up again for examination, later." "Do natives go up there today?" "No. The little red feathers are out of style now. No one wants them bad enough to go after them and the rock-clingers union broke up long ago. Your modern chief wants a pith helmet and a pink silk shirt from America and let the ducks wear the feathers. It was the rock-clingers who took the mummies up the cliffs and put them in the caves. No native would go there today even if he could -afraid of "Tapapau'." "Papenoo must have plenty of spirits in it, between the ghost cows and the rock-clingers who died in the attempt and all the mummies up in the pigeon holes. Nice ghostly place to live, miles from the nearest neighbor. It's a good thing we don't believe in ghosts."

When all this conversation was relayed to me I was on tiptoes for the trip. Harry and I arranged with our American neighbors for bed and board for the girls and princess Palico. We wanted to try out a new horse, one we thought we might buy, so we borrowed him for the trip to see if he were a bridge-idiot. He was larger than the usual run of island horses, with a strain from an Australian grandsire who had been imported by the government to improve the stock here. Being slightly crossed-eyed, he was promptly named 

Cock-Eye-De-Loco. But he turned out to be not so loco as his name; a steady worker, quiet on the

fiercest bridges, he had an easy running walk which matched Cream Puff's single foot rather nicely. Cock-Eye was a gentleman, always, so we had none of those stirring times we had with Boy and Zebra feuding round-the-island. 

Harry and I rode 23 miles the first day around the windward side of the island. At Mahaena, the 'close-swimming-islet', we saw surf riding for the first time in Tahiti. It is almost a dead sport here, though there are old stories about it which make us believe it was once a common pastime.

A group of native men and girls were competing for a prize offered by a Frenchman who wished to see the old sport kept alive. He was cheering enthusiastically for a girl who was the most skilled in the group.

The Frenchman explained the fine points of the sport to us and regretted that his health would not allow him to take part in it. "This," and he waved toward the group of brown people in the surf, "reminds me of the old tale of the demi-god 

Turi. According to Tahitian legend, Turi and his wife came to this same beach for surf riding. His wife was the most famous of all the gods and goddesses at this sport. Such a myth from the past shows that at some time the women were more adept at the game than men. And it seems to be the same way here today. girl! Her poise and balance!'' 

Look at that 

We left him exclaiming and applauding and went on to the home of friends for the night.

PAPENOO VALLEY 

307 We were under way early next morning. The stormy weather of the windward side of the island followed us and wet us a good many times during the day, but the brilliant sunshine which came on the heels of every shower dried our clothing quickly. A brisk breeze made the horses lively. We passed through the district of Ti'Arei which was famous for its man-killers in ancient times. Just as the people of a certain region may become famous for a special type of work, so those of Ti'Arei had been famous for man-slaying. Their job was to secure the 'long-pig' for religious sacrifices.

The slayers from this district were taken into other districts as favorites of the chiefs. The chief or priest would point out a victim. The killer would shadow him and creep close while the unsuspecting man was at his work. A smart blow from behind with a heavy club would crush a skull and earn the gratitude of the pagan gods. 

An old adage of Tahiti ran, "Tis dangerous to crack the shells of the turbo in the districts near 

Ti'Arei, because the man-slayers will hear and creep up for their appointed work." We were glad that Christianity had done away with that system of gathering ‘long pig'.

We crossed the bridge over Papenoo river early in the afternoon. The village strings along on each side of the road for a mile, with its church, himine house, school, teacherage, Chinese store, village pump, like a fire hydrant, chief's house and some twenty or thirty native homes. The

people of the village were on the lookout for the 'cowboy' and his vahine. We were directed to the home of the native named Ari who was to guide us up into the great valley. His home was of the kind favored by every native who can afford it a big lumber box with a corrugated iron roof.

During the World War copra prices soared because explosives were made from coconut oil.

Many natives got unexpectedly rich. Some drank their fortunes up but others succumbed to the craze of that period, cracker box houses with tin roofs and an absurd amount of gingerbread work, following the French style of a by-gone day.

There was an epidemic- one might say a scourge -of building these atrocities. They were built by native carpenters unhindered by any notions of proportion, line or beauty. Probably they were not so unsightly back in 1916 when they were new and painted. They must have looked fresh then, at any rate. Nearly a quarter century has passed since they were built and few have been repaired or re-painted in that time. They are grimy with the accumulated grease and dirt of many hands. 

Their tin roofs are spotted brown with rust. Doors sag or are missing. Windows are broken and stuffed with rags. Floor boards on porches are rotting away. Pillars lean crazily. All windows and doors are placed exactly in the middle of each wall space, a sure death to beauty, and each room is exactly square. All windows are small, shutting out the sea breeze. The hot sun on the tin roofs

PAPENOO VALLEY 

309 make the little rooms unbearably hot. 

These houses are horrors compared with the niao houses with thatched roofs which follow the ancient style of building. The niao houses have large 'opens' which let in the breeze and a thatched roof is the coolest thing possible. The bird cage houses of up-bamboo haven't such large 'opens' (sometimes none at all) yet they are well ventilated for every crack between the bamboo poles does its bit letting in air. It will be a blessing to the island and a relief to the eyes when all these decaying frame boxes crumble and are replaced by thatch houses. A blessing from my viewpoint, not from the natives'. Any Tahitian would rather live in the most dilapidated frame house a white man's house than a freshlymade picturesque thatch.

His choice is not made on the grounds of convenience or health for the box horrors have no running water, stoves or plumbing. They are simply wooden shells. The choice is made by the native on a basis of prestige. He feels important living in the worst shack made of lumber but works up an inferiority complex in the finest niao house. We are looked upon rather scornfully by our native neighbors, because we continue to live in a niao house instead of building a box with a tin roof.

The house of Ari, to which we were directed, was one of the worst. Ari and nine lean hounds were scratching their fleas on the front porch.

Ari rose and received us as into a castle. He told

us we couldn't start up the mountain until morning. So we picketed the horses out and amused ourselves with sea bathing and wandering on the beach. We found a pretty spot, built a fire and cooked our supper. Our bed was a mattress on the floor of Ari's front room. We were glad we had our own bedding.

In the morning he served us coffee and bread in the cooking shack near his house. We saddled and were off for Papenoo. Back through the mile-long village we went. Ari and his pal carried a lantern and some warm clothing for night use for they wore pareaus only. A boy came along and the half starved hounds got under the horses feet and made a great clamor, bragging to the other dogs of the village about their prospective trip. Their friends came to see them off and joined the procession till we had an escort of seventeen dogs. Men and boys followed, too. . . we looked like a mob. At the Chinese store Harry bought sugar and many tins of salmon. The

Chinese woman, in her black pants and coat, made up a bundle of fifteen loaves of bread. The fish, sugar and bread were for Ari and his pal. Harry and I had cocoa and dried fruit and whole wheat for mush.

When we entered the river for our first crossing the villagers waved us goodbye and made their part of the dog escort stay at home. The first pools were clear and pretty, more than belly deep on the horses so I began by pulling my feet up fastidiously to keep my sandals and slacks dry. I

PAPENOO VALLEY 

311 didn't know then that before morning was over I would be in the stream to my neck a dozen times.

The horses slipped on the rounded stones of the river bed. They couldn't keep up with the native men who used poles to help them stand against the current. The nine hounds launched themselves into the sweep of the water, yipping excitedly and swimming with their tails straight out like rudders. They knew where they were going and knew that a trip to Papenoo meant a feast on wild pig.

Following a bit of trail on the other side of the river we overtook the men by trotting our horses. 

And so it went all morning the men getting ahead at every one of the 28 crossings and the horses catching up on the trail between, and always working up and down toward the main valley. When Harry had first told me we would have to cross the river so many times I had been a bit scornful, saying, "But what's the sense of crossing the same river back and forth that way?" Now I found out. In many places sheer cliffs came to the river's edge and dropped out of sight in still green pools. Then the natives sought the least troublesome ford and in we went again, slipping and sliding on the water-worn boulders to the other side.

On the eleventh crossing, a particularly nasty one, Cream Puff lost her footing. She fell forward and I pitched over her head, Flying-Dutchman style, and landed in four feet of water. 

Cream Puff got to her feet and stood trembling.

After that she was so nervous about the crossings that I walked and led her. Or was it I who was nervous about pitching off again? Anyway we were both happier with the new arrangement, so 

I rode only between the fords. I found I couldn't walk upright as Ari did or I fell constantly, so I straddled along, more or less on all fours much to the amusement of the natives. They seemed sure of their footing on the slickest rocks. When I reached the deeper pools it was easier to squat down in them and stride forward with big steps while the water supported my weight. Harry laughed at my straw hat bobbing along, followed by Cream Puff. I would liked to have seen it myself.

Half way up the river we began to get glimpses of the great peaks above us... some of those the rock-clingers used to scale. To them no problem, perhaps, but to me it looked impossible. At this point Ari's pal and the boy took the dogs and disappeared in the jungle to hunt wild pigs. Later we heard them high up the mountain side, the dogs baying on the trail of pig, the man and boy shouting as they followed. During the rest of the climb we heard the dogs occasionally but couldn't see them or the men. On we went. The twenty-eighth crossing, at last! Out of the river we came, onto a steep grassy bank and saw the house where we were supposed to live. 

We had been six hours on the way, six hours of strenuous effort to get from one's home to the nearest store. Six hours to get down to the truck

PAPENOO VALLEY 

313 in case of illness or a broken leg, to say nothing of the chance of getting caught by a flood at any point along the way. Caught between crossings, you couldn't go forward or backward. In a week of rain you would be trapped in the wilderness without food or shelter. Did we want to live up here? 

The view from the house was overwhelming. 

The great cliff facing us rose 2000 feet, almost perpendicular. From all sides dark mountains stared down on us till we felt the size of ants. We found the house to be not a home, but a camp for natives who might come here to round up cattle. 

Wooden bunks were built in layers to sleep fifty natives. The front porch was taken up by a big table cemented in the floor and large enough to seat half a hundred people. 

We changed to dry clothes and cooked dinner. 

Ari ate three loaves of bread and a tin of salmon.

When he could walk about again, he showed us to the cave nearby. It was large enough for a score of men to sleep in. The walls were black from the smoke of many fires. We imagined the outlaws of king Teta's time living here, guarding this narrow entrance to the great valley, where all comers could be stopped for inspection and the password.

It began to rain in a determined way. Ari looked worried. He crouched over a smoky fire in the cooking shed, shivering in his wet pareau. He peered at the thick clouds which had gathered suddenly and looked anxiously at the river.

"Bime'by plenty water. No good." Said Ari. 

The other man and the boy arrived in the downpour, the man bent under a load a wild pig which dripped blood and mud down his bare back. 

The lean dogs circled the fire, shivering, and looked expectant. They had got a taste of meat and blood for they had caught the sow and pulled her down before the man had caught up with them. They had gnawed a hole in one ham. A foreleg was riddled by teeth and the throat was chewed to ribbons where the dogs had got a death hold.

The sow had done her share of the fighting, too. 

She had ripped the foreleg of one dog with her three inch tusks. One hound was limping with a mashed toe and all had minor cuts and scratches inflicted by the fighting sow, or by rocks and underbrush. The hounds dug shallow hollows in the earthen floor of the shack near the fire and sat in them licking their wounds and whining with hunger and excitement. The men brought dry wood from under the house and made a blaze which drove the dogs back, their hides steaming. 

Ari's companion had disemboweled the sow on the mountain. He explained to us that she had 'got baloon' and the dogs had gulped the litter of half-formed piglets she had been carrying. Then men held the sow over the blaze. The coarse hair burned off with a great stench and hissing of steam. Throwing the pig down for a moment, the men put rocks in the fire to heat. The hounds sniffed the pig with its delicious odor of burned

PAPENOO VALLEY 

315 hide eyed the men and retired to their hollows with saliva stringing from their mouths. They trembled and licked their chops. They knew every step in the process of native pig cookery.

We weren't so sure about what would happen next so we followed the men, in the rain, down to the river bank. They stood in water to their waists with the pig between them, scraping the hide clean of soot and stray hairs. Presently one of the men lifted his head and listened, yelled to the other and they were both on the bank in an instant with the pig. They pointed up stream where the water roared around the curve of the canyon. "Plenty water come," Ari yelled.

There seemed to be plenty already, green and foamy, hurdling rocks and singing a wild way to the sea, but that was nothing to what was coming. We heard an extra roar, dull, low and rather frightening. Then around the rocky corner came a brown-yellow wall of water; I won't try to say how many feet high. I was in no state of mind to judge it calmly. Anyway, I retreated farther up the bank as it swept past like a great brown carpet being rapidly unrolled, covering the green and white of the original stream. If you were unlucky enough to be in the path of a steam roller like that, you might come out ‘flat and slightly curved', but you wouldn't come out from under that water wall till you reached the sea, perhaps with choice bits of you left at each of the 28 crossings.

Back at the cook house the men tested the temperature of the rocks by the good old fashioned method of spitting on them to see if they sizzled. 

They did. Ari opened an old roasting pit in a corner of the shed. The men plucked the hot rocks from the fire with quick flips and paved the pit. In went the sow, whole. Banana leaves sheeted her and a few old sacks made a blanket for her. Over all went more big leaves, then earth. "Two hours -finnie!" Ari told us.

It would be against all the rules of the cook books and the Good Housekeeping Institute to roast a hundred pound sow in two hours, so we were skeptical. Ari made a mysterious trip into a side valley below the cave and came back with a load of large, green bamboo poles. While the pig cooked, both men busied themselves cutting the bamboo into yard lengths, leaving a joint partition at the bottom of each big tube. When they finished they had thirty tubes ready, but for what purpose we had no idea. 

Just before sunset the rain stopped and spectacular orange clouds floated among the purple peaks, as vivid as a stage backdrop. On the face of the great cliff opposite twenty-one separate waterfalls had sprung into being like so many white ribbons fluttering against the black rocks. 

The heights were gleaming with recent rain and a 'clinger' up there at the moment would have to be good indeed. One false step would end his career as feather-gatherer for the king!

PAPENOO VALLEY 

317 During the half hour we watched, the slender waterfalls ran their courses and one by one dwindled into nothingness. The cliffs were so nearly vertical that the most resolute waterfall couldn't last long since there were no basins at the top to retain water. Such falls as these come and go a thousand times a summer in Papenoo, following each tropical rain. 

Ari came out to look at the sky knowingly and prophesied a clear, cold night with sunshine on the morrow. We hoped he knew as much about the weather as he pretended so we could go farther in to the big valley kingdom the next day. 

At dark the men opened the oven and pulled the pig out. The dogs quivered and smiled. The pig looked surprisingly 'done' but we couldn't believe it until we ate some. We stuffed ourselves. The dogs had a feast on bones and skin oozing with grease. When Ari and his pal couldn't stow away any more pig, they cut meat up in small chunks and packed it in the bamboo tubes. They covered the tops of the tubes with clean leaves tied on securely with purau fibre. When all the tubes were filled they were placed in rows in the oven which was still very hot. Then they took the leaf sheets and the sack blankets for a warm night's rest, which was more than we got. Ari was right about the clear cold night. 3000 feet above the sea, even our wool blankets couldn't keep us warm. I don't see how the natives slept at all. Probably their skins are conditioned by constant exposure to sun, wind and rain. Rolled

tightly in their mats, heads and all, they looked like three enormous sausages. 

Ari missed his guess about the sunshine next day for it started to drizzle at dawn. The river had subsided during the night so fording it would be possible if we got out before more heavy rain came. Ari and his friend studied the sky and consulted for a long time, finally deciding that we should leave while we had the chance to. They fastened the pork containers to the ends of carrying poles. They would sell the pork right in the bamboo tube in the Papeete market. Their loads were not heavy compared with the burdens of fei they bring from the mountains, but most any load would be too much for a white man on the slippery stones of the 28 crossings. 

Ari listened carefully before going into each ford on the look-out for another water wall, but if any more walls came down that day they didn't catch us in the way. By one o'clock we were back in the village. Our hounds were greeted like long lost brothers by their dog friends. We cooked dinner and back-tracked that same afternoon to our friends.

We hadn't seen a ghost cow, a mummy or the big part of Papenoo but still we felt as though we had been somewhere. And we had decided not to take a cowboy job that would mean putting 

Polly and Peggy into such a wilderness. Vairao, we thought, was primitive enough for the simple life.

Peggy and polly in their outrigger canoe. Peggy is seated. She is fifteen years old. Polly is fifteen years and fifteen minutes.

Ours was to be a reuniun-and now? Have we solved problems coming to Tahiti-or created more and worse problems? 

Four years! We can scarcely realize time in our island home. Sometimes it seems as though we left America but yesterday. Again it seems we have been here almost forever. Time plays tricks on you down here. Days and weeks slip away astonishingly. There's no morning paper to keep us posted on the date. The radio news sheet arrives one, two or three days late, according to when the native policeman sees fit to deliver it, so we can't depend on it to find out the day of the week or month. There's no factory or office schedule to set Wednesday apart from Thursday.

No routine to keep Thursday from tangling with Friday.

Occasionally we ask each other in a puzzled way, "What day is today?" 

Harry will say, "Let's see, I made the last trip up the valley to see the cows on Monday. Or was it Tuesday? No, it must have been Monday, 'cause the truck comes out later on Mondays and I heard it tooting as I went up through the plantation. 

Then next day I hoed weeds in the tarua. So today must be Wednesday."

"But how could it be, dear? You know we were to take the tourist girls from New Zealand up the mountain to the waterfall on Thursday. And that trip can't be tomorrow because they were here this morning and spoke as if the picnic were two or three days off." "Maybe they are getting fooled on the days, too." 

We appeal to Peggy and Polly for help. "Girls, what day is this?" "Tuesday," says Peggy confidently, "because we started school again yesterday, and wrote about the invention of the cotton gin. Don't you remember, mother?"

But Polly states with equal firmness, "It's Wednesday, because the natives went by this morning carrying pigs and fei for their feast tomorrow, and you said the feast would be on 

Thursday the feast to go with the prize barrel of wine they won in the canoe race, two weeks ago.' 

We look hopefully toward the calendar which hangs on the wall. A big calendar sent to us from 

America. It is difficult for us to decipher the 

French and Chinese calendars which the stores give out down here. But even the plainest calendar can't help when you don't know the date or the day of the week to get started from. 

Mother Angel comes by and we hail her, "What's the date, mother Angel?" "Well, let me see. The next boat is supposed to arrive on the 18th, isn't it?"

"Yes. But it will probably be late again." "Let's look at the calendar." Mother Angel ruminates. "Today must be about the 14th. I'm going in to meet the boat this time and I planned to go on the Thursday truck, so today must" "Excuse me, but what do you mean by the 

Thursday truck? Early Thursday morning, or 

Thursday near midnight?" "Well, Thursday night, unless the truck is late as usual and then it will be early Friday morning." "Then Friday is boat day the 18th. Well the calendar agrees with that, but what is today?" "Well, it must be Tuesday, or maybe Wednesday." And after such terrific mental strain we are bogged down in the days just as much as ever. Taihai appears. 

It's Thursday." Taihai states positively. "Harry, I work you three days." "Mercy me!" says mother Angel, "if it's Thursday, I've got to get home and make ready for town." .

But Taihai has already recanted his belief in Thursday and upholds Peggy on her claim of 

Tuesday. But he willingly switches to Polly's 

Wednesday idea as he remembers what he came for-to tell Harry he wouldn't work the next day because he was going to the feast. We settle the matter by asking Peppy, the Chinese baker next door. He must keep track of the days for on 

Saturdays he bakes doughnuts 'extra', and on 

Sundays, layer cakes.

The time of day is just as elusive. Harry's watch, our alarm clock, mother Angel's clock (which came from a ship and strikes 'eight bells' and 'two bells' instead of hours) and the octagon wall clock at the store, all run according to their own consciences and never agree. It's good that we don't really need agreement. We find that when our time pieces run down we can set them best by watching for old papa Poria to go home to his dinner. He works in his vanilla patch, watches the sun and stumps home on his swollen feet, groaning at the labor of carrying his elephantiasis around. Many natives have a marvelous time sense, more accurate than our clocks which seem to be always gaining, losing or unconscious.

We have to keep a cool head to keep our feet steady among the months and seasons. It's still a little startling to hear someone remark "How short the days are getting. It's midwinter already July," and we look at the calendar to reassure ourselves that it is not January. The longer we stay the less confidence we have in rainy and dry seasons. What should we call it when a month's drouth burns up our watermelons when the rainy season is supposed to be on full blast? Or when cloud bursts, torrents and floods split the dry-season-myth wide open? Thinking about the seasons or planning for them is a waste of time here. 

If it were not for the girl's future, Harry and I would stay here for the rest of our lives and let

the 'land of the free and home of the brave' rock along as best it could without us. But young girls pretty white girls need something more than Tahiti can offer. They need a chance at a higher education if they wish it; an opportunity to try their wings at economic independence; travel, and a more intimate knowledge of their homeland; meetings with the right sort of young 

American men, for their future should hold married happiness if their lives are to be complete. It was but yesterday that Peggy remarked naively, "I shall teach my daughters while they are still young, that it won't matter whether their husbands are handsome or not, but they should. be kind and faithful like daddy." "And I'll teach mine," said Polly, "that the

Apollos are apt to be spoiled by too many girls, and be flighty, like so many of the white men here. "Oh," chimed in Peggy, "I shall marry an

American man whom I can be proud of. I mean when I finish college and travel and work awhile. 

Then I am going to marry a buckeroo like daddy and have twins."

We all laughed at that because it recalled a day in camp many years ago when the twins had been bubbling with talk of brides and weddings. 

Two of their teachers had resigned to get married the previous year. Interest ran high among the girls. They had seen the brides in all their white satin finery. For months the dormitories had been full of pictures of brides with

orange blossoms and grooms sporting gardenias.

The next winter, both ex-teachers had entertained the girls at parties, and proudly displayed their new babies. Peggy and Polly were impressed. 

They came to me with anxious looks. "Mother, did you ever get married?" "Of course. Daddy and I were married long ago." "In a church with flowers?" "No, dear." "In a garden with vines over some sticks?" "No." 

They looked worried. Then hopefully, "Oh, in a home with your family 'round?" "Well, no. In an office." They were profoundly disappointed. "But, Mother, didn't the minister wear a black dress with a white collar?" "No, dear. It was a dreadful' hot day and he had his collar open at the neck. But I remember he slipped on his coat while he married us. I recall the sheriff's badge on his coat." "Wasn't he even a preacher, Mother?" "No, just an officer -a Justice of the Peace." 

Something very irregular here! They went away to talk it over. Presently they returned, hand in hand. "Did Daddy have a flower in his coat?" "Oh, no. You see it was far out on the desert" "Mother, did you wear orange blossoms on your head?"

"No. They would have looked queer with a cowboy hat- 99 "Did you wear a satin dress Mother?" I was slipping down and down in their estimation. Peggy was still hopeful: "Mother did you have little slippers with shiny white things on them?" "No, I was wearing high boots." They probably thought the marriage wasn't legal by that time. 

They disappeared for another consultation. Presently it was: "Mother, why didn't you have a baby, like the teachers at school?" "Why I did have. a baby. Two of them. I had you girls right away." I was up to standard on that point, but Polly looked more worried than ever. "But, mother, I mean a real baby. We're twins. 

That's different." 

At last Polly found a solution. "Now we know," she beamed. Get married in church and have a baby. Get married in an office and have twins!"

Both girls are taking a long view of their case for they have said many times "Daddy, you may sell our ranch in California, but please don't ever sell our Tahiti plantations. Maybe we will get tired of living in civilization and long for our island home. Maybe our husbands will want to come here to live, then we'll have the land for our children." So we promise to hold their share of 

Eden for the future.

In about two years the girls should be boarding a steamer outbound for America. A year at

school, then college, then employment for them. we hope, with a period of earning their own money so they will know the value of dollars and cents. If they become self-supporting their lives shall be their own to use as they see fit. Perhaps the more primitive life of Tahiti will lose its appeal for them in the whirl of up-to-the-minute America. 

When Peggy and Polly start for the States there will be a hundred questions for us to answer as best we can. Shall they go alone? Shall I go with them, leaving Harry to batch for himself? 

Shall we all go? If we desert the plantations here they will be weed-covered in a month, brushcovered in half a year and in three years they will be a purau tangle which will take months to comb out. We don't know anyone, brown or white, who would take care of the land as Harry does. If Harry stays here alone, how long before he will be seized with such madness to see his family that he will board the first North-bound steamer, reckless of consequences? How long would I be satisfied in the States without him?

Or how long could I be happy in Tahiti without the girls? And what does Harry want? First, he wants the greatest happiness for his family, and second he wishes to live here the rest of his life. What about Peggy and Polly? America is a long way from Tahiti. Would the girls feel lonely in the States with daddy and mother six or nine thousand miles away?

Then there will be the money problem all over

The Twins Today

We are self-supporting here, but that is all. There is no surplus to help get a foothold in America again. If Harry goes back could he get a job or is everything closed to men over forty? Could I find a teaching position after six or seven years out of the schools? Our expenses will be great: school, college, necessary transportation for the girls and for us to hunt work. If Harry stays in 

Tahiti he can send us only a little money, for the income from the plantations is small. 

Polly and Peggy are keenly aware of these problems, of our happiness and our finances, for we discuss them in a family group. They are ambitious to become self-supporting. "When we get jobs," they say, "we will share our money with you, daddy, and we'll send mother some money every month."

They are doing their bit, now, for they have a little fund started toward college and add to it when they can. They have saved most of the francs they have earned at their daily chores, washing dishes, sweeping and making beds. When we had our pigs they bought four little piglets with their savings and when the porkers were sold they made a clear gain for daddy didn't charge any of the overhead to them.

Within the last year they have found another source of revenue in making and selling purses fashioned from coconut fiber. Taihai climbs the young trees and cuts the sheaths from the new leaves. It is a strong material woven by nature, about the texture of heavy linen and a pale gold-

en-brown in color. Each piece comes curled around a young leaf and when cut off is about a foot wide and three feet long. Then comes 

Peggy's and Polly's work. They soak the sheathes overnight in water. The next day they lay them on the kitchen table and scrub them with warm water and a brush, then spread them on the grass to dry. They plan the shape of the purse, the style, and cut the fiber. Then comes the part they enjoy most of all, the painting of the design. We have searched books on Polynesia for designs in the style of Tahiti, Samoa and Fiji. Flower designs, wave motifs and designs starring the "Tiki", the pagan idols of a by-gone day. Linings and bindings are cut from the pareau cloth which the natives use so much in their dress. A little coin purse to match the big mama purse completes the set. Some have been sent to Australia and the States where they are usable souvenirs of the 

South Seas. The girls add these earnings to their college fund which grows, little by little. 

However, it would take the sale of more purses than they could ever make to put two girls through four years of college. Some day, will

Harry and I wish we had never given up the positions we had in America which were bringing in enough money to buy the advantages we want for our daughters? Did we solve problems by coming to Tahiti and staying here, or did we create more and worse problems for ourselves? Have we been living in a fool's paradise? While we discuss these aspects of life occasionally, we do not make

ourselves unhappy over them. Who knows what windfall may come between now and the day the girls board the boat for California? 9 When a part of this book made its debut in an American newspaper, under the name of "Island Interlude", I received a flood of letters from appreciative readers. Letters asking if I am a real person, if my Tahiti story is true. Letters asking for more. Writers who sympathised with us because we had no hot and cold water, electric refrigeration or a basement bar, and letters that envied my simple way of living. Begging information on rents, plantations, copra making, import duties and romance, inclosing quarters, telephone slugs and street car tickets to cover the cost of replies. Asking if Polly and Peggy were as pretty as their pictures; if I wished to borrow money; if 

Tahiti would be a good place to begin manufacturing ice cream; if the Churches have lightning rods. if we would lend portions of our valley to nature men who want to come and show the natives how to lead a natural life; warnings against a return to the States before 1941 when dire events are forecast; orders for purses; one inquiry for the telephone number of Pupure, the fair one; one letter addressed in beautiful simplicity to "Hester,

Island Interlude, Tahiti" wishing to know if Tane, the native doctor, could cure chillblains; another asking how the score stood between the pigs and children of Teata and Hutia, whether more pigs. had eaten children or more children had eaten pigs up to the present date; letters asking, "Why

are Chinamen?" and "Are papayas feathered or four-legged?" One sailor explained his ship's system of spreading clothes on the deck and scouring them with a scrubbing brush. I tried it and our clothes are no longer tattletale grey. 

Some day I hope to meet these new friends and tell them how much their letters were appreciated.

I call it my office-oh shades of school-teaching days. Harry dubbed it "The Little House," Peggy christened it "Mother's Den," and Polly reduced it to "The Cage," suiting the name to the occupant. 

It appears quite spacious, but really it's about the size of your bathroom. The loveliest part is the roof, the weaving and tying on of the niao. The oddshaped things at the corner of the paper I am copying are small stones to hold the paper down when the trade winds blow. They are also used to scare away insolent roosters who crow and disturb me and the cows who reach over the fence and eat our bean vines.

! 

Author's note to readers: "This is an uninteresting chapter. If the editor doesn't throw it out, and the publisher is foolish enough to print it, then you had better skip it." 

Editor's note to publisher: "By far one of the most exciting chapters in Mrs. Parsons' story. 

This is the Tahiti every one will want to know about. 

Who wants to know that there are 670 square miles in the island and 10,000 people in Papeete? 

Who cares if there are a good many French, more half-castes, a swarm of Chinese and a smattering of Europeans and Americans in Papeete? The population is largely native in the outlying districts, except those two flanking Papeete where the white folks have settled closely, with pretty bungalows, garages, electric lights, plumbing and all the comforts of home.

Who is interested in the fact that small houses may be rented for $5 a month in and around Papeete? Or that palaces may be had for $30 a month? Small houses sell for $500. Large ones, with conveniences, up to $2000. 

There's an ordinance in Papeete that each family must have a toilet. About one third have plumbing, and the rest have "a little house on

the back of the lot" with a crescent or a star in the door. There's electricity. There are about 500 telephones and 600 cars, so you have a mixture of the primitive and the modern. 

About half the people in town cook with charcoal. You make your own stove using a can opener, an empty coal oil tin and a little grate which fits over the open top of the can. Cut a little door in the side which will allow a draft. The ashes drop below. When you move into a house it won't have any stove because the last tenants took it with them, but just buy a grate for 40 cents, order a sack of charcoal from the nearest Chinaman, make your stove in five minutes and start dinner.

A few people try to use oil stoves, but they rust away in record time in the salt air, and leave them without anything to cook on. A few depend on good old-fashioned wood stoves such as our grandmothers used, but wood is expensive in town, and a nuisance for the man of the house to chop if he lives in the country. 

Twenty per cent of the Papeete people eat out. 

The Chinese cafes serve an American meal to fit a farmhand's appetite for 30 cents. When the girls and I go to town, on rare occasions, we get all we can eat at one sitting for 40 cents for the three of us. But a native will eat as much as all of us together so it must seem expensive to him. 

Chinese food is much cheaper. It's a mixture of pork, bean sprouts, red beans, onions, macaroni and chopped vegetables, with a side dish of rice. 

All you can eat for 8 cents -unless you have a

native's appetite. Occasionally a native gets more than he can stow at the moment. He carries the remainder away in a paper bag. Or you may have the Chinese food delivered at your house in town at 8 cents an order.

The Chinese are the businessmen of the island, the "butchers, the bakers and the candle-stick makers." With their cheaper standard of living, they are crowding the French. Four years ago there were four French butchers operating in the city market. Only one is left. The Chinese have taken over the business of the others. 

The Chinese are the bakers in the town and in the outlying districts. With small loaves of bread selling for one and three-quarters cents each, they

I must reduce expenses to a minimum. They build their own ovens of rock and native cement, and line them with bricks. There is only one compartment and one small door to an oven, and fire and bread go in and out the same place by turns. The baker burns coconut husks because they are plentiful and free. The fire is started inside the oven and burns on the bricks for four hours.

Coals and ashes are raked out with a brush made from stiff weeds plucked from the back yard-it's really "brush". A tin of water is thrown in on the hot bricks until the oven is thick with steam, then another brushing removes most of the soot.

The rolls of dough are put in on long paddles, also home made. In about 25 minutes the bread comes out, piping hot and brown. The loaves are brushed to take off clinging cinders.

Before dawn the delivery begins and Ah Woo, Ah Moo or Ah Loo starts out in his little cart exchanging bread for strings of fish, taro or fei if he can't get money. At the end of the month he takes chickens, pigs or copra to settle bills which have run up. Every day we exchange our can of garbage for bread. The garbage is fruit and vegetable peels, and grated coconut which has been "milked"; good pig feed. Where in the States could you give garbage and get a fresh loaf of bread?

It's the Chinese who raise the strong native tobacco, dry it at home and put it into little pink packages. On Moorea where there is swamp land, they raise rice. They are the gardeners supplying Papeete. At 3 A. M. they are on their way to market with fresh vegetables in carts, on bicycles, or in baskets slung on carrying poles. 

They are the dairymen, delivering milk to hotels and homes in beer bottles, with leaves stuck in fór corks. How are the bottles ever cleaned? It's a deep mystery. People have learned to boil the milk.

The man who owns the most cattle on the island is a Chinese. Several half-castes have large herds, but a big per cent of the cattle are raised by twos and threes, here and there, as a side line, or to help keep plantations clear. There are no big open cattle ranches as we know them in the western states. Many die every year from Texas fever carried by ticks. Government officers are trying to teach the natives to eradicate them.

But it's uphill work against carelessness and superstition. Most of our neighbors declare that 

Harry is wasting time and injuring the cows when he cleans the ticks off with disinfectant. 

They believe the ticks are good for the cows. 

Didn't God put them there, same as he put fleas on dogs and lice on chickens?

There are three stores in Papeete run by Europeans and some 300 owned by Chinese. The latter live behind or over their shops. They all speak French, a few know English and many have a smattering of English and other languages. if you go into a store where no one knows English, you may be offered a chair while a child is sent for some neighbor to come and interpret. 

The storekeepers outside of Papeete are all Chinese. Their shops are like the one near us, with a limited display of canned goods from 

France and America, a few bolts of cloth, and a meager assortment of combs, talcum powder, cheap perfume and notions. My white neighbors and I never find what we want here, so we send to our favorite Chinese in town. He is entrusted with such delicate missions as selecting shoes guided by paper foot patterns, matching thread, choosing cloth for pajamas or shorts, registering letters and sending telegrams.

The tailors and dressmakers in Papeete are all Chinese. They will make up any kind of material you order, copy any piece of clothing you show them or tailor natty white ducks for men, to measurement. They put out cheap ready-made

clothes for the poorer trade, but they can't buck the American imported clothing, for American styles appeal to native psychology. Chinese ideas of style and fit are not American. The dressmaker must have a dress to copy or an exact pattern. You will regret it if you rely on his judgment.

The cafes are run by three Europeans versus a hundred Chinese. Hotels are Chinese, Euro pean and one American. The barber shops are run, one French to a score of Chinese. Importers and exporters are two-thirds Chinese, the rest French and foreigners who speak French fluently. 

The only undertaker I know of is a French- Tahitian. But there must be Chinese undertakers who work among their own people. I'm told that embalming is rare here, there's no cremation, and only three funeral carriages. 

Burials are prompt and simple. Plenty of flowers, but mourners, even in town, follow mostly on foot or bicycle. 

Taxi drivers are half or quarter castes, but the bosses are French and American. The bus drivers are half-castes, but most of the busses are owned by Chinese. Mechanics are French, half-caste or Chinese, and the blacksmiths of the horse-shoeing variety are all Chinese . . . so are the harness makers, shoemakers and cabinet makers. These last are responsible for most of the simple furniture of the island, the beds and mattresses, food cupboards, chairs and tables. 

Rich folk send away for furniture, and people

like us cheat the poor Chinese by making our own things of boxes. 

The street peddlers are Chinese. They wheel mushy ice cream about in contraptions which pedal like bicycles, or sell sweets and fruits from pushcarts.

There is one ice plant in town, and cold storage rooms which holds meat over if not sold the first day in market, but there is no refrigeration in the market itself. Very little ice is used here as judged from the American standpoint. One of my neighbors tried to order ice from Papeete for a party. It arrived, after four hours on the roof of the truck, a lump the size of your fist, in a wet sack.

There's a sugar refinery run by Chinese, set down in a big cane field. Their seventy workers are native. They can't make enough sugar to supply the island, and the factory closes three months every year because they haven't enough cane to keep it running. Then the island is flooded with coarse sugar from Java which is just as good as sand to chew on. White sugar comes in from the States all the year for those who prefer it. The local sugar is soft and dark. We like it far better than white sugar, now, in spite of the story which is going around about a worker who fell into the sugar vat, making the output a little darker than usual. 

One aspiring American has opened a pineapple cannery recently, but it operates only eight days a month because of lack of fruit. The American

has a big pine plantation, and natives are also planting here and there to feed the cannery. 

By this time you're wondering what is left for the French-government and the professions. The governor comes direct from France. The officials under him are French or French with a little native color somewhere in the family. Lawyers, teachers, doctors, nurses, druggists and dentists are French or near-French. The hospital and free dispensary give excellent service with beautiful French politeness.

Teachers are local or graduates of schools in 

France. No Americans need apply. There are three public schools in Papeete and two religious schools, one Catholic and one Protestant. All are conducted in the French language and along French lines, stressing mathematics, French history and penmanship with flourishes. They reach American high school level. There are

Chinese schools for the little sons and daughters of the dragon. Many a slant-eyed boy and girl graduate from both French and Chinese schools. 

The chief of a district cannot be elected to office unless he is part Tahitian, but he's not capable of filling the position, usually, unless he has a strain of white, so the chiefs are of mixed blood, island born. The policemen of Papeete and the districts are half and quarter-casts. 

Now, what's left for the full-blooded Tahitians to do? Not much, and they're glad of it. They live and love and dance. They're not a bit downhearted because the Chinese do the work. They

make copra and charcoal when they feel like it; sell fish, fei and firewood; raise a few pigs and occasionally keep a cow. Some tend bees in old boxes and sell a little honey, but most of it they use for beer. They work by the day for white people when the spirit moves them, but they drop work at any moment to go fishing or to gather fei in the mountains. When a native needs a new house, his wife and children weave the 'niao' and he makes the house himself very neatly so long as he uses native materials. As soon as he tries to use lumber he is more awkward than the traditional woman about driving nails. 

Native women patiently collect tiny snails, take out the bodies, bore holes in the shells and make strings of beads and hat bands. They make bracelets and necklaces of dried seeds to sell to tourists. A few make grass dancing skirts. Some are expert hat weavers, making 'panamas' from the fiber of pandanus and bamboo. There is no native cloth industry in these days. Tapa pounding is a lost art on most of the islands for the government found it necessary to prohibit the sacrifice of the breadfruit trees. They were being cut down faster than they could grow for the inner bark was popular for tapa making. The paper mulberry used to be raised for making tapa but that is given up now since the white man's cloth is cheap and easy to get. I have seen a few pieces of beautiful tapa, three centuries old and still in good condition, but they are in curio stores or the homes of the rich.

Since we came, land buying has been closed to foreigners. Young men who hope to come here, marry the village belle and live on her land will be disappointed for, I'm told, the government wisely denies a license unless the would-be husband can show that he will be able to support a family. If there's "no money, no job and no land" then it's "no girl", too. Make your money in America and come here when you have a steady income and can retire on it, or come any time as a tourist.

It all sums up to this: government and the professions are in the capable hands of the French and 'almost-French'; the Chinese dominate business and trade and can live so cheaply that white men cannot compete with them; natives do nothing quite happily. There are no dazzling prospects for jobs anywhere for Americans.

You can buy anything in Papeete: cars, motor boats, motorcycles, bath tubs, electric ice boxes, radios, electric irons and up-to-date gadgets. 

Most of them are useless in the uncivilized parts of the island, because there is no electricity, piped water or gas stations except in limited areas, we are forty miles from electricity and eight miles from the nearest car owner.

However there are plenty of other things that are all to the good in the back districts: oil burning ice boxes, wire screening, bicycles, Delco systems, carved chests, cut glass, fine coffins, bar fixtures, charcoal irons, Chinese silks, flashlights, thumb tacks and your favorite toothpaste.

The first year down here, we thought there were many things we couldn't buy, but that was be- Icause we didn't know where to find them. The 

Chinese stores are a jumble until you get used to them. But to date the only things we haven't been able to locate are rubber-set hairbrushes and iodized salt. We find that life can be lived without those things.

People taking up residence here are charged import duty on their cars by the pound, so your old-fashioned, big car will be taxed higher than a new, small car. Used household goods up to the value of $500 may be brought in duty-free. 

This does not include musical instruments, firearms, typewriters or bicycles.

Imported articles have freight and duty added to the original prices in the States, so of course they all come higher here. A kodak film which costs 30 cents there, costs 56 cents here. A 15 cent dishpan costs 50 cents here. Medicines are high, but there are many native cures you may try if you wish: pini leaves for sores and cuts, herbs and roots cooked for stomach troubles, coconut oil and other ingredients for sore throats and colds.

Dentistry seems exhorbitant to the natives, but to one accustomed to American price scales, it is not: a dollar and a half for a filling and sixty cents for an extraction. Complete hospital service in Papeete, room, board, nurse and doctor costs two dollars a day.

With our modest income from our cows, tarua

and copra we are self-supporting. We do not earn much, but we don't have to, for the scale of living is much lower here. There is no struggle to outdo your neighbor in clothes, cars or radios.

We wanted a rest from the mad scramble of town life and relief from noisy traffic. The two daily busses are usually the only vehicles that smell of gas. They pass at 2 A. M., Papeete bound, and return about 6 P. M.We are not awake to hear them in the morning, and we find we can put up with their tooting in the evening without too much nerve strain. On Fridays traffic is heavier, for the French government doctor passes on his weekly rounds, whizzing by in a sport model, scattering dogs, pigs and chickens, and leaving an admiring trail of natives staring. Sundays and holidays we have a few flower-decked cars, filled with singing boys and girls. So we've missed some of the worst features of traffic. 

We had hoped to enjoy the simple life in a kindly climate where winter clothes and furnace fires are forgotten. You may believe most of what you read in the romantic novels about the golden weather here. With the vast Pacific keeping a uniform temperature of 85 degrees, day and night, summer and winter, the little specks of islands take nearly the same temperature. It's cooler during rain storms and in the evenings when a light sweater feels good. We sleep under light blankets. Cool enough for perfect rest, never hot like summer nights back home.

Most of the weather in the dry season is like

the finest day you can remember in southern 

California, or like those days you reveled in on the 

Florida beaches. Air soft, sunshine bright, breeze cool. There's no need to perspire on the rainy parts of Tahiti unless you engage in hard labor or walk or cycle on the roads at noon. 

Rain, more or less, all the year; regular rainy season from December to March. Often the rain comes in passing showers while the sun shines on, not a bit discouraged. Then they call it "liquid sunshine". No one bothers to take shelter from these rains; clothes will dry again in ten minutes. If I went on for two pages, I couldn't say it better than Peggy did: "Not hot and cold, just warm and cool."

When you visit Oceania take an inter-island schooner and cruise about a bit. Moorea is ten miles away. Makatea a night's sail, and Bora-Bora a day beyond that. The Paumotus and the Marquesas lie farther from the tourist trails where schooners call once in two months only, so the natives don't have quite the same coating of white civilization that the Tahitians do.

Tiny, out-of-the-way islands like Rurutu and Maka still have something of the old time Polynesian civilization. The traders are there, too, but they haven't ruined everything yet. And they'll never be able to ruin the gracious climate. or the incredible beauty of the islands. 

THE END
